## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
1. Seed Biology
## 1. Introduction
Seed biology is the study of the structure, development, physiology, genetics, viability, dormancy,
germination, vigor, storage behavior and biological functions of seeds.
A seed is much more than something that is simply planted in soil. It is a living biological system
containing a developing or mature plant, stored reserves and protective structures that allow the plant to
survive between generations and initiate a new crop.
The overall concept is:
Flower→Pollination→Fertilization→Seed Development→Seed Maturation→Dormancy/
Storage→Germination→Seedling          
For agriculture, seed biology is fundamental because successful crop establishment begins with a
biologically viable and physiologically strong seed.

2. Definition of a Seed
A seed is a mature ovule of a seed-producing plant that generally contains an embryo, stored food or
storage tissue, and protective coverings.
In angiosperms:
Ovule→Seed   
and:
Ovary→Fruit   
A mature seed generally consists of:
Seed=Embryo+Storage Reserves+Protective Covering  =   +
  +    
The exact structure differs among crop species.

3. Biological Importance of Seeds
Seeds perform several important biological functions:
- reproduction,
- survival between generations,
- dispersal,
- protection of the embryo,
- storage of nutrients,
- adaptation to unfavorable conditions,
- establishment of new plants,
- maintenance of genetic continuity.
Agriculturally:
Seed Quality→Crop Establishment→Plant Population→Yield Potential 
    

4. Seed as a Living Organism
A dry seed may appear inactive, but a viable seed is still biologically alive.
It contains:
- living cells,
- DNA,
- enzymes,
- membranes,
- mitochondria,
- stored food,
- metabolic machinery.
Its metabolism is usually very low during dry storage, but it can rapidly increase after water uptake.
Dry Seed→Imbibition→Metabolic Reactivation     

5. Seed Structure
A generalized seed contains:

1. Seed coat
Protective outer covering.

## 2. Embryo
Young plant contained within the seed.

3. Storage tissue
Provides nutrients during germination and early seedling development.
Depending on the crop, storage reserves may occur mainly in:
- endosperm,
- cotyledons,
- other storage tissues.

6. Seed Coat
The seed coat protects the seed from:
- mechanical damage,
- pathogens,
- excessive water loss,
- environmental stress,
- premature germination.
It may also regulate:
- water entry,
- oxygen entry,
- dormancy,
- gas exchange.
The seed coat develops primarily from the integuments of the ovule.
Integuments→Seed Coat   

## 7. Embryo
The embryo is the young plant contained inside the seed.
Major embryonic structures include:
- radicle,
- plumule,
- hypocotyl,
- epicotyl,
- cotyledon(s).
The embryo eventually develops into:
Embryo→Root System+Shoot System   +
   

## 8. Radicle
The radicle is the embryonic root.
During germination, it is commonly the first major embryonic structure to emerge.
Radicle→Primary Root    
Its functions include:
- anchorage,
- water absorption,
- nutrient absorption,
- establishment of the root system.

## 9. Plumule
The plumule is the embryonic shoot.
It develops into:
- stem,
- leaves,
- branches,

- eventually reproductive structures.
Plumule→Shoot System    

## 10. Cotyledons
Cotyledons are embryonic leaves.

Eudicots
Usually have:
2 cotyledons  
Examples:
- soybean,
- chickpea,
- groundnut,
- pea,
- bean.

Monocots
Have:
1 cotyledon  
In cereals such as wheat and maize, the cotyledon is called the scutellum.

11. Cereal Seed Biology
Cereal grains have specialized structures.
Examples:
- wheat,
- rice,
- maize,
- barley,
- sorghum.
A cereal grain generally contains:

- embryo,
- endosperm,
- aleurone layer,
- seed coat/pericarp-associated coverings.
The embryo contains structures such as:
- scutellum,
- plumule,
- radicle,
- coleoptile,
- coleorhiza.

## 12. Scutellum
The scutellum is the single cotyledon of grass embryos.
It functions primarily in transferring nutrients from the endosperm to the developing embryo.
Conceptually:
Endosperm→Scutellum→Embryo     

## 13. Coleoptile
The coleoptile is a protective sheath surrounding the emerging shoot of grasses.
It helps protect the plumule as it grows through the soil.
Examples:
- rice,
- wheat,
- maize,
- barley.

## 14. Coleorhiza
The coleorhiza is a protective sheath associated with the embryonic root of grasses.
It protects the radicle during emergence.

## 15. Endosperm
The endosperm is a nutritive tissue that develops following double fertilization in angiosperms.
Typically:
2 polar nuclei+sperm→Primary endosperm nucleus  +  
  
Often the endosperm is triploid:
3n 
because two maternal polar nuclei combine with one paternal sperm nucleus.
Endosperm can store:
- starch,
- proteins,
- oils.

16. Seed Storage Reserves
Seeds commonly store three major classes of food:

Carbohydrates
Mainly:
- starch,
- soluble sugars.
Common in many cereal grains.

Proteins
Important in:
- pulses,

- legumes,
- oilseeds.

Lipids
Important in:
- soybean,
- groundnut,
- sunflower,
- sesame,
- mustard and other oilseeds.
Thus:
Seed Reserve=Carbohydrate+Protein+Lipid  =   +
  +    
with proportions varying by crop.

17. Seed Reserve Mobilization
During germination, stored reserves are converted into forms that growing cells can use.
For example:
Starch→amylasesSugars      
Proteins→proteasesAmino acids      
Lipids→lipasesFatty acids + glycerol      
These products provide:
- energy,
- carbon skeletons,
- building materials.

18. Seed Development
Seed development begins after successful fertilization.

Fertilization→Embryogenesis+Endosperm Development→Seed Filling→Maturation   +      
The embryo develops into the future plant.
The endosperm or other storage tissue accumulates reserves.
The seed coat develops around the developing seed.

## 19. Embryogenesis
Embryogenesis is the process by which the zygote develops into an embryo.
In many eudicots, developmental stages include:
Zygote→Proembryo→Globular→Heart→Torpedo→Mature Embryo        
The exact developmental pattern varies among species.

20. Seed Filling
During seed filling, storage reserves accumulate.
Source→Phloem→Developing Seed     
Sources may include:
- mature leaves,
- stems,
- pods,
- other photosynthetic organs.
Stored materials include:
- starch,
- proteins,
- oils.
Seed filling strongly influences final seed weight.

Seed Yield≈Number of Seeds×Average Seed Weight     

21. Seed Maturation
Seed maturation involves:
- embryo development,
- reserve accumulation,
- increasing dry matter,
- acquisition of desiccation tolerance in many species,
- changes in metabolic activity,
- development of dormancy in some species.
A generalized sequence is:
Development→Reserve Accumulation→Dry Matter Increase→Physiological Maturity      

22. Physiological Maturity
Physiological maturity generally refers to the stage when a seed has completed its major
developmental accumulation and reached approximately maximum dry matter, although the exact
definition and indicators vary among crops.
After physiological maturity, the seed may continue to lose water while undergoing further drying.
Therefore:
Physiological Maturity≠Safe Storage Moisture    

23. Seed Moisture
Water content is extremely important in seed biology.
Seed moisture affects:
- respiration,
- enzyme activity,

- microbial growth,
- insect activity,
- deterioration,
- storage life.
Generally:
High Moisture→Metabolic Activity↑→Deterioration Risk↑       
The appropriate moisture level for storage is crop- and storage-system-specific.

## 24. Imbibition
Imbibition is the uptake of water by dry biological materials due to their affinity for water.
When a dry seed encounters water:
Dry Seed→Water Uptake→Swelling     
Imbibition is the first major physiological event in germination.

25. What Happens After Imbibition?
The sequence is approximately:
Water Uptake→Membrane Reorganization→Respiration↑→Enzyme Activation→Reserve Mobilizatio
n→Cell Division/Expansion→Radicle Emergence          

26. Seed Respiration
Once water enters the seed, metabolic activity increases.
Aerobic respiration can be represented as:
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+Energy O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
   

The ATP generated supports:
- cell division,
- cell expansion,
- transport,
- enzyme activity,
- synthesis of new molecules.

27. Seed Germination
Germination is the process by which a viable seed resumes active growth and develops into a seedling.
A simplified sequence:
Viable Seed→Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Seedli
ng        

28. Germination Requirements
Important requirements include:

Water
Required for:
- imbibition,
- enzyme activation,
- metabolism,
- cell expansion.

Oxygen
Required for aerobic respiration in most seeds.

Suitable temperature
Each crop has its own temperature range.
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      

Viability
The embryo must be alive and physiologically capable of germination.

Light
Some species have light-sensitive germination, while many agricultural seeds can germinate in
darkness if other conditions are suitable.

29. Seed Dormancy
Seed dormancy is a condition in which a viable seed does not germinate even when apparently
favorable environmental conditions are present, because internal or external mechanisms prevent
immediate germination.
Viable Seed+Apparently Favorable Conditions+No Germination→Possible Dormancy  +   +     
Dormancy is different from a dead seed.

30. Dormancy vs Quiescence
Dormancy
The seed does not germinate because of internal physiological or structural constraints.

Quiescence
The seed is capable of germination but does not because environmental requirements are absent.
For example:
No water→Quiescence  while:
Suitable conditions + internal block→Dormancy 
 31. Types of Seed Dormancy
Major categories include:

- physical dormancy,
- physiological dormancy,
- morphological dormancy,
- morphophysiological dormancy,
- combinational dormancy.

Physical dormancy
Seed coat restricts water or gas movement.

Physiological dormancy
Internal physiological mechanisms prevent germination.

Morphological dormancy
Embryo is not fully developed when the seed is dispersed.

Combinational dormancy
More than one dormancy mechanism occurs together.

32. Dormancy-Breaking Mechanisms
Depending on species, dormancy may be reduced through:
- scarification,
- stratification,
- temperature treatments,
- light treatment,
- after-ripening,
- chemical treatments,
- alternating temperatures.
The correct treatment is species-specific.

33. Seed Hormones
Important hormones/regulators include:

Abscisic acid — ABA
Generally associated with:
- seed maturation,
- dormancy,
- stress responses.

Gibberellins — GA
Important in:
- germination,
- reserve mobilization,
- seedling growth.
A simplified relationship is:
ABA→Dormancy   
GA→Germination/Growth Promotion   
This is an oversimplification of a complex regulatory network, but useful for understanding the basic
concept.

34. Cereal Germination and Gibberellin
In many cereal seeds:
Embryo→GAAleurone Activation→Hydrolytic Enzymes→Endosperm Reserve Mobilization         
One important enzyme is α-amylase, which helps break down starch into smaller carbohydrates.

35. Seed Viability
Seed viability is the ability of a seed to remain alive and capable of producing a normal plant under
appropriate conditions.
A viable seed:
Alive+Physiologically Functional  +    
A non-viable seed cannot produce a normal plant even when conditions are favorable.

36. Viability vs Germination
These are related but not identical.

Viability
Can the seed remain alive and potentially germinate?

Germination
Does the seed actually initiate the germination process under specified test conditions?
A seed may be viable but fail to germinate immediately because of dormancy.
Therefore:
Viability≠Germination   

37. Seed Vigor
Seed vigor refers to the sum of properties that determine the potential for rapid, uniform emergence
and development of normal seedlings under a wide range of environmental conditions.
Thus:
Vigor→Speed+Uniformity+Stress Tolerance+Establishment Ability   +   +   +    
Two seed lots can have similar germination percentages but different vigor.

38. Germination Percentage
A common calculation is:
Germination %=Number of normal seedlingsNumber of seeds tested×100  =         100  
For example, if 92 normal seedlings emerge from 100 tested seeds:
Germination=92% =92\%

39. Germination Rate
Germination percentage measures how many seeds germinated.
Germination rate/speed considers how quickly they germinated.
Therefore:
Germination Percentage=Quantity  =    
Germination Rate=Speed  =    
Both can be important for crop establishment.

40. Seedling Emergence
Germination occurs at the seed level.
Emergence occurs when the developing seedling becomes visible above the soil surface or reaches the
defined emergence criterion.
Therefore:
Germination→Emergence   
but:
Germination≠Emergence   
A seed can germinate underground but fail to emerge because of:
- crusting,
- deep sowing,
- compaction,
- drought,
- disease,
- mechanical resistance.

41. Seed Quality
Seed quality has several dimensions.
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Seed Health  =
  +   +   +    

Genetic quality
Correct variety/cultivar/hybrid identity.

Physical quality
- purity,
- uniformity,
- absence of inert material,
- absence of damaged seeds.

Physiological quality
- viability,
- germination,
- vigor,
- moisture.

Seed health
Freedom from important seed-borne pathogens and pests within applicable standards.

42. Genetic Quality
Genetic quality determines whether the seed represents the intended genetic material.
It affects:
- maturity,
- yield potential,
- quality,
- disease resistance,
- stress tolerance,
- plant architecture.
Therefore:
Correct Genetics→Correct Crop Type    

43. Physical Purity
A seed lot may contain:
- pure seed,
- other crop seeds,
- weed seeds,
- inert matter.
Physical purity is therefore an important quality parameter.

44. Seed Health
Seed can carry:
- fungi,
- bacteria,
- viruses or virus-associated agents,
- nematodes,
- insects in some circumstances.
Seed-borne organisms can cause problems after planting.
Thus:
Healthy Seed→Lower Seed-Borne Disease Risk    

45. Seed Testing
Important seed tests include:

Germination test
Measures production of normal seedlings under defined conditions.

Viability tests
Examples include:
- tetrazolium test.

Vigor tests
Various tests estimate seed performance under stress or rapid-establishment conditions.

Moisture test
Measures seed moisture content.

Purity test
Determines physical composition.

Seed health test
Detects important pathogens or pests.

46. Tetrazolium Test
The tetrazolium test is a biochemical viability test.
Living tissues with active respiration can reduce tetrazolium compounds and develop characteristic
staining.
It can provide a relatively rapid indication of seed viability.
Important:
Tetrazolium Result≠Direct Germination Percentage    
It assesses viability through a biochemical response rather than waiting for seedling development.

47. Seed Storage Biology
Seed storage aims to slow deterioration.
The major environmental factors are:
- moisture,
- temperature,
- relative humidity,
- oxygen,
- insects,
- microorganisms.

A simplified relationship:
High Moisture+High Temperature→Rapid Deterioration  +     

48. Seed Longevity
Seed longevity is the length of time a seed lot remains sufficiently viable and vigorous under specified
storage conditions.
It depends on:
- species,
- genotype,
- initial seed quality,
- moisture,
- temperature,
- oxygen,
- storage environment,
- packaging.
Therefore:
Seed Longevity=f(Genetics+Initial Quality+Storage Environment)  =
f( + + )  

49. Orthodox Seeds
Orthodox seeds generally tolerate drying to relatively low moisture and can be stored for extended
periods under suitable cool, dry conditions.
Many agricultural crops produce orthodox seeds.
Examples include many:
- cereals,
- pulses,
- oilseeds.

50. Recalcitrant Seeds
Recalcitrant seeds are sensitive to drying and generally cannot be stored using the same conventional
low-moisture approach used for orthodox seeds.
They often occur in some:
- tropical trees,
- perennial species.
Therefore:
Storage Biology=f(Seed Type)  = f( )  

51. Seed Aging
Seed deterioration can involve:
- membrane damage,
- oxidative stress,
- enzyme dysfunction,
- DNA damage,
- protein damage,
- mitochondrial deterioration,
- reduced repair capacity.
Conceptually:
Aging→Cellular Damage↑→Vigor↓→Germination↓         

52. Seed Moisture and Respiration
As seed moisture increases, metabolic activity generally increases.
Moisture↑→Respiration↑→Heat Production↑       
This can create a deterioration feedback loop:
Moisture↑→Respiration↑→Heat↑→Deterioration↑        53. Seed Priming
Seed priming is a controlled hydration treatment in which seeds are partially hydrated to initiate
physiological processes without allowing radicle emergence, followed by drying or sowing depending
- n the technique.
Potential benefits include:
- faster germination,
- more uniform emergence,
- improved establishment,
- improved performance under some stresses.
Types include:
- hydropriming,
- osmopriming,
- halopriming,
- biopriming,
- nutrient priming.
Effects are crop-, variety- and protocol-dependent.

54. Seed Treatment
Seed treatment can be used to protect seeds or improve establishment.
It may involve:
- fungicidal treatment,
- insecticidal treatment where appropriate,
- biological treatments,
- micronutrient treatments,
- inoculation.
Treatment should be crop-, product- and regulation-specific.

55. Biological Seed Treatment
Biological treatments may use beneficial microorganisms.
Examples include:
- rhizobial inoculation in compatible legumes,
- selected beneficial fungi,
- selected plant-growth-promoting microorganisms.
The purpose can include:
- biological nitrogen fixation,
- nutrient mobilization,
- improved establishment,
- disease suppression.
Performance is context-dependent.

56. Rhizobium and Legume Seeds
Legume crops can form symbiotic relationships with compatible rhizobia.
The relationship is:
Legume Root+Compatible Rhizobium→Root Nodules  +
    
Nitrogen fixation converts atmospheric nitrogen into biologically usable nitrogen forms.
N2→Reduced Nitrogen Compounds   
This is particularly important for:
- soybean,
- chickpea,
- pea,
- lentil,
- pigeon pea,
- many other legumes.

57. Seed and Environmental Stress
Seed performance is influenced by:
- drought,
- salinity,
- waterlogging,
- heat,
- cold,
- soil crusting,
- compaction.
For example:
Drought→Insufficient Imbibition→Poor Germination    while:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Respiration Stress→Poor Establishment   O 2   58. Temperature and Seed Biology
Temperature affects:
- germination rate,
- enzyme activity,
- respiration,
- membrane function,
- seedling growth.
The general response follows:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      
Below the optimum:
Temperature↓→Metabolism↓→Germination Speed↓      Above the optimum:

Temperature↑→Stress↑→Germination/Seedling Performance↓      59. Oxygen and Seed Biology
Most germinating seeds require oxygen for efficient aerobic respiration.
Poor soil aeration can therefore reduce germination and establishment.
Waterlogging→Air-Filled Pores↓→O2↓→Respiration Stress     O 2   

60. Seed and Light
Light sensitivity differs among species.
Some seeds germinate better:
- in light,
- in darkness,
- or across a broad range of conditions.
Light can also affect seed dormancy through photoreceptors such as phytochrome.
Therefore:
Light Requirement=Species/Genotype Specific  =
   

61. Seed Biology and Photosynthesis
A newly germinated seedling initially depends heavily on stored reserves.
Later:
Stored Reserves→Seedling Development    
and eventually:
Photosynthesis→Autotrophic Growth    
The transition from reserve dependence to photosynthetic independence is a major developmental
change.

62. Seed Biology and Source–Sink Relationships
During seed filling:
Source→Phloem→Seed Sink     
During germination:
Seed Reserves→Embryo/Seedling Growth    
Thus the direction and nature of carbon flow change during the crop life cycle.

63. Seed Biology and Crop Establishment
A strong seed lot contributes to:
- rapid germination,
- uniform emergence,
- good plant population,
- synchronized crop development.
Therefore:
High-Quality Seed→Uniform Establishment→Better Crop Stand 
   
However, seed quality alone cannot overcome severe problems such as unsuitable soil moisture,
temperature, salinity or compaction.

64. Seed Rate
Seed biology is directly connected to seed rate.
A simplified relationship is:
Required Seeds≈Desired Established PlantsExpected Establishment Fraction          
For example, if the target is 100,000 established plants and expected establishment is 80%:

Required seeds=100,0000.80=125,000  =   0.80  = 125,000
Actual agronomic seed rates depend on crop, cultivar, seed size, germination, vigor, field conditions
and recommended population.

65. Seed Size
Seed size can influence:
- stored reserves,
- early seedling growth,
- emergence under some conditions.
Larger seeds often contain more reserves, but:
Larger Seed≠Automatically Better Seed   
Genetic quality, viability, vigor, health and environmental conditions also matter.

66. Seed Color, Shape and Appearance
Physical appearance can provide clues about:
- maturity,
- damage,
- disease,
- purity,
- mechanical injury.
But appearance alone cannot reliably determine:
- viability,
- genetic purity,
- vigor,
- internal damage.
Laboratory testing may be required.

67. Mechanical Damage
Seed can be damaged during:
- harvesting,
- threshing,
- shelling,
- cleaning,
- grading,
- transportation.
Damage may affect:
- seed coat,
- embryo,
- internal tissues.
Consequences can include:
Mechanical Damage→Reduced Vigor→Poor Germination/Storage 
   

68. Seed-Borne Disease
A seed may carry pathogens:
- externally,
- internally,
- on associated plant debris.
Seed-borne disease can result in:
- poor germination,
- seed rot,
- seedling disease,
- disease introduction into a field.
Therefore seed health is a major component of seed quality.

69. Seed Biology and Genetics
Seeds carry the genetic information required to produce the next generation.
DNA determines or contributes to characteristics such as:
- plant architecture,
- maturity,
- yield potential,
- quality,
- disease resistance,
- stress responses.
Environmental conditions modify expression.
Phenotype=Genotype+Environment+G×E  =   +
  + G E  

70. Seed Biology and Genetic Purity
For a variety or hybrid, genetic purity matters because contamination can produce plants with
undesirable characteristics.
This is especially important in:
- certified seed,
- hybrid seed,
- breeder seed,
- foundation seed,
- commercial seed production.

71. Hybrid Seed Biology
A hybrid can be produced by crossing genetically distinct parents:
P1×P2→F1 
The F₁ seed may express heterosis or hybrid vigor.

Hybrid seed should not automatically be treated as equivalent to a stable true-breeding variety for seed
saving because the next generation can segregate.

72. Seed Dormancy and Agriculture
Dormancy can be beneficial because it prevents premature germination before suitable conditions
- ccur.
For example:
Dormancy→Protection from Premature Germination    
But excessive dormancy can create problems in:
- field emergence,
- seed testing,
- uniform crop establishment.

73. Seed Biology and Germination
Synchronization
Uniform seed lots generally support synchronized crop development.
This is useful because plants may then experience:
- flowering,
- irrigation,
- fertilizer application,
- pest management,
- harvesting
at more predictable times.

74. Seed Biology and Crop Phenology
Seed quality can affect the starting point of crop development.

Seed Quality→Emergence→Vegetative Growth→Flowering→Maturity 
     
Poor establishment can create uneven crop development throughout the season.

75. Seed Biology and Storage Environment
The ideal storage environment depends on the biological behavior of the seed.
For many orthodox seeds:
Cool+Dry+Clean+Pest-Protected→Better Longevity  +   +   +
    
But exact conditions must be determined for the specific crop and storage system.

76. Seed Bank and Germplasm Conservation
Seeds can be stored as genetic resources in seed banks/gene banks.
Purpose:
- conserve genetic diversity,
- preserve landraces,
- maintain breeding resources,
- conserve rare genetic material,
- support future crop improvement.
Conceptually:
Genetic Diversity→Conservation→Future Breeding     

77. Seed Biology and Biodiversity
Genetic diversity within crops provides potential variation for:
- drought tolerance,
- heat tolerance,

- disease resistance,
- pest resistance,
- salinity tolerance,
- quality traits.
Therefore:
Seed Diversity→Breeding Options→Future Crop Resilience     

78. Seed Biology and Climate Change
Climate variability can affect:
- seed production,
- seed maturation,
- seed drying,
- storage conditions,
- germination,
- seedling establishment.
Extreme:
- heat,
- drought,
- flooding,
- humidity
can reduce seed quality.
Therefore climate-resilient seed systems are important.

79. Seed Biology — Complete Process
The entire biological cycle can be summarized as:
Flowering↓Pollination↓Fertilization↓Embryo + Endosperm Development↓Seed Filling↓Seed Maturatio
n↓Drying/Dormancy/Storage↓Imbibition↓Metabolic Activation↓Reserve Mobilization↓Radicle Emerge
nce↓Seedling↓Vegetative Plant↓Reproductive Plant  & \\

& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ &     
The cycle then repeats.

80. Important Seed Biology Relationships
Seed formation
Ovule→Seed   

Fruit formation
Ovary→Fruit   

Germination
Water+Oxygen+Suitable Temperature+Viability→Germination  +  
+   +     

Seedling establishment
Germination→Root+Shoot→Seedling   +
    

Seed storage
Appropriate Moisture+Appropriate Temperature+Pest Protection→Better Seed Longevity  +   +     

81. Critical Distinctions
Concept
Meaning
Seed
Mature reproductive structure containing embryo and reserves
Seed biology
Study of seed structure, development and functioning
Seed formation Development of seed from fertilized ovule
Seed maturation Development toward physiological maturity
Seed filling
Accumulation of reserves

Concept
Viability
Germination
Vigor
Dormancy
Quiescence
Emergence
Seed quality
Seed storage

Meaning
Ability to remain alive and potentially germinate
Resumption of active growth under suitable conditions
Ability to germinate rapidly, uniformly and perform well
Internal mechanism preventing immediate germination
Failure to germinate because environmental conditions are unsuitable
Seedling becomes established/visible according to the defined criterion
Genetic + physical + physiological + health attributes
Preservation of seed quality over time

82. Seed Biology vs Seed Technology
Seed biology
Studies:
- structure,
- physiology,
- development,
- dormancy,
- germination,
- aging,
- genetics.

Seed technology
Applies this knowledge to:
- production,
- processing,
- testing,
- treatment,
- certification,
- packaging,
- storage,
- distribution.
Therefore:

Seed Biology→Scientific Foundation→Seed Technology     

83. Agricultural Importance of Seed Biology
Understanding seed biology helps in:
- selecting high-quality seed,
- determining planting time,
- determining sowing depth,
- managing seed moisture,
- choosing storage conditions,
- understanding dormancy,
- improving germination,
- improving crop establishment,
- calculating seed rate,
- diagnosing poor emergence,
- developing seed treatments,
- conserving genetic resources,
- producing quality seed.

84. Diagnostic Framework for Poor
Germination
When germination is poor, investigate:
Seed Quality+Moisture+Temperature+Oxygen+Dormancy+Disease+Soil Conditions  +   +   +   +   +
  +    
For example:

Low germination in laboratory
Possible causes:

- low viability,
- dormancy,
- aging,
- seed damage,
- poor storage.

Good laboratory germination but poor field emergence
Possible causes:
- drought,
- crusting,
- compaction,
- waterlogging,
- incorrect sowing depth,
- pests,
- soil-borne disease,
- unsuitable temperature.
This distinction is extremely useful:
Good Lab Germination⇏Guaranteed Field Emergence 
   

85. Master Seed Biology Model
The entire topic can be reduced to:
Genetics+Structure+Reserves+Water+Temperature+Oxygen+Hormones+Environment→Seed Perform
ance  +   +   +   +  
+   +   +     
And:
Seed Performance→Germination→Emergence→Plant Population→Crop Establishment→Yield Potent
ial        

86. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed biology is the study of the structure, development, physiology, genetics, viability,
dormancy, germination, vigor, storage behavior and biological functions of seeds. A
seed is a mature ovule that generally contains an embryo, stored food or storage tissue
and protective coverings. Seed biology explains how seeds develop after fertilization,
accumulate reserves, mature, undergo dormancy or storage, absorb water, reactivate
metabolism, mobilize reserves and germinate to produce seedlings. Important
concepts include seed structure, embryo, cotyledons, endosperm, seed reserves,
viability, germination, vigor, dormancy, seed moisture, seed aging, seed storage, seed
quality and environmental requirements for establishment. Understanding seed
biology is essential for producing, testing, storing and planting high-quality seed and
for achieving rapid, uniform crop establishment.

87. Final Memory Map
Seed structure
Seed=Embryo+Storage Tissue+Seed Coat  =   +   +    

Embryo
Embryo=Radicle+Plumule+Cotyledon(s)+Embryonic Axis  =   +
  +   +    

Development
Fertilization→Embryogenesis→Seed Filling→Maturation      

Germination
Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Seedling       

Seed quality
Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  +   +
  +    

Seed performance
Viability+Vigor+Suitable Environment→Successful Establishment  +
  +     

Most important distinction
Viability≠Germination≠Vigor≠Emergence     
One-line memory: Seed biology explains how a seed is formed, how it stays alive, how
it stores and uses food, how it responds to environmental conditions, how it remains
viable during storage, and how it transforms into a new plant through germination
and seedling establishment.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
2. Seed Quality
## 1. Introduction
Seed quality refers to the overall characteristics of a seed lot that determine its identity, purity,
viability, germination, vigor, health, storability and ability to produce a healthy, uniform and
genetically appropriate crop.
A simple model is:
Seed Quality=Genetic Quality+Physical Quality+Physiological Quality+Seed Health  =   +   +   +
   
High-quality seed is the foundation of successful crop establishment.
High-Quality Seed→Good Germination→Uniform Emergence→Good Plant Stand→Yield Potential xed         
However, good seed cannot completely compensate for unsuitable soil, moisture, temperature, sowing
depth, salinity, waterlogging or poor management.

## 2. Definition
Seed quality is the combination of genetic, physical, physiological and health
characteristics of a seed lot that determine its suitability for planting and its ability to
produce normal, vigorous and genetically true plants under suitable conditions.

3. What Is a Seed Lot?
A seed lot is a defined quantity of seed that is sufficiently uniform in identity and quality and is
handled as a unit for production, processing, testing, storage or sale.
Seed quality is normally evaluated at the lot level, not merely by looking at one individual seed.

4. Four Major Components of Seed Quality
Component
Main question
Genetic quality
Is it the correct variety/hybrid/genetic type?
Physical quality
Is the seed physically pure, clean and undamaged?
Physiological quality Is it viable, germinable and vigorous?
Is it free from important seed-borne pests/pathogens within applicable
Seed health
standards?
Together:
SQ=f(G,P,Ph,H) 
where:
- GG = genetic quality,
- PP = physical quality,
- PhPh = physiological quality,
- HH = health.

5. Genetic Quality
Genetic quality refers to the genetic identity and purity of the seed.
It determines whether the seed will produce plants with the intended characteristics.
Important traits include:

- variety/cultivar identity,
- hybrid identity,
- maturity,
- plant height,
- yield potential,
- disease resistance,
- pest resistance,
- stress tolerance,
- grain/fruit/fiber quality.

6. Genetic Purity
Genetic purity describes how closely the seed lot conforms to the intended genetic identity.
A genetically pure seed lot should produce plants that are consistent with the intended variety or hybrid
within the applicable standards.
Genetic contamination can occur through:
- unintended cross-pollination,
- varietal mixtures,
- mechanical mixing,
- volunteer plants,
- incorrect labeling,
- improper seed production practices.

7. Genetic Purity vs Physical Purity
These are different.

Genetic purity
Asks:
Is this the correct genetic type?

Physical purity
Asks:
Is this seed lot physically clean and free from unwanted materials?
A seed lot can be physically clean but genetically contaminated.
Likewise, a genetically correct seed lot can contain physical impurities.

8. Physical Quality
Physical quality concerns the physical composition and condition of the seed lot.
It includes:
- pure seed,
- other crop seeds,
- weed seeds,
- inert matter,
- damaged seeds,
- broken seeds,
- shriveled seeds,
- malformed seeds,
- foreign material.

9. Physical Purity
Physical purity indicates the proportion of the seed lot that consists of the intended pure seed according
to the relevant testing standard.
A simplified calculation is:
Physical Purity (%)=Weight of Pure SeedTotal Sample Weight×100 
=         100  
For example, if 980 g of a 1,000 g sample is classified as pure seed:
Purity=9801000×100=98%  =   1000  100 = 98\%
Actual seed testing follows prescribed procedures and categories.

10. Inert Matter
Inert matter may include:
- soil particles,
- stones,
- chaff,
- plant debris,
- broken non-seed material,
- other non-seed materials.
High inert matter means less useful planting material per unit weight.

11. Other Crop Seeds
A seed lot may accidentally contain seeds from another crop.
For example:
Wheat seed lot+Other crop seed→Physical contamination  +    This can affect:
- crop uniformity,
- harvesting,
- processing,
- crop quality.

12. Weed Seeds
Weed seeds in a crop seed lot are particularly undesirable because planting them can introduce or
increase weeds in the field.
Contaminated Seed→Weed Introduction→Future Weed Management Problem     

Therefore certified seed standards generally place limits on undesirable seed contaminants.

13. Physiological Quality
Physiological quality mainly describes the biological performance of seeds.
Important parameters include:
- viability,
- germination,
- vigor,
- moisture content,
- storability.

14. Seed Viability
Viability is the ability of a seed to remain alive and capable of producing a normal plant under suitable
conditions.
Viable Seed=Living Seed with Potential to Germinate  =    
A seed can be alive but dormant.
Therefore:
Viability≠Immediate Germination   

15. Seed Germination
Germination is the process by which a viable seed resumes active growth under suitable conditions.
A typical sequence is:
Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence      

16. Germination Percentage
A common expression is:
Germination (%)=Number of Normal SeedlingsNumber of Seeds Tested×100  =         100  
Example:
If 95 normal seedlings are obtained from 100 seeds:
Germination=95% =95\%

17. Normal vs Abnormal Seedlings
A germination test generally distinguishes between:

Normal seedlings
Have sufficient essential structures to continue normal development under the test conditions.

Abnormal seedlings
Show defects that prevent normal continued development.
This distinction is important because merely observing some seed growth does not necessarily mean
the seed has produced a normal plant.

18. Seed Vigor
Seed vigor refers to the overall properties that determine how rapidly and uniformly seeds germinate
and produce normal seedlings across a range of environmental conditions.
A useful conceptual model is:
Vigor=Speed+Uniformity+Stress Performance+Seedling Strength  =  
+   +   +    

19. Germination vs Vigor
This is one of the most important distinctions in seed science.
Suppose:

Seed lot Germination Vigor
A
95% High
B
95% Low
Both lots may pass a standard germination test, but Lot A may:
- germinate faster,
- emerge more uniformly,
- tolerate suboptimal field conditions better.
Therefore:
High Germination⇏High Vigor     

20. Seed Moisture
Seed moisture content is a critical quality parameter.
It affects:
- respiration,
- aging,
- fungal growth,
- insect activity,
- storage life,
- mechanical properties.
Generally:
Seed Moisture↑→Metabolic Activity↑→Deterioration Risk↑       
The safe moisture range is commodity- and storage-system-specific.

21. Moisture Content Calculation
A common wet-basis expression is:
MC(%)=Fresh Weight−Dry WeightFresh Weight×100         100  
For example:

Fresh weight = 100 g
Dry weight = 90 g
MC=100−90100×100=10%MC=   100  100 =10\%

22. Seed Health
Seed health refers to the biological condition of seeds with respect to:
- fungi,
- bacteria,
- viruses or virus-associated agents,
- nematodes,
- insects and other pests where relevant.
Seed health is important because seed can act as a vehicle for introducing pathogens into a field.

23. Seed-Borne Pathogens
Pathogens may occur:
- on the seed surface,
- beneath the seed coat,
- within seed tissues,
- in associated plant debris.
They may cause:
- poor germination,
- seed rot,
- seedling diseases,
- field disease outbreaks.

24. Seed Health vs Seed Treatment
These are different.

Seed health
Describes the biological condition of the seed.

Seed treatment
Is an intervention used to protect or improve seed performance.
Examples can include:
- fungicidal treatment,
- biological treatment,
- insecticidal treatment where appropriate,
- inoculation.

25. Seed Size
Seed size can influence:
- stored reserves,
- early seedling growth,
- emergence under some conditions.
Larger seeds often contain more stored reserves.
However:
Large Seed≠Automatically High-Quality Seed   
A small seed with excellent viability and vigor can outperform a large but deteriorated seed.

26. Seed Uniformity
Uniform seed size and quality can support:
- uniform sowing,
- consistent emergence,
- synchronized crop development.
However, uniform appearance alone does not guarantee genetic or physiological uniformity.

27. Seed Damage
Mechanical damage can occur during:
- harvesting,
- threshing,
- shelling,
- cleaning,
- grading,
- transport.
Damage may be:
- visible,
- invisible,
- internal.
Consequences may include:
Mechanical Damage→Reduced Vigor→Poor Germination/Storage 
   

28. Seed Coat Damage
Damage to the seed coat can:
- increase susceptibility to pathogens,
- alter water uptake,
- expose internal tissues,
- reduce storage life.
In some seeds, excessive cracking can cause particularly severe deterioration.

29. Seed Purity and Weed Management
High-quality seed reduces the risk of introducing weed seeds.
This is important because:

Poor Seed Purity→Weed Introduction→Higher Weed-Control Cost 
   
Thus seed quality has consequences beyond germination.

30. Seed Quality and Crop Establishment
The complete relationship is:
Seed Quality→Germination→Emergence→Plant Population→Crop Establishment       
Poor seed quality may produce:
- gaps,
- uneven plants,
- weak seedlings,
- delayed emergence.

31. Seed Quality and Plant Population
The final crop stand depends on more than the number of seeds planted.
Conceptually:
Established Plants=Seeds Planted×Germination×Field Establishment 
=      
where the factors are expressed as fractions.
For example:
100,000×0.90×0.90=81,000100,000  0.90  0.90 = 81,000
approximately 81,000 established plants.

32. Laboratory Germination vs Field Emergence
A seed lot may have high laboratory germination but poor field emergence.
Why?

Field conditions may include:
- drought,
- waterlogging,
- soil crust,
- compaction,
- salinity,
- unsuitable temperature,
- pests,
- diseases,
- incorrect planting depth.
Therefore:
Laboratory Germination≠Guaranteed Field Emergence    

33. Seed Quality and Soil Conditions
Even excellent seed requires a suitable seedbed.
Seed Quality+Suitable Seedbed→Good Establishment  +     
Important soil factors include:
- moisture,
- temperature,
- oxygen,
- salinity,
- pH,
- compaction,
- structure.

34. Seed Quality and Water
Insufficient water:

Water↓→Imbibition↓→Germination↓      Excess water:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Respiration Stress→Poor Establishment   O 2   Thus both drought and waterlogging can reduce the field performance of good seed.

35. Seed Quality and Temperature
Seed germination usually has a temperature range:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      
Below the suitable range:
Metabolic Activity↓→Germination Slows   Above the suitable range:
Heat Stress↑→Germination/Seedling Performance↓    Exact thresholds vary by crop and cultivar.

36. Seed Quality and Salinity
High salinity can reduce water uptake by creating osmotic stress.
Salinity↑→Osmotic Stress↑→Water Uptake↓→Germination↓         
Some crops and varieties are more salt tolerant than others.

37. Seed Quality and Soil Compaction
Compacted soil can physically restrict:
- radicle emergence,
- root penetration,

- water movement,
- oxygen diffusion.
Thus:
Compaction→Root Emergence/Development↓     

38. Seed Quality and Storage
Initial seed quality strongly affects storage performance.
High-quality seed entering storage generally has greater potential to remain useful than already
deteriorated seed.
Initial Quality+Proper Storage→Better Quality Retention  +     

39. Seed Deterioration
Seed deterioration is the progressive loss of physiological function with age and unfavorable
conditions.
It can involve:
- membrane deterioration,
- oxidative damage,
- enzyme dysfunction,
- mitochondrial damage,
- DNA/protein damage,
- reduced repair capacity.
A simplified sequence:
Aging→Cellular Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Death         

40. Factors Affecting Seed Deterioration
Major factors include:

- seed moisture,
- temperature,
- oxygen,
- storage duration,
- initial seed quality,
- mechanical damage,
- insects,
- microorganisms.
Generally:
High Moisture+High Temperature→Faster Deterioration  +     

41. Seed Longevity
Seed longevity is the length of time a seed lot remains sufficiently viable and useful under defined
storage conditions.
It depends on:
Longevity=f(Species,Genotype,Initial Quality,Moisture,Temperature,Storage Environment)
  = f(  ,  ,  ,  ,
 ,   )  

42. Orthodox vs Recalcitrant Seeds
Orthodox seeds
Generally tolerate drying and can often be stored for extended periods under suitable cool and dry
conditions.
Many major field crops have orthodox seeds.

Recalcitrant seeds
Are sensitive to drying and may not tolerate conventional low-moisture storage.
Some tropical tree crops produce such seeds.
Therefore:

Seed Quality Management=f(Seed Storage Biology)  =
f( )  

43. Seed Quality Testing
A comprehensive seed-quality assessment may include:

Physical tests
- purity,
- other crop seeds,
- weed seeds,
- inert matter,
- seed size.

Physiological tests
- germination,
- viability,
- vigor,
- moisture.

Genetic tests
- varietal identity,
- genetic purity.

Health tests
- seed-borne pathogens,
- pests where relevant.

44. Germination Test
The germination test determines the proportion of seeds producing normal seedlings under specified
test conditions.
It is useful for:
- comparing seed lots,

- determining planting quality,
- calculating seed requirements,
- quality control.

45. Viability Test
Viability tests determine whether seeds contain living tissues capable of supporting germination.
One important method is the tetrazolium test.
The test uses a biochemical reaction associated with living tissues to produce characteristic staining.

46. Vigor Tests
Vigor can be assessed using different methods, depending on crop and testing purpose.
Examples include:
- accelerated aging,
- electrical conductivity,
- seedling growth measurements,
- controlled deterioration,
- emergence-related tests.
Different vigor tests measure different aspects of seed performance.

47. Electrical Conductivity Test
Electrical conductivity can be used to assess membrane integrity.
Deteriorated seeds may leak more electrolytes when soaked in water.
Conceptually:
Membrane Damage↑→Electrolyte Leakage↑→EC↑     EC  
It is particularly useful in some seed types, but interpretation is species- and protocol-dependent.

48. Accelerated Aging Test
Accelerated aging exposes seeds to elevated temperature and humidity for a defined period and then
evaluates their performance.
It is used as a vigor test because more vigorous seed lots often retain better germination after controlled
aging.

49. Seed Moisture Testing
Moisture can be measured using:
- oven methods,
- moisture meters,
- other validated methods.
The method must be appropriate for the seed type and properly calibrated.

50. Genetic Purity Testing
Genetic purity can be evaluated using:
- grow-out tests,
- morphological characteristics,
- biochemical methods,
- molecular markers,
- DNA-based methods.
Modern molecular approaches can provide highly specific identity information, but the appropriate
method depends on the crop and regulatory/testing purpose.

51. Seed Health Testing
Seed health tests can detect:
- fungi,
- bacteria,

- viruses/virus-associated agents,
- nematodes,
- other relevant organisms.
Methods may include:
- incubation,
- culture,
- microscopy,
- molecular diagnostics,
- immunological methods.

52. Seed Certification
Seed certification is a regulated quality-assurance process used in applicable seed systems to verify
that seed production and handling meet defined standards.
It can address:
- genetic identity,
- genetic purity,
- physical purity,
- germination,
- seed health,
- labeling,
- production standards.
Exact classes and standards vary by country and regulatory system.

53. Seed Classes
Formal seed classes vary by regulatory system, but many systems distinguish categories associated
with progressively larger-scale multiplication, such as:
- breeder seed,
- foundation seed,

- certified seed.
The exact terminology and standards must be interpreted according to the relevant national seed
regulations.

## 54. Labeling
Seed labeling can provide important information such as:
- crop,
- variety/hybrid,
- lot number,
- germination,
- purity,
- test date,
- treatment information,
- producer details,
- applicable certification information.
The exact label requirements depend on regulations.

55. Seed Quality and Seed Treatment
Seed treatment can improve protection and establishment under suitable conditions.
However:
Seed Treatment≠Replacement for Poor Seed Quality    
Treatment cannot turn genetically incorrect or dead seed into high-quality seed.

56. Seed Priming and Quality
Priming may improve:
- germination speed,

- emergence uniformity,
- performance under some stress conditions.
But it can also affect storability and must be performed using an appropriate protocol.
Therefore:
Priming Benefit=f(Crop+Cultivar+Protocol+Storage)  = f( +
 + + )  

57. Seed Quality and Sowing Depth
Even high-quality seed can fail if planted too deeply.
Excessive depth may cause:
- delayed emergence,
- depletion of seed reserves,
- poor emergence,
- increased exposure to soil pathogens.
The appropriate depth depends on:
- crop,
- seed size,
- soil type,
- moisture,
- planting method.

58. Seed Quality and Seed Rate
Seed quality affects the amount of seed needed to achieve the desired plant population.
A simplified model:
Required Seed Quantity∝Target Plant PopulationExpected Establishment          
If germination or field establishment decreases, the required planting quantity may increase, subject to
agronomic recommendations.

59. Seed Quality and Yield
Seed quality primarily affects establishment, which influences later crop performance.
Seed Quality→Stand Establishment→Crop Uniformity→Resource Capture→Yield Potential       
But yield is also strongly influenced by:
- genetics,
- climate,
- soil,
- water,
- nutrition,
- pests,
- diseases,
- crop management.
Therefore:
High-Quality Seed≠Guaranteed High Yield   

60. Seed Quality and Uniformity
Uniform seed quality can produce more synchronized:
- emergence,
- vegetative growth,
- flowering,
- maturity,
- harvesting.
This can simplify farm operations.

61. Seed Quality and Crop Rotation
Good seed quality is important in every crop of a rotation.
For example:
Crop A→Crop B→Crop C    Each crop needs quality planting material to establish properly.
Poor seed in one component can affect the productivity of the entire rotation.

62. Seed Quality in Direct Seeding vs
Transplanting
Direct seeding
Seed quality directly determines:
- germination,
- emergence,
- field population.

Transplanted crop
Seed quality affects:
- nursery emergence,
- seedling vigor,
- transplant quality,
- establishment after transplanting.
Thus seed quality remains important even when the crop is transplanted.

63. Seed Quality in Hybrid Crops
Hybrid seed requires attention to:
- parental identity,
- genetic purity,
- hybrid purity,

- germination,
- vigor.
A genetically incorrect hybrid seed lot may produce plants that do not possess the expected hybrid
characteristics.

64. Seed Quality and Farmer Decision-Making
Before planting, a farmer should consider:
Correct Crop+Correct Variety+Good Quality Seed+Suitable Seedbed  +
  +   +    
This is the foundation of crop establishment.

65. Practical Seed Quality Inspection
Before planting, inspect:

Step 1 — Identity
Is it the intended:
- crop?
- variety?
- hybrid?

Step 2 — Physical condition
Look for:
- impurities,
- weed seeds,
- broken seeds,
- insects,
- discoloration,
- mold.

Step 3 — Quality information
Check:

- germination,
- purity,
- lot number,
- testing date,
- treatment information.

Step 4 — Storage condition
Check:
- moisture,
- packaging,
- pests,
- temperature,
- humidity.

Step 5 — Field suitability
Consider:
- soil moisture,
- temperature,
- salinity,
- drainage,
- planting depth.

66. A Complete Seed Quality Decision
Framework
Seed Source→Identity Verification→Physical Quality→Germination/
Viability→Vigor→Health→Storage Condition→Field Suitability→Planting           

67. Seed Quality Failure Diagnosis
Problem: Low laboratory germination
Investigate:
- viability,
- dormancy,
- aging,
- storage,
- mechanical damage,
- seed-borne disease.

Problem: Good laboratory germination but poor field emergence
Investigate:
- moisture,
- temperature,
- oxygen,
- compaction,
- crusting,
- salinity,
- sowing depth,
- pests,
- disease.

Problem: Good emergence but poor crop uniformity
Investigate:
- seed lot uniformity,
- sowing depth,
- field variability,
- soil moisture,
- nutrient distribution,
- pests/disease,

- genetic mixtures.

68. Seed Quality vs Seed Health vs Seed Vigor
Term
Main meaning
Seed quality
Overall suitability of seed
Seed health
Biological health/pathogen status
Viability
Whether seed is alive
Germination Ability to produce normal seedlings under test conditions
Vigor
Speed, uniformity and robustness of performance
Genetic purity Correct genetic identity
Physical purity Freedom from unwanted physical/seed contaminants

69. Most Important Relationships
Quality and viability
Seed Quality→Viability  Quality and germination
Viability+Suitable Conditions→Germination + 
 Quality and vigor
High Vigor→Fast + Uniform Establishment  Quality and health
Healthy Seed→Lower Seed-Borne Disease Risk  Quality and genetics
Genetic Purity→Expected Crop Identity  70. Master Seed Quality Model
The complete model is:

Seed Quality= Genetic Quality+Physical Quality+Physiological Quality+Seed Health    =&\;  \\ &+ \\ &+
 \\ &+     
Then:
Seed Quality→Germination→Emergence→Uniform Plant Stand→Crop Growth→Yield Potential d          
But the complete outcome is:
Seed Quality+Soil+Water+Temperature+Nutrition+Pest/Disease Management+Crop Management→Fi
nal Yield  +   +   +   +
  +   +     

71. Exam-Ready Answer
Seed quality is the combination of genetic, physical, physiological and health
characteristics of a seed lot that determine its suitability for planting and its ability to
produce normal, vigorous and genetically appropriate plants under suitable
conditions. Genetic quality ensures correct variety or hybrid identity and genetic
purity. Physical quality includes purity, absence of undesirable crop and weed seeds,
inert matter and excessive mechanical damage. Physiological quality includes
viability, germination, vigor, moisture content and storability. Seed health concerns
the presence or absence of important seed-borne pathogens and pests. Important seedquality tests include physical purity, germination, viability, vigor, moisture, geneticpurity and seed-health tests. High-quality seed promotes rapid and uniform
germination and establishment, but field performance also depends on soil moisture,
temperature, oxygen, salinity, compaction, sowing depth and other management
factors. Thus, high-quality seed is necessary for good crop establishment but does not
by itself guarantee high yield.

72. Final Memory Map
Four pillars
SEED QUALITY=Genetic⏟Correct+Physical⏟Clean+Physiological⏟Alive & Vigorous+Health⏟Healthy
  =        +
       +        +         

Genetic
Correct variety/hybrid

Physical
Clean and undamaged

Physiological
Viable + germinable + vigorous + appropriately dry

Health
Free from important seed-borne problems within applicable standards

The most important distinction
Viability≠Germination≠Vigor≠Genetic Purity≠Physical Purity       
And the complete agricultural relationship is:
Good Seed→Good Germination→Good Emergence→Good Stand→Strong Crop Establishment→Yiel
d Potential        
One-line memory: Seed quality means having the correct genetic identity, physical
purity, physiological performance and health necessary to produce a healthy, vigorous
and uniform crop under suitable field conditions.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
3. Certified Seed
## 1. Introduction
Certified seed is seed produced, processed, tested, labeled and distributed under an official seed
certification system to verify that it meets prescribed standards for identity, genetic purity, physical
purity, germination and other applicable quality requirements.
In simple terms:

Certified seed is officially quality-assured seed produced according to prescribed
standards and verified through a certification system.
The basic idea is:
Approved Variety→Controlled Seed Production→Field Inspection→Processing→Seed Testing→Certif
ication→Labeled Seed Lot         

2. Why Certified Seed Is Important
The purpose of seed certification is to provide farmers with seed that is reasonably assured to have the
declared identity and required quality according to the applicable certification standards.
It helps maintain:
- varietal identity,
- genetic purity,
- physical purity,
- acceptable germination,
- seed health requirements where applicable,
- proper seed production practices,
- traceability,
- quality labeling.
Therefore:
Certified Seed→Reliable Planting Material→Better Crop Establishment 
   

3. Certified Seed vs Ordinary Seed
Not every seed sold or planted necessarily has the same certification status.
Feature
Official certification
Production standards
Field inspection
Seed testing

Certified seed
Yes, under applicable system
Defined by certification system
Generally required
Required according to applicable

Uncertified/ordinary seed
Not necessarily
May vary
Not necessarily
May or may not be formally

Feature

Certified seed
standards
Regulated certification label/tag

Uncertified/ordinary seed
tested
Requirements vary

Labeling
Genetic identity
Defined standard
Variable
assurance
Traceability
Generally stronger
Variable
Quality standards
Prescribed
May vary
The exact legal categories and requirements depend on the country's seed regulations.

4. Certified Seed Is a Seed Class, Not a Crop
Type
Certified seed can belong to many different crops.
For example:
- certified wheat seed,
- certified rice seed,
- certified soybean seed,
- certified chickpea seed,
- certified maize seed,
- certified cotton seed.
Thus:
Certified=Quality/Certification Status  =    
It is not a botanical classification.

5. Certified Seed in the Seed Multiplication
Chain
Seed multiplication commonly follows a controlled sequence such as:
Breeder Seed→Foundation Seed→Certified Seed     
The exact terminology and multiplication classes can vary by national regulatory system.

The fundamental idea is that seed multiplication proceeds through controlled generations so that
genetic identity and purity are maintained.

6. Breeder Seed
Breeder seed is seed produced and maintained under the responsibility of the plant breeder or
- riginating institution according to the applicable seed system.
It serves as an important source for further seed multiplication.
Conceptually:
Breeding Material→Breeder Seed    

7. Foundation Seed
Foundation seed is produced from an approved higher-generation seed source under prescribed
standards to maintain genetic identity and purity.
Conceptually:
Breeder Seed→Foundation Seed    
It is then used for further multiplication.

8. Certified Seed
Certified seed is produced from eligible seed sources according to prescribed certification
requirements.
Foundation/Eligible Source→Certified Seed Production 
  
The precise permitted parentage depends on the applicable certification rules.

9. Main Objectives of Seed Certification
Seed certification aims to:

1. Maintain genetic identity
Ensure that the seed belongs to the declared variety/hybrid.

2. Maintain genetic purity
Limit contamination by other varieties or off-types.

3. Maintain physical purity
Control unwanted seeds and inert matter.

4. Ensure acceptable physiological quality
Verify parameters such as germination according to prescribed standards.

5. Control seed production
Ensure prescribed field and processing requirements are followed.

6. Improve traceability
Enable identification of the seed lot and its certification status.

10. Genetic Identity
One of the most important aspects of certification is ensuring that the seed corresponds to the declared
genetic type.
For example, if a farmer purchases certified seed of a particular wheat variety, the seed production
system is designed to maintain that variety's identity.

11. Genetic Purity
Genetic purity means maintaining the desired variety without unacceptable contamination by:
- other varieties,
- off-types,
- volunteers,
- unwanted cross-pollination.
Conceptually:

Seed Production Controls→Genetic Purity    

12. Physical Purity
Certified seed is also evaluated for physical composition.
Potential contaminants include:
- weed seeds,
- other crop seeds,
- inert matter,
- broken or damaged material.
A simplified purity concept is:
Physical Purity (%)=Pure Seed WeightTotal Sample Weight×100  =
        100  
The actual certification test follows prescribed standards.

13. Germination Requirement
Certified seed must generally meet the applicable minimum germination standard for the crop and seed
class.
A common germination calculation is:
Germination (%)=Normal SeedlingsSeeds Tested×100  =
        100  
For example:
If 90 normal seedlings develop from 100 tested seeds:
Germination=90% =90\%
The minimum acceptable value is crop- and standard-specific.

14. Seed Vigor
Seed certification systems primarily operate through prescribed quality standards; vigor is an
important biological property of seed but should not automatically be assumed to have the same
mandatory certification threshold in every crop or regulatory system.
Nevertheless, high-vigor seed is desirable because it can provide:
- rapid germination,
- uniform emergence,
- stronger seedlings,
- better performance under some suboptimal conditions.

15. Seed Health
Seed certification and seed testing may include requirements relating to seed health, depending on the
crop and applicable regulations.
Potential concerns include:
- fungi,
- bacteria,
- viruses or virus-associated agents,
- nematodes,
- insects or other pests where relevant.
Seed health is important because:
Infected Seed→Seed-Borne Disease→Potential Field Spread     

16. Seed Certification Is a Process
Certification is not simply putting a label on a seed bag.
It is a chain of quality-control activities:
Source Seed→Field Selection→Seed Production→Field Inspection→Harvest→Processing→Sampling
→Laboratory Testing→Certification→Labeling            

17. Field Selection
The seed-production field must meet applicable requirements.
Considerations can include:
- previous crop,
- isolation requirements,
- soil,
- drainage,
- field history,
- volunteer plants,
- disease history,
- accessibility,
- suitability for the crop.
Previous crop is particularly important because volunteer plants from a previous crop can contaminate
the seed-production field.

## 18. Isolation
Isolation is the separation of a seed-production field from potential sources of genetic contamination.
This can be achieved through:
- physical distance,
- time separation,
- controlled pollination,
- removal of neighboring contaminating plants,
- other crop-specific measures.
It is particularly important for crops that are cross-pollinated.
Conceptually:

Isolation→Unwanted Cross-Pollination↓→Genetic Purity↑       
Exact isolation requirements vary by crop and certification standard.

19. Field Inspection
Seed-production fields may be inspected at specified stages.
Inspectors can look for:
- off-types,
- other crop plants,
- weeds,
- volunteer plants,
- disease,
- prohibited mixtures,
- compliance with field requirements.
The objective is to detect problems before harvest and processing.

## 20. Rouging
Rouging is the removal of undesirable plants from a seed-production field.
Plants may be removed because they are:
- off-types,
- diseased,
- volunteer plants,
- plants of another variety,
- otherwise unsuitable under the production standard.
Conceptually:
Off-Type Detection→Rouging→Genetic Purity Maintenance 
   

## 21. Harvesting
After the seed crop satisfies field requirements, harvesting must be performed carefully.
Poor harvesting practices can cause:
- mechanical damage,
- mixture between varieties,
- contamination,
- loss of traceability.
Equipment cleaning and lot separation are therefore important.

## 22. Processing
Seed processing can involve:
- cleaning,
- grading,
- separation,
- drying,
- treatment,
- packaging.
The objective is to improve the physical and physiological quality of the seed lot while maintaining its
identity.

23. Seed Cleaning
Cleaning removes unwanted materials such as:
- soil,
- chaff,
- stones,
- weed seeds,
- other crop seeds,
- damaged material.

Common equipment includes:
- air-screen cleaners,
- graders,
- gravity separators,
- specialized separators.

24. Seed Grading
Grading separates seeds based on characteristics such as:
- size,
- density,
- shape,
- weight.
The purpose can include improving uniformity and processing quality.
However:
Grading≠Genetic Purification   
Grading cannot reliably remove all genetically different seeds simply because they may look similar.

25. Seed Drying
Drying may be necessary to bring seed moisture to a suitable level for safe handling and storage.
The principle is:
Appropriate Moisture→Lower Deterioration Risk    
Excessive drying or excessive heat can damage some seeds, so drying conditions must be appropriate
to the crop.

26. Seed Treatment
Certified seed may be treated when permitted and required/desired according to the applicable system.

Possible treatments include:
- fungicides,
- insecticides where appropriate,
- biological agents,
- inoculants,
- other approved treatments.
Treatment information should be properly communicated on the label where required.

## 27. Sampling
After processing, representative samples are collected for testing.
This is critical because:
Representative Sample→Reliable Test Result    
An accurate laboratory test cannot compensate for a badly collected sample.

28. Laboratory Testing
Testing may examine:
- germination,
- physical purity,
- moisture,
- seed health,
- other parameters required by the applicable standard.
Testing methods are standardized or prescribed by the relevant authority.

29. Certification Decision
The certification authority evaluates whether the seed lot meets the applicable requirements.
Conceptually:

Field Standards+Laboratory Standards+Documentation→Certification Decision  +   +     
If requirements are satisfied, the seed lot can be certified according to the applicable rules.

30. Certification Tag or Label
Certified seed is normally accompanied by an official certification tag, label or other prescribed
identification.
The label can provide information such as:
- crop,
- variety,
- seed class,
- lot number,
- germination,
- purity,
- test information,
- producer/agency information,
- treatment information where applicable.
Exact labeling requirements vary by jurisdiction.

## 31. Traceability
Traceability means the ability to associate a seed lot with its:
- source,
- production field,
- processing,
- testing,
- certification,
- packaging.
Conceptually:

Seed Lot→Production Record→Testing Record→Certification Record 
    
This is important for quality control and investigation of problems.

32. Certified Seed and Seed Lot Identity
A certified seed lot should remain identifiable throughout:
Production→Harvest→Processing→Storage→Packaging→Sale        
Mixing different seed lots can compromise identity and traceability.

33. Seed Certification and Storage
Certification does not mean the seed will remain in perfect condition forever.
After certification:
Storage Conditions→Quality Retention    
Poor storage can reduce:
- germination,
- vigor,
- seed health,
- longevity.
Therefore:
Certified at Production≠Immune to Deterioration    

34. Certified Seed and Seed Quality
Certification is closely related to seed quality, but the concepts are not identical.

Seed quality
The biological and physical condition of the seed.

Seed certification
The official process used to verify compliance with prescribed standards.
Therefore:
Certification→Quality Assurance Framework    

35. Certified Seed vs High-Quality Seed
A seed can have good biological quality, but certification provides an officially regulated status under
a certification system.
Thus:
High Quality≠Automatically Certified    
and:
Certified≠Permanent Guarantee of Future Quality    
Storage and handling after certification still matter.

36. Certified Seed and Genetic Purity
This is particularly important for:
- hybrid seed production,
- varietal seed production,
- crops with significant cross-pollination,
- seed multiplication programs.
The objective is:
Parent Identity→Controlled Multiplication→Genetic Purity     

37. Self-Pollinated vs Cross-Pollinated Crops
Certification requirements differ according to the reproductive biology of the crop.

Self-pollinated crops
Generally have lower natural cross-pollination risk, although varietal mixtures and other contamination
can still occur.
Examples include many:
- wheat,
- rice,
- chickpea,
- lentil.

Cross-pollinated crops
Require greater attention to:
- isolation,
- flowering synchrony,
- pollen movement,
- neighboring fields,
- pollination control.
Examples include:
- maize,
- many forage grasses,
- several horticultural crops.
Exact reproductive behavior varies by crop and cultivar.

38. Hybrid Seed Certification
Hybrid seed production requires particularly careful control of:
- parental lines,
- male and female parents,
- pollination,

- isolation,
- removal of off-types,
- harvest and processing,
- genetic purity.
The basic crossing concept is:
P1×P2→F1 
The resulting F₁ seed must retain the intended hybrid identity.

39. Certified Seed and Farmer Benefits
Certified seed can provide farmers with greater assurance regarding:
- variety identity,
- minimum specified germination,
- physical purity,
- seed quality standards,
- traceability.
Potential practical benefits include:
- more reliable crop establishment,
- more predictable crop characteristics,
- reduced risk of unwanted seed contamination,
- better planning of crop maturity and management.

40. Certified Seed and Yield
Certified seed can contribute to better crop establishment and genetic consistency.
But:
Certified Seed≠Guaranteed Maximum Yield   
Final yield depends on:

Genetics+Seed Quality+Soil+Water+Climate+Nutrition+Pest/Disease Management+Farm Management
  +   +   +   +   +
  +   +    

41. Certified Seed and Crop Establishment
The relationship can be represented as:
Certified Seed→Specified Seed Quality→Germination→Emergence→Plant Population→Crop Establis
hment        
However, field conditions still determine actual emergence.

42. Certified Seed and Seed Rate
Seed rate should not be chosen merely because the seed is certified.
It depends on:
- crop,
- variety/hybrid,
- seed size,
- germination percentage,
- desired population,
- planting method,
- expected field establishment.
A simplified relationship is:
Required Seeds≈Desired Established PlantsExpected Establishment Fraction          

43. Certified Seed and Previous Crop
The previous crop can affect seed-production quality through:
- volunteer plants,

- disease carryover,
- weed problems,
- residual herbicides,
- soil fertility,
- soil moisture.
Therefore:
Previous Crop→Seed-Production Field Suitability    

44. Certified Seed and Isolation Distance
Isolation is particularly important when pollen from another field could contaminate the seed crop.
The general principle is:
Cross-Pollination Risk↓when appropriate isolation/control increases     
Exact isolation distances are crop- and certification-standard-specific and should not be treated as
- ne universal number.

45. Certified Seed and Seed Health
Certification can help control seed-borne disease risks where health standards apply.
But farmers should understand:
Certification≠Absolute Freedom from Every Pathogen    
Certification operates according to defined standards and testing procedures.

46. Certified Seed and Storage
After purchasing certified seed, the farmer should maintain appropriate storage conditions.
Important factors include:
- moisture,

- temperature,
- humidity,
- pests,
- packaging,
- cleanliness,
- storage duration.
The principle is:
Certified Seed+Good Storage→Quality Retention  +  
  

47. Common Problems Despite Certified Seed
Even certified seed may perform poorly if:
- stored incorrectly,
- exposed to excessive moisture,
- exposed to high temperatures,
- damaged during handling,
- planted too deeply,
- planted in dry soil,
- planted in waterlogged soil,
- affected by salinity,
- attacked by soil pests/pathogens.
Therefore:
   but:
Field Success=Seed Quality+Field Conditions+Management  =   +   +    

48. Certification vs Seed Testing
These are not identical.

Seed testing
Determines characteristics such as:
- germination,
- purity,
- moisture,
- health.

Certification
A broader official process that can include:
- verification of seed source,
- field inspections,
- production requirements,
- processing controls,
- laboratory testing,
- labeling,
- documentation.
Therefore:
Seed Testing⊂Broader Certification Process   
in systems where testing is one component of certification.

49. Certification vs Seed Processing
Processing
Physically prepares the seed:
Cleaning→Grading→Drying→Treatment    Certification
Verifies compliance with prescribed standards through a broader system.
Thus:

Processing≠Certification   

50. Certification vs Seed Treatment
Certification
Quality-assurance and regulatory process.

Seed treatment
A biological/chemical/physical intervention applied to seed.
Thus:
Certified Seed≠Treated Seed    
Certified seed may be treated or untreated depending on the seed and applicable system.

51. Certification vs Genetic Modification
These are completely different concepts.

Certification
A quality-assurance/regulatory status.

Genetic modification
A method of changing genetic material.
Therefore:
Certified≠GM    
A seed's certification status does not by itself indicate whether it was genetically modified.

52. Certification vs Hybrid
Hybrid
Describes genetic origin:
P1×P2→F1P 1 P 2 F 1

Certified
Describes quality/certification status.
Thus a hybrid seed can also be certified where the applicable certification system provides for it.

53. Important Terminology
Term
Meaning
Seed certification Official process for verifying compliance with prescribed seed standards
Certified seed
Seed lot that has met applicable certification requirements
Breeder seed
Controlled seed source associated with the breeder/originating institution
Foundation seed Controlled multiplication class used to maintain genetic identity/purity
Genetic purity
Degree to which seed conforms to intended genetic type
Physical purity
Proportion of desired pure seed relative to contaminants
Germination
Ability to produce normal seedlings under defined conditions
Vigor
Ability to germinate rapidly and uniformly and perform under varying conditions
Seed health
Biological condition regarding pathogens/pests
Isolation
Separation/control used to reduce unwanted genetic contamination
Rouging
Removal of undesirable plants
Seed lot
Defined quantity of seed handled as a unit
Seed testing
Laboratory/controlled assessment of seed properties
Seed processing Cleaning, grading, drying and related preparation

54. Certified Seed — Complete Workflow
Approved Seed Source↓Suitable Seed-Production Field↓Isolation/Production Controls↓Crop Establish
ment↓Field Inspection↓Rouging↓Harvest↓Separate Lot Handling↓Cleaning/Grading/
Drying↓Sampling↓Laboratory Testing↓Certification Decision↓Official Label/
Tag↓Storage↓Distribution↓Farmer Planting  & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & 
\\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ &     

55. What Makes Certified Seed Different?
The central difference is official verification.
Seed Quality=Biological Characteristics  =    
whereas:
Seed Certification=Official Verification of Defined Standards  =
   

56. Practical Checklist Before Buying Certified
Seed
A farmer should verify:

Identity
- Crop
- Variety/hybrid
- Seed class

Certification
- Official certification tag/label
- Lot number
- Applicable certification information

Quality
- Germination
- Physical purity
- Test information
- Seed health/treatment information where applicable

Packaging
- Intact package
- Proper labeling
- No obvious moisture or pest damage

Storage
- Properly stored before purchase
- No excessive moisture
- No visible fungal growth or insects

57. Critical Distinctions for Exams
Certified seed vs seed quality
Seed Quality=Characteristics =   
Certification=Official Quality-Assurance Process =   

Certified seed vs treated seed
Certification≠Treatment   

Certified seed vs hybrid seed
Hybrid=Genetic Origin =   
Certified=Certification Status =   

Certification vs testing
Testing≠Entire Certification Process   

Certification vs storage
Certified at one point≠Protected from future deterioration    

58. Exam-Ready Definition
Certified seed is seed produced, processed, tested, labeled and distributed under an
- fficial seed certification system and shown to meet prescribed standards for
characteristics such as varietal identity, genetic purity, physical purity and
germination, along with other applicable requirements. Certification involves
controlled seed multiplication, field inspection, isolation and rouging where required,
careful harvesting and lot handling, processing, representative sampling, laboratory
testing, certification and official labeling. Certified seed provides farmers with greater
assurance about the identity and specified quality of the planting material, but it does
not guarantee maximum yield or immunity from deterioration because field
conditions, storage and crop management also affect performance.

59. Final Master Memory Map
Seed production
Breeder/Approved Source→Foundation→Certified 
   

Quality assurance
Field Standards+Processing+Testing+Documentation→Certification  +
  +   +     

Genetic purity
Isolation+Field Inspection+Rouging+Controlled Multiplication→Genetic Purity  +   +   +     

Farmer outcome
Certified Seed→Reliable Planting Material→Good Establishment Potential→Uniform Crop→Yield Po
tential       
But always remember:
Certified Seed≠Guaranteed High Yield    
because final crop performance is determined by the interaction of genetics, seed quality, soil, water,
climate, nutrients, pests, diseases and management.
One-line memory: Certified seed is officially quality-assured planting seed whose
production, identity and specified quality are verified against prescribed certification
standards.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
4. Foundation Seed
## 1. Introduction
Foundation seed is a high-quality seed class produced under controlled conditions from an approved
higher-generation seed source, such as breeder seed, with the objective of maintaining the genetic
identity and genetic purity of a crop variety during seed multiplication.
The basic seed multiplication chain is:
Breeder Seed→Foundation Seed→Certified Seed→Farmer’s Crop 
    
The exact seed classes, generations and regulatory requirements vary among countries and crops.

## 2. Definition
Foundation seed is seed produced from an approved higher-generation seed source
under prescribed standards and supervision to maintain the genetic identity and
purity of a crop variety and to provide seed for further multiplication, particularly
certified seed production.
The key purpose is:
Foundation Seed→Maintain Genetic Identity and Purity    

3. Why Foundation Seed Is Important
Foundation seed forms an important bridge between breeding/early-generation seed and large-scale
commercial seed multiplication.
Without careful multiplication:
Repeated Multiplication→Genetic Contamination→Loss of Varietal Purity    Foundation seed helps maintain the characteristics of the original variety during multiplication.

4. Position in the Seed Multiplication System
A simplified sequence is:
Breeder Seed→Foundation Seed→Certified Seed→Farmers      

Breeder seed
Maintains the original genetic identity associated with the breeding program.

Foundation seed
Multiplies that material while maintaining high genetic purity.

Certified seed
Provides larger quantities of quality-assured seed for commercial planting under the applicable
certification system.

5. Main Objective
The central objective is:
Maximum Practical Maintenance of Varietal Identity and Genetic Purity   
Foundation seed is therefore particularly important when multiplying:
- newly developed varieties,
- important established varieties,
- parental lines,
- hybrid parents,
- other genetically valuable planting material.

6. Foundation Seed Is Not a Variety
This is an important distinction.
Foundation seed does not describe a particular crop variety.

It describes the seed class/status within a seed multiplication system.
For example:
- foundation seed of wheat,
- foundation seed of rice,
- foundation seed of chickpea,
- foundation seed of a particular maize line.
Therefore:
Foundation=Seed Class =   
not:
Foundation=Crop Type = 

7. Foundation Seed vs Certified Seed
Feature

Foundation seed

Certified seed

class

Approved higher-generation seed
