CERTIFIED = SEED CERTIFICATION STATUS   
One-line memory: Local varieties are geographically and culturally associated crop
varieties or populations maintained through continued cultivation and selection, often
providing valuable local adaptation, genetic diversity, food-quality traits and genetic
resources for future agriculture and breeding.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Improved Varieties
## 1. Introduction
An improved variety is a crop variety that has been deliberately developed or selected through
plant breeding and evaluation to possess one or more desirable characteristics compared with
existing varieties or populations.
These desirable characteristics may include:
- higher yield,
- disease resistance,
- pest resistance,
- drought tolerance,
- heat tolerance,
- improved quality,
- early or suitable maturity,
- better nutrient-use efficiency,
- lodging resistance,
- improved adaptation,
- better processing characteristics.
The central idea is:
Existing Genetic Material→Breeding + Selection + Testing→Improved Variety     

## 2. Definition
An improved variety is a crop variety developed or selected through systematic plantbreeding methods to possess desirable agronomic, biological, quality or adaptation
traits and to provide improved performance compared with an appropriate existing
variety or standard under defined conditions.
The word "improved" is relative. It means improvement for particular traits and target environments,
not that the variety is superior in every possible characteristic.

3. Why Are Improved Varieties Developed?
Plant breeders develop improved varieties to solve agricultural problems.
Major objectives include:

Yield improvement
Yield↑  Disease resistance
Disease Resistance↑  Pest resistance
Pest Damage↓  Drought tolerance
Yield Stability under Water Stress↑  Heat tolerance
Useful under high-temperature conditions.

Early maturity
Crop Duration↓  This may help fit crops into specific cropping systems.

Quality improvement
Examples:
- protein,
- oil,

- fiber,
- cooking quality,
- nutritional quality,
- processing quality,
- taste.

Lodging resistance
Lodging Risk↓  Adaptation
Development for specific:
- climates,
- soils,
- production systems,
- regions.

4. Basic Development Process
A simplified breeding pathway is:
Breeding Objective→Parent Selection→Crossing/Variation→Selection→Evaluation→MultiEnvironment Testing→Release→Seed Multiplication          
This process can take several generations and often several years.

5. Sources of Genetic Variation
Breeders need genetic variation from which desirable traits can be selected.
Sources include:
- existing varieties,
- landraces,

- germplasm collections,
- wild relatives,
- breeding lines,
- mutants,
- introduced material,
- related species.
Conceptually:
Genetic Diversity→Variation→Selection→Improvement      

6. Important Breeding Methods
### 6.1 Selection
Selection identifies plants with desirable characteristics.
Population→Select Desirable Plants→Next Generation    Types include:
- mass selection,
- pure-line selection,
- family selection,
- progeny selection,
- recurrent selection.

## 7. Hybridization
Hybridization involves crossing selected parents.
P1×P2→F1 
The objective is to combine desirable characteristics from different parents.
For example:

Parent A: High Yield  Parent B: Disease Resistance  ⇓ Breeding Population with Both Traits 
Selection is then used to identify desirable offspring.

8. Mutation Breeding
Mutations can create genetic variation.
Breeders may use:
- physical mutagens,
- chemical mutagens.
The resulting populations are screened for desirable traits.
Conceptually:
Mutation→Genetic Variation→Selection→Improved Line      

9. Backcross Breeding
Backcrossing is used to introduce a desirable trait into an otherwise suitable variety.
For example:
Elite Variety×Disease-Resistant Donor   followed by repeated crossing with the elite parent:
F1×Elite ParentF 1 and selection for the desired resistance trait.
Goal:
Desired Trait+Adapted Elite Background  +    

10. Marker-Assisted Selection
Modern breeding can use molecular markers linked to desirable genes or genomic regions.

DNA Marker→Identify Desired Genotype→Select Plant     
This can make selection more efficient for some traits.

11. Genomic Selection
Genomic selection uses genome-wide marker information to predict breeding value.
Conceptually:
Genome-Wide Markers→Prediction Model→Estimated Breeding Value→Selection      
It is particularly useful for traits controlled by many genes.

12. Biotechnology and Gene Editing
Modern crop improvement may also use:
- tissue culture,
- doubled haploids,
- molecular markers,
- genomic selection,
- transgenic approaches,
- gene editing.
However:
Improved Variety≠Automatically GM   
An improved variety may be developed entirely through conventional breeding.
Likewise:
Hybrid≠GM   

13. Improved Variety vs Local Variety
Feature
Origin
Selection
Genetic
structure

Testing

Local variety
Improved variety

Can be relatively high

Released disease-resistant variety

Local≠Improved   
But a local variety can be used as a parent in developing an improved variety.

14. Improved Variety vs OPV
An improved variety may be:
- self-pollinated,
- open-pollinated,
- hybrid,
- clonally propagated,
depending on the crop and breeding system.
Therefore:
Improved≠OPV    
"Improved" describes the breeding/improvement status, whereas "open-pollinated" describes the
reproductive/maintenance system.

15. Improved Variety vs Hybrid
This is one of the most important distinctions.

Improved variety
Describes a variety developed through breeding and selection.

Hybrid
Describes offspring produced from a specific cross between selected parents.
Therefore:
Improved Variety≠Hybrid   
But:
Improved Hybrid   
is possible.
A hybrid can itself be the result of a breeding program.

16. Improved Variety vs Certified Seed
These describe different things.

Improved variety
The genetic variety.

Certified seed
The seed certification class/status under the applicable system.
Thus:
Improved Variety≠Certified Seed    
An improved variety can be multiplied and distributed through an applicable certified-seed system.

17. Improved Variety vs Foundation Seed
Improved variety
Genetic identity/variety.

Foundation seed
A seed class used in the multiplication chain.
Therefore:
Variety≠Seed Class   
For example:
Improved Variety→Breeder Seed→Foundation Seed→Certified Seed 
   The exact seed-generation terminology and regulatory requirements depend on the applicable seed
system.

18. Improved Variety vs Landrace
A landrace is generally a locally adapted traditional population.
An improved variety is deliberately developed or selected through a breeding program.
However:
Landrace→Breeding Parent→Improved Variety     
Thus traditional genetic resources can contribute directly to modern crop improvement.

19. Improved Variety vs Cultivar
Cultivar is a broad term for a cultivated plant group maintained for particular characteristics.
An improved variety can be a cultivar.
Thus:
Improved Variety⊆Cultivated Varieties/Cultivars 
  
depending on terminology and context.

20. Genetic Purity
An improved variety must maintain its characteristic genetic identity during seed multiplication.

Genetic purity means that the seed lot largely conforms genetically to the intended variety, within
applicable standards.
Factors that can reduce genetic purity include:
- unwanted cross-pollination,
- mechanical mixtures,
- off-types,
- volunteer plants,
- incorrect seed handling.
Therefore:
Isolation+Rouging+Proper Seed Production→Genetic Purity  +  
+     

21. Field Evaluation
Before release, a candidate variety is evaluated for relevant traits.
Examples:
- yield,
- maturity,
- plant height,
- lodging,
- disease resistance,
- pest response,
- grain/fruit quality,
- drought response,
- adaptation.

22. Multi-Environment Testing
A variety that performs well in one location may not perform similarly elsewhere.
Therefore candidates may be tested across:
- locations,

- seasons,
- soil types,
- climatic conditions.
Conceptually:
Variety×Environment→Observed Performance     
This is important because:
Good Performance in One Location≠Guaranteed Performance Everywhere    

23. Genotype × Environment Interaction
A variety's performance depends on its genetic characteristics and environment.
P=G+E+G×E 
where:
- PP = observed phenotype/performance,
- GG = genotype,
- EE = environment,
- G×EG E = interaction.
This is why improved varieties are generally developed for specific target environments or adaptation
zones.

## 24. Stability
A useful breeding objective is yield or trait stability across environments.
For example:
Stable Performance→Lower Sensitivity to Environmental Variation 
 But "stability" can be defined and measured in different ways.

25. Improved Varieties and Yield
Yield can be improved through different components.
For cereals:
Yield≈Plants/Area×Grains/Plant×Grain Weight      
Breeding can affect:
- tillering,
- productive tillers,
- grain number,
- grain size,
- harvest index,
- duration,
- resource-use efficiency.

26. Improved Varieties and Disease Resistance
Breeders may introduce resistance to:
- fungal diseases,
- bacterial diseases,
- viral diseases,
- nematodes.
Conceptually:
Resistance Gene/Trait→Reduced Disease Impact→Yield Protection     
Resistance is generally disease- and genotype-specific.

27. Improved Varieties and Pest Resistance
Breeding can also target resistance or tolerance to insects and other pests.
Possible mechanisms include:

- antibiosis,
- antixenosis,
- tolerance.
This can reduce crop losses under relevant pest pressure.

28. Improved Varieties and Drought
Breeding objectives may include:
- drought escape,
- drought avoidance,
- drought tolerance,
- efficient water use,
- root traits,
- reproductive stability.
The general relationship is:
Improved Genetics→Better Response to Water Stress→Potential Yield Stability     

29. Improved Varieties and Heat
Breeders may select for:
- heat-tolerant reproductive development,
- pollen viability,
- grain filling,
- canopy temperature regulation,
- appropriate maturity.
Again:
Improved Variety≠Heat-Proof Variety   Performance depends on stress intensity, duration and developmental stage.

30. Improved Varieties and Maturity
Breeding can modify crop duration.
For example:
Long Duration→Medium Duration→Early Maturity    Early varieties may help:
- fit multiple crops,
- avoid late-season drought,
- escape some heat,
- match rainfall duration.
But shorter duration does not automatically mean higher yield.

31. Improved Varieties and Lodging
Lodging is the bending or falling of plants.
Breeding can improve:
- stem strength,
- plant height,
- root anchorage,
- architecture.
Therefore:
Improved Architecture→Lower Lodging Risk→Potential Harvest Efficiency Improvement     

32. Improved Varieties and Quality
Improvement may target:

Cereals
- grain size,

- milling quality,
- cooking quality,
- protein,
- nutritional traits.

Pulses
- protein,
- cooking quality,
- seed size,
- dhal recovery.

Oilseeds
- oil content,
- fatty-acid composition,
- meal quality.

Cotton
- fiber length,
- fiber strength,
- micronaire and other quality traits.

Fruits and vegetables
- size,
- firmness,
- flavor,
- color,
- shelf life,
- processing quality.

33. Improved Varieties and Nutritional Quality
Breeding can target:
- protein,

- micronutrients,
- provitamin compounds,
- amino-acid composition,
- biofortification traits.
Conceptually:
Breeding→Nutritional Trait Improvement→Biofortified Crop     

34. Improved Varieties and Resource-Use
Efficiency
Breeding can target improved efficiency in:
- water use,
- nitrogen use,
- phosphorus acquisition,
- radiation use.
For example:
Higher Yield per Unit Water→Improved Water-Use Efficiency 
 But efficiency depends on both genotype and management.

35. Improved Varieties and Climate Adaptation
Crop breeding is an important long-term adaptation strategy.
Potential breeding targets include:
Heat+Drought+Flood+Salinity+Disease→Climate-Resilient Varieties  +
  +   +   +     
No single variety can necessarily tolerate all stresses.

36. Variety Release
A candidate variety normally undergoes appropriate evaluation before official release/notification
where such a regulatory system applies.
The general process is:
Breeding Line→Testing→Evaluation→Approval/Release→Seed Multiplication→Distribution        
The exact legal process varies by country.

37. Seed Multiplication After Development
Once a variety is developed and released, its seed must be multiplied while maintaining genetic identity
and quality.
A simplified chain is:
Breeder Material→Foundation Multiplication→Certified/Commercial Seed→Farmer      
The exact classes depend on the seed system and regulations.

38. Improved Variety and Seed Quality
An improved genetic variety does not automatically mean every seed lot is high quality.
Remember:
Variety Quality≠Seed Lot Quality    
A good variety can be sold in a poor-quality seed lot if seed production, processing or storage is
inadequate.
Seed quality includes:
Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  +   +
  +    

39. Improved Variety and Farming System
Improved varieties can be used in:
- conventional farming,
- organic systems where permitted,
- integrated farming,
- conservation agriculture,
- irrigated farming,
- rainfed farming,
- precision agriculture.
Therefore:
Improved Variety≠Farming System   

40. Improved Variety and Inputs
An improved variety may be developed to perform under particular input levels.
Some varieties are selected for:
- high-input conditions,
- low-input environments,
- rainfed conditions,
- irrigated conditions,
- specific nutrient conditions.
Therefore it is important to match:
Variety+Environment+Management  +   +
   

41. Improved Variety Selection by Farmer
When selecting an improved variety, consider:
1. target crop,
2. agro-climatic conditions,

3. soil,
4. irrigation availability,
5. maturity duration,
6. disease/pest pressure,
7. yield potential,
8. quality requirements,
9. market,
10.cropping system,
11.seed availability,
12.input requirements,
13.lodging risk,
14.stress tolerance,
15.local performance evidence.
A variety should be selected according to the production objective, not simply because it is called
"improved."

42. Improved Variety in Crop Rotation
Variety choice should fit the rotation.
For example:
Early-Maturing Variety→Earlier Harvest→Time Available for Next Crop 
  Thus varietal duration can influence cropping intensity and rotation planning.

43. Improved Variety and Plant Population
Improved varieties may have different optimal population requirements.
Yield depends on interactions among:
- plant density,
- architecture,

- tillering/branching,
- resource availability.
Therefore:
Improved Variety+Appropriate Plant Population→Better Performance 
+     

44. Improved Variety and Agronomic
Management
Genetic improvement works together with management.
Genetics+Environment+Management→Actual Performance  +
  +     
A high-performing variety cannot fully express its genetic potential when severely limited by:
- water,
- nutrients,
- temperature,
- pests,
- diseases,
- poor soil,
- poor management.

45. Major Categories of Improvement
Improved varieties can be classified by their main objective:
Improvement objective
Example trait
Yield improvement
Higher grain yield
Disease resistance
Resistance to a fungal disease
Pest resistance
Reduced insect damage
Drought adaptation
Better performance under water stress
Heat tolerance
Better reproductive performance under heat
Flood tolerance
Better survival under submergence/waterlogging
Salinity tolerance
Better growth under salt stress
Early maturity
Shorter crop duration

Improvement objective
Example trait
Lodging resistance
Stronger stem
Quality improvement
Better cooking/milling/processing
Nutritional improvement Higher micronutrient content
Oil improvement
Higher oil content/desired fatty acids
Fiber improvement
Better fiber properties
Resource-use efficiency Better water/nutrient efficiency

46. Important Genetic Concepts
Genotype
The genetic constitution of the plant.

Phenotype
Observable characteristics.
P=G+E+G×E 

Heritability
The proportion of phenotypic variation attributable to genetic variation in a particular population and
environment.
It helps breeders understand how effectively a trait may respond to selection.
High heritability does not automatically mean a trait is more important or that improvement is
guaranteed.

47. Improved Variety and Heterosis
Some improved varieties are developed through hybrid breeding and exploit heterosis, also called
hybrid vigor.
P1×P2→F1 
The F₁ can show improved performance for certain traits relative to the parents.
However:
Improved Variety≠Necessarily Hybrid   

48. Improved Variety and Genetic Diversity
Breeding can sometimes narrow genetic diversity if a small number of varieties become widely
cultivated.
Therefore crop improvement must be balanced with conservation of genetic resources.
Crop Improvement+Genetic Resource Conservation  +    
Both are important for long-term agriculture.

50. Critical Comparisons
Term
What it primarily describes
Local variety
Geographical/traditional association
Landrace

OPV

Improved variety Variety developed/selected through systematic breeding
Hybrid
Specific offspring from selected parental cross
Cultivar
Cultivated plant grouping
Foundation seed Seed multiplication class
Certified seed
Seed certification status
Germplasm
Genetic material used for conservation/breeding
The same crop material can sometimes fit multiple descriptions.

For example:
Improved OPV   
is possible.
Likewise:
Improved Hybrid   
is possible.
And an improved variety can be produced from local germplasm:
Local Germplasm→Breeding→Improved Variety     

51. Common Misconceptions
"Improved variety means hybrid."

❌ No.

An improved variety can be an OPV, inbred variety, hybrid or another breeding product.

"Improved means genetically modified."

❌ No.

Conventional breeding can produce improved varieties.
Breeding Improvement≠GM  "Improved variety always produces higher yield."

❌ Not necessarily.

Performance depends on:
G+E+G×EG+E+G E
and management.

"Improved variety is always better than a local variety."

❌ Not universally.

The appropriate comparison depends on:

- location,
- crop,
- trait,
- farming system,
- market,
- stress conditions.

"Improved variety means certified seed."

❌ No.

A variety and a seed certification class are different concepts.

"Once a variety is improved, it never changes."

❌ Not necessarily.

Genetic purity can decline through:
- contamination,
- admixture,
- unwanted cross-pollination,
- poor seed multiplication.

52. Exam-Ready Answer
An improved variety is a crop variety deliberately developed or selected through
systematic plant-breeding methods to possess one or more desirable characteristics
such as higher yield, disease or pest resistance, drought or heat tolerance, suitable
maturity, lodging resistance, improved quality, nutritional value or adaptation to
particular environments. Improved varieties are developed using genetic resources
such as existing varieties, landraces, breeding lines and wild relatives and may involve
selection, hybridization, backcrossing, mutation breeding, molecular-marker-assisted
selection, genomic selection or other breeding technologies. Candidate varieties are
evaluated for agronomic performance, quality, resistance and adaptation, often across
multiple environments, before release under the applicable regulatory system. After
development, seed is multiplied through appropriate seed classes while maintaining
genetic identity and seed quality. An improved variety should not be confused with a
hybrid, open-pollinated variety, local variety, foundation seed or certified seed.

“Improved” primarily describes the result of genetic improvement through breeding,
“hybrid” describes the genetic origin from a specific cross, “open-pollinated”
describes a reproductive/maintenance system, “foundation” describes a seed
multiplication class, and “certified” describes a seed certification status. The actual
performance of an improved variety depends on genotype, environment and
management, so a variety improved for one trait or environment is not automatically
superior in every situation.

53. Final Master Memory
Improved variety
Genetic Variation→Breeding→Selection→Testing→Improved Variety       

Main objectives
Yield+Resistance+Stress Tolerance+Quality+Adaptation  +   +
  +   +    

Performance
Actual Performance=Genotype+Environment+G×E+Management Effects  =   +   + G E +    

Most important distinction
IMPROVED = WHAT HAS BEEN GENETICALLY IMPROVED  
OPV = HOW THE VARIETY IS REPRODUCED/MAINTAINED  
HYBRID = HOW THE F1 GENETIC COMBINATION IS PRODUCED  1  
FOUNDATION = SEED MULTIPLICATION CLASS  
CERTIFIED = SEED CERTIFICATION STATUS  
LOCAL = GEOGRAPHICAL/TRADITIONAL ASSOCIATION  
One-line memory: An improved variety is a deliberately bred and evaluated crop
variety developed to provide one or more desirable improvements—such as yield,
resistance, stress tolerance, maturity, quality or adaptation—under defined
agricultural conditions.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Disease-Resistant Varieties
## 1. Introduction
A disease-resistant variety is a crop variety that possesses genetic characteristics that enable it to
reduce, restrict, delay, or otherwise withstand infection and/or disease development caused by a
particular plant pathogen, compared with a susceptible variety under comparable conditions.
The pathogens responsible for plant diseases include:
- fungi,
- oomycetes,
- bacteria,
- viruses,
- viroids,
- nematodes,
- phytoplasmas and other disease-causing organisms.
The basic concept is:
Genetic Resistance→Reduced Disease Development→Reduced Damage/Loss     
However, resistance is always relative to a particular pathogen, pathogen strain/population, crop
genotype, environment, and disease-development conditions.

## 2. Definition
A disease-resistant variety is a crop variety containing inherited characteristics that
reduce the ability of a particular pathogen to infect, colonize, reproduce, or cause
disease in the host, resulting in lower disease severity or slower disease development
under specified conditions.
The key word is resistant, not necessarily immune.
Resistance≠Immunity   

3. What Is Plant Disease?
Plant disease is a harmful disturbance of normal plant structure or function caused by a pathogen or
- ther disease-causing factor.
A simplified disease process is:
Host+Pathogen+Favorable Environment+Time→Disease  +   +
  +     
This is related to the disease triangle.

4. Disease Triangle
Disease development generally requires interaction among:
1. susceptible host,
2. pathogen,
3. favorable environment.
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease  +   +  
  
A resistant variety modifies the host component.
Resistant Host→Disease Development↓     
But environmental conditions and pathogen characteristics still matter.

5. Disease Resistance Is Genetic
Disease resistance is often controlled by genes or combinations of genes.
For example:
Resistance Gene/Alleles→Host Defense→Reduced Disease    Resistance can involve:
- single major genes,

- several genes,
- many genes with small effects,
- combinations of resistance mechanisms.

6. How Does a Resistant Plant Defend Itself?
Plants have multiple layers of defense.

Pre-existing defenses
These may include:
- waxy cuticle,
- thick cell walls,
- structural barriers,
- antimicrobial compounds.

Induced defenses
After pathogen recognition, plants may activate:
- defense genes,
- reactive oxygen species,
- antimicrobial compounds,
- cell-wall reinforcement,
- phytoalexins,
- pathogenesis-related proteins,
- hypersensitive responses,
- systemic defense responses.
Simplified:
Pathogen Recognition→Defense Activation→Pathogen Growth↓ 
    

7. Major Types of Disease Resistance
A. Vertical resistance
Often associated with resistance to particular pathogen races or strains and can be controlled by one or
a few major genes.
Characteristics:
- often strong resistance,
- pathogen-specific,
- sometimes relatively easy to select,
- can be vulnerable if the pathogen population changes.
Conceptually:
R gene↔Specific pathogen factor    

8. Horizontal Resistance
Horizontal resistance generally involves multiple genes and provides broader, often partial resistance.
Characteristics:
- controlled by multiple genes,
- often reduces disease development rather than completely preventing infection,
- potentially broader durability,
- influenced by environment.
Multiple Genes→Partial/Broad Resistance    
The vertical/horizontal terminology is useful but can oversimplify the complexity of modern plantpathogen genetics.

9. Major-Gene Resistance
A major gene can have a relatively large effect on resistance to a particular pathogen population.
Major Gene→Large Resistance Effect    

Such resistance can sometimes be overcome when the pathogen evolves or when a different pathogen
race is present.

10. Quantitative Resistance
Quantitative resistance involves multiple genes, each potentially contributing a smaller effect.
G1+G2+G3+⋯+Gn→Reduced Disease Severity   
It may be expressed as:
- slower disease development,
- lower pathogen reproduction,
- reduced lesion size,
- delayed epidemic progression.

11. Complete vs Partial Resistance
Complete or near-complete resistance
Disease development may be very low or undetectable under particular test conditions.

Partial resistance
Disease can occur, but its:
- severity,
- rate,
- spread,
- pathogen reproduction,
may be reduced.
Therefore:
Resistance≠No Infection in Every Situation   

12. Resistance vs Tolerance
This distinction is extremely important.

Resistance
The plant limits pathogen infection or development.
Resistance→Pathogen/Disease Development↓   Tolerance
The plant may become infected but maintains relatively better growth or yield despite disease.
Tolerance→Yield Loss per Unit Disease↓   Thus:
Resistance≠Tolerance   
A plant can be relatively resistant, tolerant, both, or neither.

13. Resistance vs Immunity
Immunity
No disease development under the specific interaction and conditions.

Resistance
Disease development is reduced.
In agriculture, the term resistant is generally more appropriate than assuming absolute immunity.
Resistance=Reduced Disease =   
not necessarily:
Resistance=Zero Disease = 

14. Disease Resistance vs Pest Resistance
Disease resistance
Targets disease-causing pathogens.

Examples:
- fungal resistance,
- bacterial resistance,
- viral resistance.

Pest resistance
Targets damaging organisms such as insects and mites.
Therefore:
Disease Resistance≠Insect Resistance   
Although both are forms of genetic crop protection.

15. Examples of Disease-Resistance Traits
Disease-resistant varieties may be developed against diseases such as:
- wheat rusts,
- wheat powdery mildew,
- rice blast,
- bacterial blight of rice,
- maize downy mildew,
- late blight of potato,
- tomato bacterial/viral diseases,
- chickpea wilt,
- chickpea Ascochyta blight,
- cotton bacterial blight,
- soybean rust and other diseases.
The actual resistance depends on the particular variety and pathogen population.

16. Rice Example
Rice breeding programs may target resistance to diseases such as:

- blast,
- bacterial blight,
- sheath blight and others.
Conceptually:
Resistant Rice Variety→Disease Development↓→Potential Yield Loss↓       
But resistance to one disease does not mean resistance to every rice disease.

17. Wheat Example
Wheat varieties may be evaluated for resistance to:
- leaf rust,
- stem rust,
- stripe/yellow rust,
- powdery mildew,
- other diseases.
A variety resistant to one rust type may not necessarily be resistant to another.
Therefore:
Disease Resistance is Disease-Specific   

18. Potato Example
Potato breeding may target resistance to:
- late blight,
- early blight,
- viruses,
- bacterial diseases,
- nematode-associated problems.
Late-blight resistance is particularly important because pathogen populations can evolve.

19. Chickpea Example
Chickpea varieties may be developed for resistance to diseases such as:
- Fusarium wilt,
- Ascochyta blight,
- Botrytis gray mold in relevant environments.
A disease-resistant genotype can reduce losses when the corresponding pathogen is present.

20. Disease Resistance and Pathogen Variability
Pathogens are not genetically uniform.
A pathogen population may contain different:
- races,
- strains,
- pathotypes,
- isolates,
- genotypes.
Therefore:
Resistance to Pathogen Population A≠Guaranteed Resistance to Population B    
This is one reason resistance must be evaluated under relevant conditions.

21. Pathogen Evolution and Resistance
Breakdown
Pathogens can evolve through:
- mutation,
- recombination,
- selection,
- migration,

- gene flow,
- changes in pathogen populations.
If a resistant variety is widely cultivated, strong selection pressure can favor pathogen variants capable
- f overcoming that resistance.
Conceptually:
Resistant Crop→Selection Pressure→Resistant Pathogen Variant→Resistance Breakdown     This does not occur for every resistance mechanism, but it is an important risk.

22. Durable Resistance
Durable resistance is resistance that remains effective for a long period across relevant pathogen
populations and environments.
Breeding strategies may attempt to increase durability through:
- multiple resistance genes,
- gene pyramiding,
- quantitative resistance,
- diverse genetic backgrounds,
- deployment strategies.

23. Gene Pyramiding
Gene pyramiding means combining multiple resistance genes into one genetic background.
For example:
R1+R2+R3→Combined ResistanceR 1+R 2+R 3  The objective is to make it more difficult for a pathogen population to overcome all resistance
mechanisms simultaneously.
However, pyramiding does not guarantee permanent resistance.

24. Marker-Assisted Selection
Molecular markers can help identify plants carrying resistance genes or genomic regions.
DNA Marker→Identify Resistance Genotype→Select Plant     
This can accelerate breeding for some resistance traits.

25. Genomic Selection
For quantitatively inherited disease resistance:
Genome-Wide Markers→Prediction→Selection     
This can help breeders select promising plants before extensive field testing, although field validation
remains important.

26. Disease Screening
Candidate varieties are evaluated by exposing them to disease pressure under controlled or field
conditions.
Measurements may include:
- disease incidence,
- disease severity,
- lesion size,
- disease progress,
- pathogen load,
- yield loss.

Disease incidence
Disease Incidence (%)=Diseased Plants or UnitsTotal Plants or Units×100  =         100  

27. Disease Severity
Disease severity measures the amount of plant tissue affected.
For example:
Disease Severity (%)=Diseased Tissue AreaTotal Tissue Area×100  =         100  
Actual scoring systems can use disease-rating scales rather than direct area measurements.

28. Disease Progress
Disease does not necessarily remain constant.
It can increase over time:
Initial Infection→Disease Development→Spread→Peak Disease     Researchers may measure the area under the disease progress curve (AUDPC) to summarize disease
development over time.
Conceptually:
AUDPC≈∑[yi+yi+12](ti+1−ti)   2   ]
(t  i+1 -t i)  
where:
- yiy i = disease severity at observation ii,
- tit i = observation time.

29. Resistance Evaluation
A simplified evaluation process is:
Candidate Variety→Pathogen Exposure→Disease Measurement→Comparison with Standards→Resist
ance Classification       

30. Resistant Variety vs Fungicide
These are different disease-management tools.

Resistant variety
Uses genetic characteristics of the plant.

Fungicide
Uses a chemical treatment to manage fungal/oomycete diseases where applicable.
Therefore:
Genetic Control+Chemical Control +   
can sometimes be combined as part of integrated disease management.

31. Role in Integrated Disease Management
Disease-resistant varieties are an important component of integrated disease management (IDM).
A simplified IDM approach is:
Resistant Variety+Healthy Seed+Crop Rotation+Field Sanitation+Cultural Practices+Biological/
Chemical Measures where appropriate  +   +
  +   +   +    
The exact combination depends on the disease.

32. Resistant Variety and Seed Health
A resistant variety does not automatically mean that its seed is free of pathogens.
Remember:
Disease Resistance≠Seed Health    
A resistant variety can still carry seed-associated pathogens if seed health is not properly managed.

33. Resistant Variety and Soil Health
Soil conditions influence disease development.

Important factors include:
- soil moisture,
- drainage,
- temperature,
- pH,
- organic matter,
- soil microbiome,
- crop residues.
For example:
Waterlogging→Root Stress→Potentially Greater Disease Susceptibility 
  depending on the pathogen and crop.

34. Resistant Variety and Environment
Resistance expression can be influenced by:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- rainfall,
- leaf wetness,
- soil moisture,
- plant age,
- nutrition.
Therefore:
Resistance Expression=f(Genotype,Pathogen,Environment)  =
f( , , )  

35. Resistant Variety and Crop Stage
A variety may show different resistance levels at different developmental stages.
For example:

Seedling Resistance≠Adult-Plant Resistance   Some resistance mechanisms become stronger or weaker as the plant develops.

36. Adult-Plant Resistance
Some varieties may be more susceptible during early growth but develop stronger resistance later.
This is called adult-plant resistance when the resistance is expressed primarily at later developmental
stages.
This can be particularly important in some cereal diseases.

37. Seedling Resistance
Resistance expressed during early plant development is called seedling resistance.
Seedling Stage→Resistance Response   Seedling resistance and adult-plant resistance may be genetically distinct.

38. Host–Pathogen Interaction
Disease resistance can be understood as an interaction:
Host Genotype×Pathogen Genotype×Environment     
This is sometimes described as a G × G × E interaction.
It explains why the same variety can respond differently to different pathogen populations or
environments.

39. Gene-for-Gene Concept
In some plant-pathogen systems, a resistance gene in the host interacts with a corresponding pathogen
avirulence factor.
Simplified:

Rhost+Avrpathogen→Recognition→Defense   + Avr    
   
If the pathogen loses or changes the corresponding recognition signal:
Recognition↓→Resistance may break down   This is a simplified model and does not describe every resistance system.

40. Why Disease-Resistant Varieties Are
Important
1. Reduce disease losses
Disease↓→Yield Loss↓    2. Reduce dependence on some chemical treatments
Where resistance is effective, fewer disease-control interventions may sometimes be needed.

3. Improve yield stability
Disease Resistance→Greater Yield Stability under Disease Pressure 
 4. Reduce production risk
Disease outbreaks can create major uncertainty.

5. Support sustainable crop protection
Genetic resistance is often an important component of integrated disease management.

## 41. Limitations
Disease-resistant varieties are not perfect.

## 1. Disease-specific
Resistance to one pathogen does not guarantee resistance to another.

2. Pathogen evolution
Resistance can sometimes be overcome.

## 3. Environment-dependent
Resistance expression may vary with environmental conditions.

4. Genetic trade-offs
Breeding for one trait may sometimes affect other traits.

5. Resistance may be partial
Disease can still occur.

6. Seed quality still matters
Poor seed health or vigor can cause establishment problems even when the genotype is resistant.

7. Management remains important
A resistant variety does not eliminate the need for:
- crop rotation,
- sanitation,
- appropriate irrigation,
- field monitoring,
- healthy seed,
- other suitable disease-management practices.

42. Disease Resistance and Yield
A resistant variety does not necessarily have the highest yield under disease-free conditions.
Its advantage may become especially apparent under disease pressure.
Conceptually:
Yield Advantage from Resistance∝Disease Pressure×Effectiveness of Resistance     
This is conceptual rather than a universal quantitative equation.

43. Disease Resistance and Economic Benefit
Potential economic benefit comes from:
Yield Saved+Reduced Control Costs−Additional Seed/Management Costs 
+   -    
Therefore the value of resistance depends on:
- disease prevalence,
- disease severity,
- variety performance,
- seed cost,
- treatment cost,
- market price.

44. Selecting a Disease-Resistant Variety
Farmers should consider:
1. Which disease is important?
2. Which pathogen causes it?
3. Is the variety resistant to that particular disease?
4. Is resistance effective against local pathogen populations?
5. At which crop stage is resistance expressed?
6. What is the variety's maturity duration?
7. What is its yield performance?
8. What are its quality characteristics?
9. Is seed available from a reliable source?
10.Does it fit the local climate and soil?
11.Does it fit the cropping system?
12.Is additional disease management still required?

45. Disease-Resistant Variety Selection
Framework
Disease Identification→Pathogen Identification→Resistance Information→Local Validation→Variety
Selection→Integrated Management        

46. Disease Resistance vs Disease Avoidance
These are different.

Resistance
Plant genetically restricts disease development.

Escape/avoidance
The plant avoids exposure to favorable disease conditions because of:
- early maturity,
- altered flowering time,
- plant architecture,
- different planting time.
For example:
Early Maturity→Crop Leaves Disease-Favorable Period Earlier   This is not necessarily genetic resistance to the pathogen.

47. Disease Resistance vs Disease Tolerance vs
Escape
Mechanism
Main effect
Resistance Reduces infection/pathogen development
Tolerance Maintains performance despite disease
Escape
Avoids exposure or favorable conditions
Immunity No disease under the defined interaction/conditions

This distinction is highly important in plant breeding.

48. Disease-Resistant Variety vs Improved
Variety
A disease-resistant variety is a type of improved variety when disease resistance is a principal
improvement objective.
Disease-Resistant Variety⊆Possible Improved Varieties 
  
But:
Improved Variety≠Necessarily Disease-Resistant   
An improved variety could instead be improved for:
- yield,
- quality,
- drought,
- heat,
- maturity,
- lodging,
- nutrient-use efficiency.

49. Disease-Resistant Variety vs Local Variety
A local variety may possess disease resistance because of long-term local adaptation or genetic
diversity.
But being local does not automatically mean resistant.
Local≠Disease Resistant   
A local variety can also serve as a source of resistance genes for breeding.
Local Germplasm→Resistance Trait→Breeding→Improved Resistant Variety      

50. Disease-Resistant Variety vs Hybrid
These are different classifications.

Disease-resistant
Describes a trait.

Hybrid
Describes genetic origin through a cross.
Therefore:
Disease-Resistant Hybrid   
is possible.
Similarly:
Disease-Resistant OPV   
is possible.

51. Disease Resistance and Seed Classes
Disease resistance describes a genetic characteristic.
Seed classes such as:
- breeder seed,
- foundation seed,
- certified seed,
describe seed multiplication/certification categories.
Therefore:
Disease Resistance≠Seed Class   

52. Disease-Resistant Variety and Seed
Treatment
Even resistant varieties may sometimes benefit from appropriate seed treatment when the diseasemanagement recommendation calls for it.

Why?
Because resistance to a pathogen during crop growth does not necessarily mean protection against:
- seed-borne pathogens,
- soil-borne pathogens,
- unrelated diseases.
Thus:
Resistant Variety+Healthy Seed+Appropriate Management  +
  +    
provides a more complete disease-management strategy.

53. Breeding Process for Disease Resistance
A simplified breeding pathway:
Identify Disease Problem   
↓
Identify Resistance Source   
↓
Cross Resistant Parent with Adapted Parent    P1×P2→F1P 1 P 2 F 1
↓
F2/Later Generations→Select Resistant Plants    
↓
Disease Screening   
↓
Yield + Quality + Adaptation Testing   
↓
Multi-Environment Evaluation   
↓
Release of Resistant Variety   

54. Resistance Source
Resistance may come from:
- existing improved varieties,
- local varieties,
- landraces,
- breeding lines,
- wild relatives,
- related species.
Thus:
Genetic Resources→Resistance Genes→Breeding     

55. Resistance Monitoring
After release, resistance should ideally be monitored because:
Pathogen Population→Evolution→Possible Resistance Change    Breeders and plant pathologists may monitor:
- pathogen races,
- disease incidence,
- disease severity,
- resistance breakdown,
- new pathogen variants.

56. Complete Disease-Resistance Model
Resistance Gene(s)→Pathogen Recognition/Defense→Pathogen Growth↓→Disease Severity↓→Yield
Loss↓          
But:

Effectiveness=f(Host,Pathogen,Environment,Crop Stage)  = f( ,
 , , )  

57. Critical Terminology
Term
Meaning
Disease resistance
Genetic ability to restrict disease development
Susceptibility
Ability of host to develop disease when exposed
Tolerance
Ability to maintain performance despite disease
Immunity
No disease development under defined conditions
Vertical resistance
Often strong and pathogen-race specific
Horizontal resistance Often broader and quantitative/partial
Major-gene resistance Resistance with relatively large genetic effect
Quantitative resistance Resistance controlled by multiple genes
Durable resistance
Resistance remaining effective over long periods
Gene pyramiding
Combining multiple resistance genes
Disease incidence
Proportion of plants/units diseased
Disease severity
Amount of tissue/plant affected
## Audpc
Summary measure of disease development over time
Pathotype/race
Pathogen population differing in virulence characteristics
Adult-plant resistance Resistance expressed mainly at later plant stages
Seedling resistance
Resistance expressed during early development

58. Most Important Comparisons
Concept
Primary meaning
Improved variety
Variety deliberately developed for desirable traits
Disease-resistant variety Variety genetically reducing a particular disease
Local variety
Variety/population associated with a locality
Landrace
Locally adapted traditional population
OPV
Open-pollinated reproductive/maintenance system
Hybrid
F₁ from selected parental cross
Foundation seed
Seed multiplication class
Certified seed
Certification status
Disease resistance
Host genetic defense
Disease tolerance
Maintains performance despite disease
Disease escape
Avoids disease exposure/favorable conditions

59. Common Misconceptions
"Disease-resistant means disease-proof."

❌ Not necessarily.

Resistance≠Immunity  "A disease-resistant variety is resistant to all diseases."

❌ No.

Resistance is usually disease/pathogen-specific.

"Resistance means the pathogen cannot enter the plant."

❌ Not necessarily.

Some resistance mechanisms allow infection but restrict:
- pathogen multiplication,
- movement,
- lesion development,
- disease progression.

"Resistant varieties never need disease management."

❌ No.

Integrated management remains important.

"A local variety is automatically disease-resistant."

❌ No.

Local adaptation does not guarantee resistance.

"Hybrid means disease-resistant."

❌ No.

Hybrid describes genetic origin; resistance describes a trait.

"Certified seed means disease-resistant seed."

❌ No.

Certification and genetic resistance are separate concepts.

60. Exam-Ready Answer
A disease-resistant variety is a crop variety possessing inherited genetic characteristics
that reduce, restrict or delay infection, pathogen development or disease severity
caused by a particular plant pathogen. Disease resistance is an important objective of
plant breeding and may be controlled by major genes, multiple genes or combinations
- f resistance mechanisms. Plants can defend themselves through structural barriers
and induced biochemical and molecular defenses following pathogen recognition.
Disease resistance may be complete or partial and may be race-specific, quantitative,
broad, or relatively durable. It should be distinguished from tolerance, in which the
plant maintains productivity despite disease, and disease escape, in which the plant
avoids favorable conditions or exposure. The effectiveness of resistance depends on the
host genotype, pathogen population, environment and crop developmental stage.
Pathogen evolution can sometimes overcome resistance, so breeders may use strategies
such as gene pyramiding, quantitative resistance and diverse genetic backgrounds to
improve durability. Disease-resistant varieties are an important component of
integrated disease management and can reduce yield losses and sometimes reduce
dependence on other disease-control measures, but they do not eliminate the need for
healthy seed, crop rotation, sanitation, monitoring and other appropriate management
practices. Disease resistance is a genetic trait and should not be confused with hybrid
status, open-pollinated status, foundation seed or certified seed.

61. Final Master Memory
Disease-resistant variety
Resistance Genes+Pathogen Recognition+Plant Defense→Disease Development↓  +   +      

Disease triangle
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease  +   +  
  
A resistant variety changes the host component:

Resistant Host→Disease Risk/Severity↓     

Resistance is not absolute
Resistance≠Immunity≠Tolerance≠Escape      

Breeding
Resistance Source→Crossing→Selection→Disease Screening→Yield/Quality Testing→Resistant Vari
ety        

Most important distinction
DISEASE-RESISTANT = GENETICALLY REDUCES A PARTICULAR DISEASE  
IMPROVED = DEVELOPED FOR DESIRABLE TRAITS  
HYBRID = PRODUCED FROM A CONTROLLED PARENTAL CROSS  
OPV = MAINTAINED THROUGH OPEN POLLINATION  
CERTIFIED = SEED CERTIFICATION STATUS  
One-line memory: A disease-resistant variety uses inherited genetic traits to restrict or
reduce disease development by a particular pathogen, thereby helping protect crop
growth, yield and quality, but its effectiveness depends on the pathogen, environment,
crop stage and resistance mechanism.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Drought-Resistant Varieties
## 1. Introduction
A drought-resistant variety is a crop variety that possesses inherited characteristics allowing it to
maintain survival, growth, reproduction, yield, or yield stability better than susceptible varieties
when exposed to water-deficit conditions.

Drought resistance is not one single trait. It is a combination of mechanisms that help plants cope with:
- low rainfall,
- prolonged dry periods,
- low soil moisture,
- high atmospheric water demand,
- intermittent drought,
- drought during sensitive growth stages.
The central concept is:
Drought-Adapted Genetics→Better Water-Stress Response→Improved Survival/Growth/Yield Stabilit
y     

## 2. Definition
A drought-resistant variety is a crop variety possessing inherited traits that enable it
to maintain physiological function, growth, reproduction, survival and/or yield
relatively better than susceptible varieties under defined water-deficit conditions.
The term resistant should be interpreted relatively.
Drought Resistance≠Complete Drought Immunity 
  
A drought-resistant variety can still suffer damage when drought becomes sufficiently severe.

3. What Is Drought?
Drought is a condition in which water availability is insufficient to meet the water requirements of
plants over a period of time.
Agricultural drought can involve:
Low Rainfall→Soil Moisture Deficit→Reduced Root Water Uptake→Plant Water Stress      
Drought can result from:
- low rainfall,

- delayed monsoon,
- prolonged dry spell,
- high temperature,
- high evapotranspiration,
- low soil-water storage,
- inadequate irrigation.

4. Drought Resistance vs Drought Tolerance
These terms are often used interchangeably, but they can be distinguished conceptually.

Drought resistance
Broad ability of a plant to maintain performance under water deficit.

Drought tolerance
Ability to continue functioning despite low tissue water status.

Drought avoidance
Ability to maintain relatively favorable plant water status despite dry conditions.
For example:
Deep Roots→Water Access→Water-Status Maintenance     
is an example of drought avoidance.
Whereas:
Cellular Osmotic Adjustment→Function Maintained at Low Water Potential    
is associated with drought tolerance.

5. Drought Escape
Drought escape is another important strategy.
A variety may complete its reproductive cycle before severe drought develops.
For example:

Early Maturity→Earlier Reproduction→Drought Avoided Through Timing     
Therefore:
Resistance≠Tolerance≠Avoidance≠Escape     

6. Major Drought-Adaptation Mechanisms
A drought-resistant variety can use several mechanisms.

1. Drought escape
Complete life cycle before severe drought.

2. Drought avoidance
Maintain plant water status.

3. Drought tolerance
Maintain physiological functions despite dehydration.

## 4. Recovery
Recover after drought stress is relieved.

7. Deep Root System
One important drought-adaptation trait is the ability to access water from deeper soil layers.
Deep/Extensive Roots→Greater Soil Volume Explored→Potentially Greater Water Access     
Important root traits include:
- rooting depth,
- root length,
- root density,
- root branching,

- root angle,
- root hydraulic properties.
However, deep roots are useful only where deeper soil contains accessible water.

8. Root-to-Shoot Balance
Under drought, plants may alter biomass allocation.
Drought→Root/Shoot Allocation Changes   A greater root investment can potentially improve water acquisition, although excessive root
investment also has carbon costs.
Therefore:
More Roots≠Automatically Better   
Root effectiveness depends on:
- soil depth,
- soil moisture distribution,
- root conductivity,
- soil compaction,
- nutrient availability.

9. Stomatal Regulation
Stomata control:
- CO₂ entry,
- water vapor loss.
During drought:
Soil Moisture↓→ABA Signaling→Stomatal Closure     
This reduces water loss but also reduces CO₂ entry.
Therefore:
Stomatal Closure→Water Loss↓    but potentially:
CO₂ Uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓    A drought-adapted genotype must balance water conservation and carbon gain.

10. Abscisic Acid — ABA
ABA is an important plant hormone in drought responses.
A simplified pathway is:
Water Deficit→ABA↑→Stomatal Regulation+Stress-Response Genes 
 ABA  +    
ABA can contribute to:
- stomatal closure,
- stress signaling,
- gene expression,
- osmotic adjustment,
- root responses.

11. Water-Use Efficiency
A drought-adapted variety may show improved water-use efficiency.
A simplified definition is:
WUE=Biomass or YieldWater Used        
However, WUE can be defined at different levels:
- instantaneous leaf WUE,
- intrinsic WUE,
- agronomic WUE,
- crop water productivity.
These should not be treated as identical measurements.

12. Transpiration Efficiency
Transpiration efficiency relates carbon gain to water lost through transpiration.
Conceptually:
Carbon Gain÷Transpiration    
A genotype may conserve water through:
- stomatal regulation,
- leaf characteristics,
- canopy architecture,
- reduced unnecessary transpiration.

13. Leaf Area Reduction
Some drought-adapted plants may reduce leaf area.
Leaf Area↓→Transpirational Surface↓→Water Loss Potential↓       
But reduced leaf area also means reduced potential light interception.
Therefore an optimal balance is required.

14. Leaf Rolling
Some cereals can respond to water stress through leaf rolling.
Drought→Leaf Rolling→Exposed Leaf Area↓→Water Loss Potential↓       Leaf rolling can be an adaptive response, although its value depends on severity and timing.

15. Osmotic Adjustment
Under drought, some plants accumulate compatible solutes such as:
- proline,

- soluble sugars,
- amino acids,
- glycine betaine,
- other osmolytes.
Conceptually:
Drought→Osmolyte Accumulation→Osmotic Adjustment→Cell Water Retention      
This helps maintain cellular hydration and function under water deficit.

16. Cell-Membrane Stability
Drought can cause:
- membrane damage,
- oxidative stress,
- electrolyte leakage.
Drought-adapted plants may maintain better membrane stability.
One experimental indicator is electrolyte leakage.
Better Membrane Stability→Lower Cellular Damage 
  

17. Antioxidant Defense
Drought can increase reactive oxygen species (ROS).
Drought→ROS↑→Oxidative Stress   ROS  Plants use antioxidant systems such as:
- superoxide dismutase (SOD),
- catalase (CAT),
- ascorbate peroxidase (APX),
- glutathione-related systems.

Conceptually:
Antioxidant Defense→ROS Control→Cell Protection     

18. Photosynthesis Under Drought
Drought commonly reduces photosynthesis through:
1. stomatal closure,
2. reduced CO₂ availability,
3. biochemical limitations,
4. leaf damage,
5. oxidative stress.
Drought→Stomatal Closure→CO2↓→Photosynthesis↓    CO 2    
A drought-resistant variety may maintain photosynthetic function better under stress.

19. Drought and Plant Temperature
When water is available, transpiration provides cooling:
Water Evaporation→Latent Heat Loss→Leaf Cooling    During severe drought:
Water Availability↓→Transpiration↓→Cooling↓→Tleaf↑       T     Therefore canopy or leaf temperature can sometimes be used as an indicator of drought response.

20. Canopy Temperature
A commonly used indicator is canopy temperature depression:
CTD=Tair−Tcanopy  -T      

A cooler canopy under certain drought conditions can indicate continued transpiration and water
availability.
But interpretation depends on:
- humidity,
- wind,
- radiation,
- crop stage,
- measurement time,
- genotype.

21. Drought During Germination
Water is required for seed imbibition.
Drought→Insufficient Water Uptake→Poor Germination     
Drought-resistant seed or variety may have better establishment under limited moisture, but seed
quality and soil conditions remain important.

22. Drought During Seedling Stage
Seedlings are vulnerable because their root systems are still developing.
Drought can cause:
- poor emergence,
- slow growth,
- leaf expansion reduction,
- root/shoot imbalance,
- seedling death.

23. Drought During Vegetative Growth
Drought can reduce:

- leaf expansion,
- stem growth,
- branching,
- tillering,
- canopy development,
- photosynthesis.
A drought-adapted variety may maintain greater biomass or recover better after rewatering.

24. Drought During Flowering
Flowering is often a sensitive stage.
Drought may cause:
- flower abortion,
- reduced pollen viability,
- reduced stigma function,
- poor fertilization,
- reduced fruit/grain set.
Therefore:
Drought at Flowering→Reproductive Failure→Yield Loss 
   

25. Drought During Grain Filling
Drought during grain filling can reduce:
- photosynthesis,
- assimilate supply,
- grain-filling duration,
- grain weight.
Drought→Assimilate Supply↓→Grain Weight↓       
Drought-adapted varieties may maintain better grain filling under water stress.

26. Drought and Yield Components
For cereals:
Yield≈Plants/Area×Grains/Plant×Grain Weight      
Drought can reduce one or more of these components.
A drought-resistant variety may maintain:
- plant survival,
- productive tillers,
- grain number,
- grain weight,
- reproductive success.

27. Drought Escape Through Early Maturity
An early-maturing variety may complete its critical reproductive stages before severe drought occurs.
Early Maturity→Drought Escape    
This is particularly useful where drought occurs predictably late in the season.
But early maturity can sometimes reduce yield potential if the crop has insufficient time to accumulate
biomass.

28. Drought and Root Architecture
Important root traits include:
Root trait
Potential significance
Greater depth
Access to deeper water
Greater root length
Greater soil exploration
Root branching
Increased soil exploration
Root density
Water acquisition
Root angle
Distribution through soil profile
Hydraulic conductance Water transport

Root trait
Root plasticity

Potential significance
Adjustment to soil-water distribution

29. Root Plasticity
Roots can change their growth according to soil conditions.
Changing Soil Moisture→Root Growth Adjustment    
This is called root plasticity.
It can help plants exploit available water.

30. Drought and Water Potential
Water moves according to gradients in water potential.
A simplified relationship is:
Soil→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere       
Under drought:
Soil Water Potential↓→Water Uptake Becomes More Difficult   Drought-adapted plants may maintain water uptake or tolerate lower water potentials better.

31. Drought and VPD
Drought is not only about soil moisture.
High atmospheric demand can also increase plant water stress.
VPD=es−ea 
High VPD means the atmosphere has a greater drying demand.
Thus:
Low Soil Moisture+High VPD→High Plant Water Stress  +  
 32. Drought × Heat Stress
Drought and heat often occur together.
Heat→Water Demand↑     
and:
Drought→Transpirational Cooling↓     
Together:
Heat + Drought→Very High Crop Stress    
Therefore a variety tolerant to drought alone may not necessarily perform equally well under combined
heat and drought.

33. Drought × Wind
Strong wind increases atmospheric water loss.
Wind↑→Boundary Layer Resistance↓→Water Vapor Loss↑      Therefore:
Drought + Strong Wind→Greater Water Stress Risk    

34. Drought × Soil Type
The same variety may behave differently in different soils.

Sandy soil
- low water-holding capacity,
- rapid drainage.

Clay soil
- potentially high water storage,
- but some water may be tightly held,
- drainage can be slow.

Loam
Often provides a balanced combination of water storage and drainage.
Therefore:
Drought Response=f(Genotype+Soil+Climate+Management)  =
f( + + + )  

35. Drought Resistance and Irrigation
A drought-resistant variety does not mean irrigation is unnecessary.
Instead:
Drought-Resistant Variety+Efficient Irrigation→Better Water Management  +     
Irrigation can reduce water stress, while drought-adapted genetics can provide additional protection
when water is limited.

36. Drought Resistance and Water-Use
Efficiency
A variety may produce more crop output per unit water:
WUE=YieldWater Used        
However, high WUE does not necessarily mean high yield.
A genotype can conserve water very strongly but produce less biomass.
Therefore:
Water Conservation≠Automatically Maximum Yield 
  

37. Drought Resistance and Yield Stability
One important breeding objective is maintaining yield across variable rainfall conditions.
For example:
Normal Rainfall→High Yield  Moderate Drought→Acceptable Yield  A variety showing less yield reduction under drought may be described as more drought-resilient in
that environment.

38. Drought Tolerance Index Concepts
Researchers use several indices to compare drought performance.
One commonly used conceptual measure is stress tolerance index (STI):
STI=Yp×YsYpˉ 2     \,2    
where:
- YpY p = yield under non-stress conditions,
- YsY s = yield under stress,
- Ypˉ  = mean yield under non-stress conditions.
Different indices emphasize different aspects of drought performance, so no single index is universally
sufficient.

39. Breeding for Drought Resistance
A simplified breeding pathway is:
Identify Drought Problem→Find Drought-Adapted Germplasm→Cross Parents→Select→Stress Scree
ning→Yield Testing→Multi-Environment Evaluation→Release          

40. Sources of Drought Resistance
Breeders may obtain useful traits from:
- local varieties,
- landraces,
- improved varieties,
- breeding lines,
- wild relatives,

- germplasm collections.
Thus:
Local Genetic Diversity→Drought Traits→Breeding→Improved Variety      

41. Marker-Assisted Selection
If a drought-related genetic region is identified:
DNA Marker→Identify Desired Genotype→Select Plant     
This can assist breeding for specific traits.

42. Genomic Selection
For complex drought-response traits:
Genome-Wide Markers→Prediction→Breeding Value→Selection      
Drought response is often genetically complex, making genomic approaches potentially useful.

43. Phenotyping for Drought Resistance
Breeders may measure:

Morphological traits
- root depth,
- root biomass,
- leaf area,
- plant height,
- tillering.

Physiological traits
- stomatal conductance,
- photosynthesis,
- leaf water potential,
- canopy temperature,
- transpiration.

Biochemical traits
- proline,
- soluble sugars,
- antioxidant activity.

Agronomic traits
- grain yield,
- fruit yield,
- seed yield,
- harvest index,
- yield stability.

44. Drought Screening
A simplified screening process:
Normal Water TreatmentvsDrought Treatment     
Then compare:
- survival,
- growth,
- flowering,
- reproductive success,
- yield,
- recovery.

A useful evaluation requires clearly defined stress conditions.

45. Controlled vs Field Drought Testing
Controlled conditions
Advantages:
- precise stress timing,
- controlled soil moisture,
- repeatability.
Limitations:
- may not represent field complexity.

Field testing
Advantages:
- realistic environmental interactions,
- natural soil and climate variability.
Limitations:
- difficult to control stress precisely.
Therefore:
Controlled Screening+Field Validation  +    
is often more informative than either alone.

46. Genotype × Environment Interaction
Drought performance depends strongly on environment.
P=G+E+G×E 
A variety may perform very well under:
- moderate drought,
but differently under:
- severe drought,

- heat + drought,
- sandy soil,
- deep soil.
Therefore:
Drought Resistance is Environment-Specific   
to an important degree.

47. Drought Resistance and Crop Stage
Drought sensitivity varies during the crop life cycle.
Drought Impact=f(Intensity+Duration+Timing)  = f( +
 + )  
Important stages may include:
- germination,
- seedling establishment,
- flowering,
- pollination,
- fruit/grain set,
- grain filling,
- maturity.

48. Drought-Resistant Variety vs DroughtEscape Variety
Feature
Drought-resistant/tolerant
Drought-escape
Main mechanism
Maintains function under stress
Completes life cycle before severe stress
Root response
Often important
May be less important
Physiological tolerance Important
Not necessarily required
Early maturity
Not essential
Often important
Stress exposure
Can withstand some exposure
Avoids part of exposure
Yield strategy
Maintain performance under stress Finish before severe stress

49. Drought-Resistant Variety vs DiseaseResistant Variety
Feature

Drought-resistant

Target
Main stress

Water deficit
Abiotic

Water acquisition/conservation/tolerance

Examples

Deep roots, stomatal regulation, osmotic
adjustment

factor

Yes
A variety can simultaneously possess:

Disease-resistant
Pathogen

Host defense/pathogen
restriction

responses

Yes

Drought Resistance+Disease Resistance  +  
 

50. Drought-Resistant Variety vs Improved
Variety
A drought-resistant variety is one type of improved variety when drought adaptation is an improvement
- bjective.
Drought-Resistant Variety⊆Possible Improved Varieties 
  
But:
Improved Variety≠Necessarily Drought-Resistant 
  

51. Drought-Resistant Variety vs Local Variety
A local variety may contain drought-adapted characteristics.
But:
Local≠Drought Resistant   

A local variety may instead be used as a genetic resource for developing drought-resistant improved
varieties.

52. Drought Resistance vs Seed Quality
A drought-resistant genotype does not automatically mean high-quality seed.
Remember:
Genetic Drought Resistance≠Seed Quality    
Seed still needs appropriate:
- viability,
- germination,
- vigor,
- purity,
- moisture,
- seed health.

53. Drought Resistance and Seed Treatment
Seed treatment may improve establishment under some conditions, but it does not replace genetic
drought resistance.
Genetics+Seed Quality+Seed Treatment where appropriate+Water Management  +   +   +    
together influence establishment.

54. Management Along With Drought-Resistant
Varieties
Genetics works together with agronomic management.
Useful practices may include:
- appropriate sowing time,

- drought-adapted variety selection,
- efficient irrigation,
- mulching,
- residue retention,
- conservation tillage,
- soil organic matter management,
- weed control,
- appropriate plant population,
- rainwater harvesting,
- moisture conservation,
- suitable nutrient management.
Conceptually:
Drought-Adapted Variety+Moisture Conservation+Efficient Water Use→Better Drought Management\
boxed    +   +  
  

55. Drought and Crop Selection
Crop selection should consider:
- rainfall pattern,
- drought probability,
- soil water-holding capacity,
- irrigation availability,
- crop duration,
- market,
- temperature,
- heat risk,
- soil depth.
A drought-resistant variety is most useful when matched with its target environment.

56. Drought and Climate Change
Climate change can increase the importance of:
- drought tolerance,
- heat tolerance,
- early maturity,
- yield stability,
- water-use efficiency.
An important challenge is combined stress:
Heat+Drought+High VPD→Compound Stress  +   +     
Therefore future breeding increasingly considers multiple stresses rather than drought alone.

57. Major Traits Used in Drought Breeding
Trait
Possible importance
Deep roots
Access deeper water
Root density
Soil exploration
Root plasticity
Adaptation to moisture distribution

Stomatal regulation

Leaf rolling
Reduce exposed area
Osmotic adjustment Maintain cell hydration

Reduce cellular injury
