Canopy temperature Indicator of plant water status
Stay-green
Maintain photosynthesis during stress
Reproductive stability Maintain grain/fruit set
Recovery ability
Resume growth after rewatering

58. Stay-Green Trait
Some varieties maintain green leaves longer during grain filling.
This is called stay-green.

Stay-Green→Photosynthetic Area Maintained Longer→Potentially Better Grain Filling     
However, stay-green can be:
- functional,
- cosmetic.
Functional stay-green involves continued physiological activity, not merely delayed yellowing.

59. Drought and Harvest Index
Harvest index is:
HI=Economic YieldBiological Yield×100        100  
Drought can alter biomass production and allocation to economic yield.
A drought-adapted variety may maintain a better partitioning of biomass toward the harvested product
under stress.

60. Complete Drought-Resistance Model
Drought→Soil Water Deficit→Root Sensing + ABA Signaling→Stomatal Regulation→Water Conserv
ation+Root Water Acquisition+Osmotic Adjustment+Antioxidant Defense→Physiological Protection
→Growth/Reproduction Maintained→Yield Stability   
& \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ &+  \\ &+  \\ &+  \\ & \\ & \\ &  

61. Critical Comparisons
Concept
Drought resistance
Drought tolerance
Drought avoidance

Meaning
Broad ability to maintain performance under drought
Maintain function despite dehydration/stress
Maintain favorable water status

Concept
Meaning
Drought escape
Complete life cycle before severe drought
Drought-resilient variety Maintains or recovers performance under variable drought conditions
Water-use efficiency
Output per unit water under a defined measurement
Improved variety
Variety developed for desirable traits
Local variety
Geographically/traditionally associated variety
Disease-resistant variety Genetically restricts disease development
Hybrid
F₁ from selected parental cross
OPV
Maintained through open pollination
Certified seed
Seed meeting applicable certification requirements

62. Common Misconceptions
"Drought-resistant means it doesn't need water."

❌ No.

All crops require water.
Drought Resistance≠No Water Requirement   

"Drought-resistant varieties can survive unlimited drought."

❌ No.

Resistance is relative and stress-dependent.

"Deep roots always make a variety drought-resistant."

❌ No.

Deep roots help only when usable water is available at depth.

"Early maturity is the same as drought tolerance."

❌ No.

Early maturity is primarily a drought-escape strategy.

"High WUE always means high yield."

❌ No.

Water conservation and maximum yield are not always the same objective.

"Drought resistance works independently of soil."

❌ No.

Soil depth, texture, water storage and root restrictions strongly affect drought response.

"Drought-resistant means heat-resistant."

❌ No.

Drought and heat are different stresses, although they frequently interact.
Drought Resistance≠Heat Resistance   

63. Exam-Ready Answer
A drought-resistant variety is a crop variety possessing inherited characteristics that
enable it to maintain survival, physiological function, growth, reproduction and/or
yield relatively better than susceptible varieties under defined water-deficit conditions.
Drought resistance involves several mechanisms, including drought escape, drought
avoidance and drought tolerance. Important traits may include deep and extensive
root systems, root plasticity, efficient stomatal regulation, improved water-use
efficiency, osmotic adjustment, maintenance of membrane stability, antioxidant
defense, leaf rolling or reduced leaf area, canopy temperature regulation, stay-green
characteristics and improved reproductive stability under water stress. Drought
resistance is strongly influenced by genotype, soil, climate, drought intensity, duration
and crop developmental stage. Drought during flowering and grain or fruit formation
can be particularly damaging because it can reduce pollination, fertilization,
reproductive success and filling. Drought-resistant varieties are developed through
plant breeding using sources such as landraces, local varieties, improved varieties,
breeding lines and wild relatives, followed by selection, drought screening, yield
evaluation and multi-environment testing. Molecular approaches such as markerassisted selection and genomic selection can also assist breeding for complex droughtresponse traits. Drought-resistant varieties should not be confused with drought
escape, disease resistance, seed quality, hybrid status or certified seed. They also do
not eliminate the need for irrigation and moisture-conservation practices. Their
effectiveness depends on the particular drought environment and the interaction
among genotype, soil, climate and management.

64. Final Master Memory
Drought
Low Water Availability→Plant Water Stress    

Drought adaptation
Root Water Acquisition+Water Conservation+Osmotic Adjustment+Cell Protection+Reproductive Stab
ility  +   +  
+   +    

Four major strategies
Escape+Avoidance+Tolerance+Recovery  +   +  
+    

Breeding
Drought Genetic Resource→Crossing→Selection→Stress Screening→Field Testing→DroughtResistant Variety        

Performance
Drought Response=f(Genotype,Soil,Water,Climate,Management,Crop Stage)  = f( , , , , , )  

Most important distinction
DROUGHT RESISTANCE = MAINTAINING PERFORMANCE UNDER WATER DEFICIT  
DROUGHT AVOIDANCE = MAINTAINING PLANT WATER STATUS  
DROUGHT TOLERANCE = FUNCTIONING DESPITE DEHYDRATION  
DROUGHT ESCAPE = FINISHING THE LIFE CYCLE BEFORE SEVERE DROUGHT  
One-line memory: A drought-resistant variety uses inherited traits such as effective
root systems, water-conserving stomatal regulation, osmotic adjustment, cellular
protection, appropriate maturity and reproductive stability to maintain growth and
yield better under water-deficit conditions.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
High-Yielding Varieties (HYVs)
## 1. Introduction
A high-yielding variety (HYV) is a crop variety that has been developed or selected to produce
relatively high economic yield under suitable environmental conditions and appropriate crop
management, compared with relevant existing varieties or standards.
The important point is that “high-yielding” is a comparative and management-dependent concept.
High-Yielding Variety→High Yield Potential+Suitable Environment+Good Management   +   +
   
A high-yielding variety does not automatically produce high yield everywhere.

## 2. Definition
A high-yielding variety is a genetically improved crop variety selected or bred for high
yield potential and capable of producing relatively high economic yield when grown
under suitable environmental conditions and appropriate management.
The term HYV is therefore mainly associated with yield potential/performance, rather than a
particular seed class or reproductive system.

3. What Does "High Yield" Mean?
Yield is the quantity of economically useful product obtained per unit area.
Examples:
- wheat → grain,
- rice → grain,
- maize → grain,
- potato → tubers,
- cotton → lint,

- groundnut → pods/seeds,
- sugarcane → cane/stalk biomass,
- tomato → marketable fruit.
A simplified yield relationship is:
Yield=Yield Components×Environmental/Management Effects    
For cereals:
Yield≈Plants/area×Grains/plant×Average grain weight 

4. Yield Potential vs Actual Yield
This is one of the most important concepts.

Yield potential
The maximum yield a genotype can approach under defined favorable conditions without major
limitations from:
- water,
- nutrients,
- pests,
- diseases,
- weeds,
- temperature and other stresses.

Actual yield
The yield obtained under real farm conditions.
Therefore:
Actual Yield≤Yield Potential 
in the usual conceptual sense.
A high-yielding variety may have high genetic yield potential, but actual yield can be much lower
because of drought, nutrient deficiency, disease, poor management, etc.

5. Genetic Basis of High Yield
Yield is usually a complex quantitative trait.
It is controlled by many genes and strongly affected by the environment.
P=G+E+G×E 
where:
- PP = observed phenotype/performance,
- GG = genetic effect,
- EE = environmental effect,
- G×EG E = genotype × environment interaction.
Therefore, a high-yielding variety is not simply a variety containing one "high-yield gene."

6. Major Characteristics of High-Yielding
Varieties
Important characteristics can include:
- high yield potential,
- efficient biomass production,
- efficient partitioning of biomass,
- good harvest index,
- appropriate plant architecture,
- strong stems,
- favorable maturity duration,
- good response to nutrients,
- efficient water use,
- suitable tillering/branching,
- favorable grain/fruit/seed number,
- suitable grain/fruit/seed size,
- resistance to important diseases,
- resistance/tolerance to important pests,

- adaptation to target environments.
Not every HYV possesses all of these traits.

7. Yield Components
Different crops achieve high yield through different yield components.

Cereals
Yield≈Plants/area×Productive tillers/plant×Grains/tiller×Grain weightYield  Plants/area  Productive\ tillers/plant  Grains/tiller  Grain\ weight

Pulses
Yield≈Plants/area×Pods/plant×Seeds/pod×Seed weightYield  Plants/area  Pods/plant
 Seeds/pod  Seed\ weight

Fruit crops
Yield≈Plants/area×Fruits/plant×Average fruit weightYield  Plants/area  Fruits/plant  Average\ fruit\ weight
Thus breeding can improve different components to increase final yield.

8. Harvest Index
An important concept in high-yielding crops is harvest index (HI).
HI=Economic YieldBiological Yield×100   Biological\ Yield 
 100  
where:
- economic yield = harvested useful product,
- biological yield = total plant biomass.
For example, a cereal variety may produce more grain because it partitions a greater proportion of
biomass into grain.
High Yield⇏Maximum Total Biomass     
Efficient biomass partitioning is important.

9. Photosynthesis and High Yield
Photosynthesis provides the carbon skeletons and energy-containing products needed for biomass
production.
6CO2+6H2O→lightC6H12O6+6O26CO 2+6H 2O   C 6H  12 O 6+6O 2
A high-yielding variety may have characteristics that improve:
- canopy development,
- light interception,
- photosynthetic duration,
- photosynthetic efficiency,
- source activity,
- assimilate movement to sinks.

10. Source–Sink Relationship
This is fundamental to yield formation.

Source
Organs that produce or release assimilates.
Examples:
- mature leaves.

Sink
Organs that use or store assimilates.
Examples:
- grains,
- seeds,
- fruits,
- tubers,
- roots.
Source→Phloem→Sink 
High yield requires an effective relationship between:

Source Capacity+Sink Capacity+Transport 

11. Canopy Architecture
High-yielding varieties may possess canopy structures that efficiently capture sunlight.
Important characteristics include:
- leaf angle,
- leaf area,
- leaf orientation,
- tiller/branch arrangement,
- canopy height,
- canopy duration.
The goal is efficient light interception without excessive shading of lower leaves.

12. Leaf Area Index
Leaf area index is:
LAI=Total one-sided leaf areaGround area  
     
Too little leaf area:
Light interception↓  Too much dense foliage:
Self-shading↑  Therefore optimum canopy development is more important than simply maximizing leaf area.

13. Lodging Resistance
Lodging occurs when crop plants bend or fall over.
It can reduce:
- photosynthesis,

- harvesting efficiency,
- grain filling,
- yield quality.
High-yielding cereal varieties often benefit from:
- shorter/appropriate plant height,
- stronger stems,
- suitable internode structure,
- stronger root anchorage.
This was especially important in the development of many modern cereal varieties.

14. Semi-Dwarf Varieties
Semi-dwarf architecture became highly important in modern cereal breeding.
Conceptually:
Shorter Stem+Strong Stem→Lodging Risk↓ 
This allows plants to allocate a greater proportion of biomass toward the harvested product and can
improve response to favorable nutrient and water conditions.

15. High Response to Inputs
Many high-yielding varieties perform particularly well when adequate resources are available.
Important resources include:
- water,
- nitrogen,
- phosphorus,
- potassium,
- other nutrients,
- appropriate plant population,
- weed control,

- disease and pest management.
Conceptually:
HYV+Adequate Inputs+Good Management→High Yield Potential 
But this does not mean every HYV requires extremely high inputs.

16. High-Yielding Varieties and the Green
Revolution
The term HYV is strongly associated with the Green Revolution, especially in crops such as:
- wheat,
- rice.
Modern high-yielding cereal varieties were combined with:
- irrigation,
- mineral fertilizers,
- crop protection,
- improved agronomic practices,
- mechanization,
- improved seed systems.
The important concept is:
Improved Genetics+Improved Agronomy+Inputs→Large Yield Gains  +   +     
It would be inaccurate to attribute the entire yield increase to genetics alone.

17. High-Yielding Wheat
Modern wheat breeding has focused on combinations of:
- high grain yield,
- semi-dwarf architecture,
- lodging resistance,

- disease resistance,
- suitable maturity,
- grain quality,
- adaptation.
The Green Revolution demonstrated the strong interaction between improved wheat genetics and
favorable management.

18. High-Yielding Rice
High-yielding rice varieties have been developed with objectives such as:
- high grain yield,
- improved plant architecture,
- appropriate duration,
- disease resistance,
- lodging resistance,
- adaptation to irrigated or rainfed conditions.
Different rice varieties are adapted to different environments.

19. High-Yielding Maize
Maize yield depends on traits including:
- plant population,
- ear number,
- kernels per ear,
- kernel weight,
- photosynthetic capacity,
- stress tolerance,
- lodging resistance.
Hybrids are particularly important in maize because of heterosis.

20. High-Yielding Varieties vs Hybrids
These are not the same concept.

High-yielding variety
Describes yield performance/potential.

Hybrid
Describes genetic origin:
P1×P2→F1P 1 P 2 F 1
A hybrid can be high-yielding.
An open-pollinated variety can also be high-yielding.
Therefore:
HYV≠Hybrid 
but:
High-yielding Hybrid yielding\ Hybrid  
is possible.

21. High-Yielding Variety vs Improved Variety
An improved variety is broader.
It may be improved for:
- yield,
- drought resistance,
- disease resistance,
- quality,
- early maturity,
- heat tolerance,
- nutrient-use efficiency,
- lodging resistance.
Therefore:
HYV⊆Possible Improved Varieties 

But:
Improved Variety≠Necessarily HYV 

22. High-Yielding Variety vs Disease-Resistant
Variety
These describe different traits.
Type
Primary characteristic
High-yielding
High yield potential/performance
Disease-resistant Reduced disease development
Drought-resistant Better performance under water deficit
Heat-tolerant
Better performance under high temperature
Early-maturing Shorter/earlier developmental duration
One variety can combine several characteristics.
For example:
High Yield+Disease Resistance+Drought Adaptation  +   +    

23. High-Yielding Variety vs Local Variety
A local variety is primarily characterized by its geographical/traditional association.
An HYV is characterized primarily by yield performance/potential.
Therefore:
Local≠High-Yielding Yielding  
However, a local variety may be high-yielding under its adapted environment.

24. High-Yielding Variety vs OPV
OPV describes a reproductive/maintenance system.
HYV describes yield potential/performance.
Thus:
High-Yielding OPV   

is possible.
Likewise:
High-Yielding Hybrid   
is possible.

25. High-Yielding Variety vs Certified Seed
These describe completely different dimensions.

HYV
Genetic/performance characteristic.

Certified seed
Seed certification class/status.
Therefore:
HYV≠Certified Seed 
A high-yielding variety can be multiplied and distributed as certified seed when it meets applicable
certification requirements.

26. High-Yielding Variety vs Foundation Seed
Foundation seed is a seed multiplication class.
High-yielding variety describes genetic performance/potential.
Therefore:
HYV≠Foundation Seed 
Foundation seed may be used to produce seed of a high-yielding variety.

27. Environmental Interaction
High-yielding varieties do not perform identically everywhere.
Their performance depends on:
- temperature,

- rainfall,
- soil,
- irrigation,
- nutrients,
- photoperiod,
- pests,
- diseases,
- management.
Yield=f(G,E,G×E,M) 
where MM represents management.

28. Yield Gap
The difference between attainable/high potential yield and actual farm yield is often called the yield
gap, although several formal definitions exist.
Conceptually:
Yield Gap=Potential/Attainable Yield−Actual Yield 
Yield gaps can arise from:
- nutrient deficiency,
- drought,
- pests,
- diseases,
- weeds,
- poor plant population,
- unsuitable sowing time,
- poor soil,
- inadequate irrigation,
- management limitations.

29. High Yield Does Not Mean Universal
Adaptation
A variety selected for one environment may not be the highest-performing variety in another.
For example:
G1×E1≠G1×E2G 1 E 1  G 1 E 2
Therefore variety recommendations should consider the target production environment.

30. Stability vs Maximum Yield
Breeding may target either:

High yield potential
Very high yield under favorable conditions.

Yield stability
Smaller yield reduction across variable environments.
Ideally:
High Yield+Yield Stability 
but these objectives can sometimes involve trade-offs.

31. High-Yielding Varieties Under Stress
A variety can have high yield potential under favorable conditions but perform poorly during:
- drought,
- heat,
- flooding,
- salinity,
- disease pressure.
Thus:
High Yield Potential≠Stress Resistance 
A variety bred specifically for both can combine the traits.

32. Nutrient Response
High-yielding varieties can have high nutrient requirements because producing more biomass and
economic yield requires more nutrients.
For example:
Higher Yield→Greater Nutrient Removal   If nutrients are not replenished:
Nutrient Removal>Nutrient Replacement  >  
can contribute to soil fertility decline.
Therefore:
HYV+Balanced Nutrient Management 
is important.

33. Nitrogen and High Yield
Nitrogen is particularly important for many high-yielding crops.
Nitrogen supports:
- chlorophyll,
- proteins,
- enzymes,
- leaf development,
- photosynthetic capacity.
But excessive nitrogen can cause:
- lodging,
- excessive vegetative growth,
- disease risk in some situations,
- nutrient imbalance,
- environmental losses.

Therefore:
More Nitrogen≠Always More Yield   

34. Water Requirement
A high-yielding variety may need adequate water to realize its potential.
Water supports:
- photosynthesis,
- nutrient transport,
- cell expansion,
- reproductive development,
- grain/fruit filling.
But:
High Yield Potential≠High Water Requirement in Every Variety 
  
Breeding increasingly targets high yield with improved water-use efficiency and stress adaptation.

35. High-Yielding Variety and Crop Duration
Yield potential interacts with maturity duration.

Long-duration varieties
May have more time for:
- biomass accumulation,
- grain filling,
- resource capture.
But they may face greater exposure to:
- drought,
- heat,
- pests,
- terminal stress.

Short-duration varieties
Can escape late-season stress.
But may have less time to accumulate biomass.
Therefore:
Yield Potential×Duration×Environment     
must be considered together.

36. High-Yielding Variety and Plant Population
Plant population strongly affects yield.
Too low:
Resource capture↓  Too high:
Competition↑  Therefore an appropriate plant population is required.
Optimum Population→Efficient Resource Use→High Yield 
   

37. High-Yielding Variety and Disease
Resistance
Disease can reduce the yield potential of a variety.
Therefore breeders may combine:
High Yield+Disease Resistance  +    
This is particularly important where a disease is a major yield-limiting factor.

38. High-Yielding Variety and Drought
Resistance
Similarly:
High Yield+Drought Resistance  +    
can provide higher yield under water-limited environments than simply selecting for high yield under
ideal conditions.
However, there can be trade-offs between maximum potential yield and stress adaptation.

39. High-Yielding Variety and Heat Resistance
Climate and temperature influence yield.
A high-yielding genotype may lose substantial yield under extreme heat.
Breeding may therefore combine:
High Yield+Heat Tolerance  +    

40. Breeding Process for High Yield
A simplified breeding pathway:
Breeding Objective   
↓
Select Parents   
↓
P1×P2→F1P 1 P 2 F 1
↓
Generate Genetic Variation   
↓
F2→F3→F4→⋯ 
↓
Select High-Performing Plants   
↓

Yield Trials   
↓
Multi-Environment Testing   
↓
Release/Notification under Applicable System   
↓
Seed Multiplication   

41. Sources Used in Breeding
Breeders may use:
- local varieties,
- landraces,
- existing improved varieties,
- breeding lines,
- hybrids,
- wild relatives,
- germplasm collections.
A high-yielding variety may therefore contain useful genes originating from traditional or wild genetic
resources.

42. Modern Breeding Technologies
High-yield breeding can use:

Conventional selection
Selecting superior plants.

Hybridization
Combining desirable parents.

Backcrossing
Introducing a specific trait into an adapted background.

Marker-assisted selection
Using DNA markers linked to desired traits.

Genomic selection
Predicting breeding value from genome-wide markers.

Doubled haploids
Rapid production of genetically fixed lines in suitable crops.

Genomic tools
Identifying genomic regions associated with yield and its components.

Gene editing
Potentially modifying specific genes, subject to biological, regulatory and crop-specific considerations.

43. Yield Testing
A candidate variety must be compared with relevant standards.
Important measurements include:
- grain yield,
- seed yield,
- fruit yield,
- biomass,
- harvest index,
- maturity,
- lodging,
- disease,
- quality,
- stress response.
Yield should be tested across appropriate environments.

44. Multi-Environment Testing
A variety should not be judged from one location or one season alone.
Testing may involve:
- different soil types,
- rainfall environments,
- irrigation conditions,
- temperatures,
- years,
- management levels.
Why?
Because:
G×E 
can be substantial.

45. High-Yielding Variety and Seed Quality
A genetically high-yielding variety still needs high-quality seed.
High-Yielding Genetics+High-Quality Seed→Good Crop Establishment Yielding\
Genetics + High Quality\ Seed  Good\ Crop\ Establishment  
Seed quality includes:
- genetic purity,
- physical purity,
- viability,
- germination,
- vigor,
- seed health.

46. High-Yielding Variety and Seed Rate
The appropriate seed rate depends on:

- crop,
- variety,
- seed size,
- germination,
- expected field emergence,
- planting method,
- target plant population.
A simplified relationship is:
Required Seeds≈Desired PlantsExpected Establishment Fraction   Expected\ Establishment\ Fraction   
High yield requires an appropriate—not necessarily maximum—plant population.

47. Economic Consideration
High yield alone does not guarantee maximum profit.
A simplified relationship:
Net Return=Gross Revenue−Total Cost 
where:
Gross Revenue=Yield×Market PriceGross\ Revenue= Yield Market\ Price
A variety producing slightly higher yield but requiring much greater input costs may have a different
economic outcome than a lower-input variety.

48. High-Yielding Variety and Sustainable
Agriculture
Modern breeding increasingly seeks combinations of:
- high yield,
- nutrient-use efficiency,
- water-use efficiency,
- disease resistance,

- drought adaptation,
- heat tolerance,
- lodging resistance,
- quality,
- reduced environmental footprint.
The objective is not simply:
Maximum Yield 
but increasingly:
High Yield+Resource Efficiency+Stress Resilience+Quality  +
  +   +    

49. Common Misconceptions
"HYV means hybrid."

❌ No.

HYV≠Hybrid 

"HYV always produces high yield."

❌ No.

Yield depends on genotype × environment × management.

"HYV requires unlimited fertilizer."

❌ No.

High-yielding crops require appropriate balanced nutrition, not unlimited fertilizer.

"HYV is automatically drought-resistant."

❌ No.

High yield and drought resistance are separate traits.

"HYV is automatically disease-resistant."

❌ No.

Disease resistance must be specifically bred or selected.

"HYV is always better than a local variety."

❌ This cannot be assumed universally.
Performance depends on:
- environment,
- stress,
- management,
- market,
- quality,
- farmer objectives.

"High yield means maximum biomass."

❌ No.

High yield can result from efficient partitioning of biomass into the harvested product.

50. Critical Comparison With Your Previous
Seed Topics

Landrace

OPV

Drought-resistant variety Better performance under water deficit
High-yielding variety
High yield potential/performance
Hybrid
Specific F₁ parental cross
Foundation seed
Seed multiplication class
Certified seed

Term
Seed quality

What it primarily describes
Genetic + physical + physiological + health quality

These categories can overlap.
For example:
High-Yielding+Drought-Resistant+Disease-Resistant+Improved  +
  +   +    
can all describe the same variety from different perspectives.
Similarly:
High-Yielding Hybrid   
and:
High-Yielding OPV   
are both possible.

51. Complete High-Yielding Variety Model
Genetic Improvement→High Yield Potential→Efficient Canopy→High Photosynthetic Capacity→Effe
ctive Source-Sink Relationship→Efficient Biomass Partitioning→High Economic Yield    & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
&  
But actual performance is:
Yield=f(G,E,G×E,Water,Nutrients,Pests,Diseases,Management) 

52. Exam-Ready Definition
A high-yielding variety (HYV) is a genetically improved crop variety selected or bred
for relatively high yield potential and capable of producing high economic yield under
suitable environmental conditions and appropriate crop management. High yield is
generally a complex quantitative trait controlled by many genes and strongly
influenced by genotype × environment interaction. Important characteristics may
include efficient canopy development, high photosynthetic productivity, favorable
yield components, efficient biomass partitioning, suitable harvest index, lodging
resistance, appropriate maturity and good response to water and nutrients. Highyielding varieties became especially important during the Green Revolution, where

improved genetics were combined with irrigation, fertilizers, crop protection and
improved agronomic practices. HYV should not be confused with hybrid seed,
improved variety, open-pollinated variety, certified seed or foundation seed. A variety
may simultaneously be high-yielding, disease-resistant, drought-resistant, hybrid or
- pen-pollinated, depending on the characteristics being considered. Actual yield
depends on genotype, environment and management, so a high-yielding variety does
not necessarily produce high yield under every condition.

53. Final Master Memory
Definition
HYV = Variety with High Yield Potential/Performance   

Yield
Yield≈Yield Components×Environmental and Management Effects    

Genetic basis
P=G+E+G×E 

Important mechanisms
Efficient Canopy+Photosynthesis+Source-Sink Balance+Biomass Partitioning+Yield Components+Lo
dging Resistance  +   +   +   +   +    
↓
High Economic Yield   

High yield does NOT automatically mean:
HYV≠Hybrid  HYV≠DiseaseResistant  HYV≠DroughtResistant 
HYV≠CertifiedSeed  HYV≠FoundationSeed 

One-line memory
A high-yielding variety is a genetically improved crop variety bred or selected for high
yield potential, whose actual yield depends on the interaction of its genetics with
environment, water, nutrients, pests, diseases and crop management.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Seed Purity
## 1. Introduction
Seed purity refers to the extent to which a seed lot contains the desired seed of the stated crop and
variety, free from unwanted materials such as:
- inert matter,
- seeds of other crops,
- weed seeds,
- other varieties,
- diseased or damaged material, depending on the testing framework.
Seed purity is a major component of seed quality.
Seed Quality=Genetic Quality+Physical Quality+Physiological Quality+Seed Health  =   +   +   +
   
Seed purity mainly concerns physical purity and genetic/varietal purity, which must be distinguished
from germination and seed vigor.

## 2. Definition
Seed purity is the degree to which a seed lot consists of the intended crop seed and
conforms to the required physical and varietal identity standards, with minimal
contamination by other crop seeds, weed seeds, inert matter, or other unwanted
components.
In seed testing, the exact meaning of "purity" depends on the applicable testing and certification
standard.

3. Why Is Seed Purity Important?
Pure seed helps ensure that the farmer plants the intended crop rather than a mixture of:

- unwanted crop species,
- weeds,
- other varieties,
- foreign material.
Poor purity can lead to:
Poor Seed Purity→Unwanted Plants→Competition→Management Problems→Potential Yield/Quality
Loss       

4. Seed Purity Is Not the Same as Germination
This is one of the most important distinctions.

Seed purity
What is actually present in the seed lot?

Germination
How many seeds produce normal seedlings under a defined test?
Therefore:
Purity≠Germination   
A seed lot can have:
- high purity + poor germination,
- high purity + high germination,
- poor purity + high germination,
- poor purity + poor germination.

5. Major Components of Seed Purity
Seed-lot purity can be considered through several dimensions.

A. Physical purity
Concerns the physical composition of the seed lot.

B. Genetic/varietal purity
Concerns whether the seed belongs to the stated variety/genotype.

C. Species purity
Concerns whether seeds belong to the intended crop species rather than other species.
These dimensions should not be treated as identical.

6. Physical Purity
Physical purity determines how much of the seed sample consists of pure seed of the intended crop
versus unwanted physical components.
A simplified composition is:
Seed Lot=Pure Seed+Other Crop Seeds+Weed Seeds+Inert Matter  =   +   +   +    
The exact categories and definitions depend on the seed-testing system.

7. Pure Seed
Pure seed generally refers to seeds belonging to the intended crop/species according to the applicable
seed-testing rules.
It may include seeds with certain minor physical defects if they still meet the relevant definition of pure
seed.
Therefore:
Pure Seed≠Perfect-Looking Seed   
It is a technical seed-testing category.

8. Other Crop Seeds
These are seeds belonging to other cultivated crop species mixed with the intended seed.
For example, a wheat seed lot contaminated with seeds of another crop would contain other-crop seeds.
These are undesirable because they can:

- reduce crop uniformity,
- compete for resources,
- complicate harvesting,
- contaminate produce.

9. Weed Seeds
Weed seeds are unwanted seeds belonging to weed species.
Examples can include seeds of:
- grassy weeds,
- broadleaf weeds,
- sedges.
Weed seeds are particularly undesirable because:
Weed Seeds→Weed Population→Crop Competition     
Some weed species are especially difficult to control or can contaminate harvested produce.

10. Inert Matter
Inert matter generally consists of materials that are not considered seed of the intended crop or
relevant other-seed categories under the applicable testing rules.
Examples can include:
- soil particles,
- stones,
- chaff,
- pieces of plant material,
- broken non-viable structures,
- other foreign material.
Thus:
Inert Matter→No Desired Crop Establishment    

11. Physical Purity Percentage
A common calculation is:
Physical Purity(%)=Weight of Pure SeedTotal Sample Weight×100        100  

Example
Suppose a 1000 g sample contains:
- 960 g pure seed,
- 15 g other crop seed,
- 10 g weed seed,
- 15 g inert matter.
Then:
Physical Purity=9601000×100Physical\ Purity =   1000  100 =96% 

12. Components Must Add Up
For a properly categorized physical purity analysis:
Pure Seed+Other Crop Seed+Weed Seed+Inert Matter=Total Sample 
Therefore:
Percentage Components≈100%  100\%  
subject to rounding and the definitions used in the testing system.

13. Example of a Seed Purity Analysis
Component Weight Percentage
Pure seed
960 g
96%
Other crop seeds
15 g
1.5%
Weed seeds
10 g
1%
Inert matter
15 g
1.5%
Total
1000 g
100%

The seed lot therefore has 96% physical purity in this illustrative example.

14. Genetic or Varietal Purity
Physical purity alone is not enough.
A seed lot may contain seeds that physically look like the intended crop but genetically belong to:
- another variety,
- an off-type,
- another genotype.
Genetic purity refers to conformity of the seed lot to the genetic identity of the stated variety.
Conceptually:
Genetic Purity=Correct Variety+Minimal Genetic Contamination  =
  +    

15. Physical Purity vs Genetic Purity
Feature
Physical purity
Main question What is physically in the lot?
Weed seeds, inert matter, other crop
Detects
seeds
Main
Physical separation/inspection
approach
Example
Wheat mixed with weed seeds
Main concern Composition

Genetic/varietal purity
Is it the correct variety genetically?
Off-types, varietal mixtures
Field inspection, grow-out, molecular/other
methods
Variety A mixed with Variety B
Identity

16. How Genetic Purity Is Maintained
Important measures include:

1. Correct source seed
Use an authenticated seed source.

2. Field isolation
Maintain appropriate separation from other varieties where cross-pollination is possible.

## 3. Rouging
Remove plants that differ from the desired variety.

4. Controlled multiplication
Maintain proper seed production procedures.

## 5. Cleaning
Prevent mechanical mixing during harvest and processing.

6. Proper storage
Keep different seed lots separately identified.

## 17. Rouging
Rouging is the removal of plants that are:
- genetically off-type,
- diseased where relevant,
- otherwise undesirable,
from a seed-production field.
Conceptually:
Off-Type Plant→Identification→Removal     
This helps maintain genetic/varietal purity.

18. Field Isolation
Cross-pollinating crops can receive pollen from nearby fields.
Therefore:
Isolation→Unwanted Cross-Pollination↓→Genetic Purity↑       
Isolation requirements depend on:
- crop,
- reproductive biology,

- seed class,
- applicable regulations.

19. Self-Pollinated Crops
Self-pollinated crops generally have lower risk of pollen-mediated contamination than strongly crosspollinated crops.
However, genetic purity can still decline through:
- mechanical mixing,
- residual off-types,
- seed handling errors,
- occasional outcrossing.
Therefore self-pollinated does not mean:
   

20. Cross-Pollinated Crops
Cross-pollinated crops have greater risk of genetic contamination through pollen.
Therefore they may require stronger measures such as:
- isolation,
- controlled pollination,
- male sterility systems,
- removal of off-types.

21. Hybrid Seed Purity
Hybrid seed requires maintaining the identity of:
- parental lines,
- parental populations,
- hybrid combination.

For a hybrid:
P1×P2→F1 
The commercial seed must represent the intended F₁ combination.
Genetic purity is therefore particularly important.

22. Seed Purity and Seed Cleaning
After harvesting, seed lots may contain:
- chaff,
- stones,
- soil,
- broken seeds,
- weed seeds,
- other crop seeds.
Cleaning and grading can remove many physical contaminants.
Typical operations include:
Harvest→Pre−cleaning→Cleaning→Grading→Treatment→Packaging 

23. Seed Processing
Seed processing aims to improve the physical quality and uniformity of a seed lot.
Operations may include:
- air-screen cleaning,
- sieving,
- aspiration,
- gravity separation,
- specific-gravity separation,
- length separation,
- color sorting,

- size grading.
The appropriate method depends on crop and contaminant.

24. Seed Purity and Seed Grading
Seed grading separates seeds according to characteristics such as:
- size,
- weight,
- density,
- shape.
Grading can improve uniformity but:
Grading≠Complete Genetic Purification   
Seeds of different varieties may have similar physical characteristics.

25. Seed Purity and Seed Health
These are different.

Seed purity
What is in the seed lot?

Seed health
Are seeds affected or contaminated by:
- pathogens,
- insects,
- other biological agents?
Therefore:
Purity≠Seed Health   

26. Seed Purity and Viability
Viability
Whether the seed is alive and capable of germination under suitable conditions.

Purity
Whether the seed lot contains the intended seed and is free from specified contaminants.
Thus:
Pure Seed≠Necessarily Viable Seed   

27. Seed Purity and Seed Vigor
Vigor
The ability of seeds to:
- germinate rapidly,
- emerge uniformly,
- establish seedlings,
- perform under a range of conditions.
Therefore:
Purity≠Vigor   

28. Complete Seed Quality Model
A useful way to remember all of this is:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  =   +
  +   +    
Where:

Genetic
- varietal identity,
- genetic purity.

Physical
- pure seed,
- other crop seeds,
- weed seeds,
- inert matter.

Physiological
- viability,
- germination,
- vigor,
- moisture.

Health
- seed-borne pathogens,
- insects and other biological problems.

29. Seed Purity and Germination Example
Consider two seed lots:

Lot A
- physical purity = 99%
- germination = 70%

Lot B
- physical purity = 90%
- germination = 95%
They have different strengths.
Lot A:
High Purity + Lower Germination 
Lot B:
Lower Purity + Higher Germination 
Therefore, one seed-quality measurement cannot describe the entire seed lot.

30. Pure Seed vs Pure Variety
These terms should not be confused.

Pure seed
A physical seed-testing category.

Pure variety/genetic purity
Conformity of plants/seeds to the desired genetic identity.
A seed lot can have:
High Physical Purity 
but:
Poor Genetic Purity 
if another variety is physically mixed with the intended variety and the seeds cannot be distinguished
by routine physical purity analysis.

31. Seed Purity and Certified Seed
Certified seed is produced under an applicable certification system.
Certification may include requirements related to:
- genetic identity,
- genetic purity,
- physical purity,
- germination,
- seed health,
- labeling.
Therefore:
Certified Seed≠Purity Alone    
Certification is a broader regulatory status.

32. Seed Purity and Foundation Seed
Foundation seed is a seed multiplication class designed to maintain high genetic identity/purity under
applicable standards.
Thus:
Foundation Seed→High Genetic Purity Maintenance→Subsequent Seed Production     
But foundation seed is not simply a synonym for "pure seed."

33. Seed Purity and OPV
An open-pollinated variety can maintain its characteristics through open pollination, but unwanted
pollen can reduce genetic purity.
Therefore:
OPV≠Automatically Genetically Pure 
Appropriate isolation and selection may be required.

34. Seed Purity and Local Varieties
Local varieties may contain considerable genetic diversity.
This is not automatically a defect.
For a landrace or traditional population:
Genetic Diversity   
can be part of its biological and adaptive characteristics.
Therefore, "purity" must be interpreted relative to the defined genetic identity of the seed lot or
variety.

35. Seed Purity and Hybrid Seed
Hybrid seed requires the intended parental cross:
P1×P2→F1P 1 P 2 F 1

If contamination occurs through:
- wrong parent,
- unwanted pollen,
- off-types,
- mechanical mixing,
the genetic purity of the hybrid seed lot may decline.

36. Sources of Seed Contamination
Seed purity can be reduced at many stages.

Field
- weed infestation,
- other varieties,
- other crops,
- volunteer plants,
- unwanted pollen.

Harvest
- mixed harvesting,
- poor cleaning of machinery.

Processing
- inadequate cleaning,
- mixing of lots.

Storage
- incorrect labeling,
- mixing bags/lots,
- poor warehouse organization.

Transportation
- accidental mixing.

Thus:
Seed Purity=Field Management+Harvest Hygiene+Processing+Storage+Traceability  =   +   +   +   +
   

37. Mechanical Mixing
One major source of contamination is mechanical mixing.
For example:
Lot A→Harvester→Residual Seed→Lot B     If equipment is not properly cleaned, seeds from Lot A can contaminate Lot B.

38. Seed Purity During Storage
Different seed lots should be:
- separately identified,
- properly labeled,
- physically organized,
- protected from accidental mixing.
Traceability is important.
A useful chain is:
Field→Harvest→Processing→Storage→Packaging→Farmer 
The identity of the seed lot should remain traceable through the chain.

39. Seed Purity Testing
A seed-testing laboratory may examine the physical composition of a representative sample.
Simplified procedure:

Seed Lot→Representative Sample→Separation→Identification→Weighing→Purity Calculation   Seed\ Lot  Representative\ Sample  Separation  Identification
 Weighing  Purity\ Calculation  

40. Why Sampling Matters
A laboratory can only test the sample it receives.
If the sample does not represent the seed lot:
Poor Sampling→Misleading Purity Result    
Therefore:
Representative Sampling→Reliable Seed Testing    
This is the same principle encountered in soil testing.

41. Seed Purity Testing vs Germination Testing
Test
Main question
Purity test
What components are present?
Germination test How many produce normal seedlings?
Viability test
Are seeds alive?
Vigor test
How strongly/rapidly do they perform?
Moisture test
How much water is present?
Health test
Are pathogens/biological contaminants present?
Genetic purity test Is the variety genetically correct?

42. Genetic Purity Testing Methods
Depending on crop and purpose, genetic identity may be assessed using:

Morphological methods
Observe plant characteristics.

Grow-out tests
Grow plants and compare their characteristics.

Biochemical methods
Use protein or enzyme profiles.

Molecular methods
Use DNA-based markers.
Examples include:
- PCR-based approaches,
- SSR markers,
- SNP markers,
- other DNA fingerprinting approaches.
Conceptually:
DNA Profile→Genetic Identity Assessment   

43. Genetic Purity Percentage
A simplified conceptual calculation may be:
Genetic Purity(%)=Number of True-to-Type PlantsTotal Plants Evaluated×100        100  
However, official genetic-purity calculations and standards can vary by crop and testing/certification
framework.

44. Factors Affecting Seed Purity
Before harvest
- variety mixtures,
- weeds,
- volunteers,
- cross-pollination.

During harvest
- mixed harvesting,

- machinery contamination.

During processing
- insufficient cleaning,
- lot mixing.

During storage
- poor identification,
- mixing,
- packaging errors.

During distribution
- improper handling,
- incorrect labeling.

45. How Farmers Can Maintain Seed Purity
For farm-saved seed where appropriate:
1. Start with correctly identified seed.
2. Select a suitable seed-production field.
3. Remove off-types.
4. Control weeds before seed production.
5. Prevent unwanted varietal mixtures.
6. Harvest separately.
7. Clean equipment.
8. Dry appropriately.
9. Store separately.
10.Label every seed lot.
11.Avoid mixing varieties.
12.Test seed when appropriate.

46. Seed Purity and Crop Uniformity
High genetic purity generally contributes to varietal uniformity.
High Genetic Purity→Greater True-to-Type Uniformity    
Uniformity can be important for:
- crop management,
- maturity,
- harvesting,
- processing,
- market quality.
However, desired uniformity depends on the crop and production system.

47. Seed Purity and Yield
Poor seed purity can reduce effective crop population.
For example:
Seed Lot→Weed Seeds/Foreign Seeds→Fewer Desired Crop Seeds    Thus the number of desired crop seeds planted per kilogram can decline.
But seed purity alone does not determine yield.
Yield=f(Genotype+Seed Quality+Environment+Management) 

48. Seed Purity and Weed Management
Weed contamination can introduce new weed populations into fields.
Contaminated Seed→Weed Introduction→Future Weed Seed Bank 
   
This is one reason high-quality planting seed is important.

49. Seed Purity and Seed Rate
Suppose a farmer needs 100 kg of pure seed but the seed lot has only 90% physical purity.
The required amount of commercial seed would be approximately:
Required Seed Lot=Required Pure SeedPurity Fraction  =
       =1000.90=   0.90 
111.11 kg   
This is an illustrative calculation; practical seed rates also account for germination, seed size and
establishment.

50. Pure Live Seed Concept
A related concept, especially useful for forage seeds, is pure live seed (PLS).
A simplified calculation is:
PLS(%)=Purity(%)×Germination(%)100 
    100   
For example:
Purity = 90%
Germination = 80%
PLS=90×80100PLS=  100  PLS=72% 
This combines physical purity and germination into one practical measure.

51. Seed Purity and Seed Lot
A seed lot is a defined quantity of seed treated as a unit for identification, handling and testing.
Purity is therefore normally evaluated for a seed lot, not simply for the crop species in general.
Purity is a property of the seed lot being tested   

52. Why Representative Sampling Is Critical
Imagine a 10-ton seed lot containing a small contaminated section.

If the sample is accidentally taken only from a clean portion:
Measured Purity>Actual Lot Purity  >  
could occur.
Therefore:
Sampling Error→Testing Error    
This is why standardized sampling procedures are important.

53. Seed Purity in the Complete Seed Production
Chain

↓

↓

↓

↓

54. Seed Purity — Complete Conceptual Model
Seed Purity=Physical Purity+Genetic/Varietal Purity→Correct Crop+Correct Variety+Minimal Contam
ination→Uniform Establishment+Reduced Weed/Crop Contamination→Better Crop Management ed      &=   +  \\
& +   +  \\

Remember that the plus signs here represent components/dimensions, not a literal numerical addition
- f percentages.

55. Important Differences
Term
Main meaning
Seed purity
Correct seed composition/identity with minimal specified contamination
Physical purity Proportion of pure seed versus other crop seed, weed seed and inert matter
Genetic purity Conformity to the stated variety/genotype
Viability
Whether seed is alive
Germination
Production of normal seedlings under defined conditions
Vigor
Speed, uniformity and robustness of seed performance
Seed health

Seed moisture

Certified seed

Foundation seed Seed multiplication class
Hybrid seed

56. Common Misconceptions
"100% purity means 100% germination."

❌ No.

Purity≠Germination 

"Pure seed means genetically pure."

❌ Not necessarily.

Physical purity and genetic purity are different measurements.

"Large seeds are always purer."

❌ No.

Seed size and purity are different characteristics.

"Cleaning automatically makes seed genetically pure."

❌ No.

Cleaning primarily removes physical contaminants.

"Certified seed means 100% purity."

❌ No.

Certification involves prescribed standards and tolerances; it does not mean literal absolute perfection.

"A disease-free seed is necessarily pure seed."

❌ No.

Seed health and seed purity are different dimensions.

"Seed purity is only about removing stones and dirt."

❌ Too narrow.

Purity also concerns unwanted crop seeds, weed seeds and, in a broader seed-quality context, genetic
identity.

57. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed purity is the degree to which a seed lot consists of the intended crop seed and
conforms to the required physical and varietal identity standards, with minimal
contamination by other crop seeds, weed seeds, inert matter or other unwanted
components. Physical purity is commonly determined by separating and weighing
pure seed, other crop seeds, weed seeds and inert matter, with physical purity
calculated as the weight of pure seed divided by the total sample weight multiplied by
100. Genetic or varietal purity refers to conformity of the seed lot to the stated variety
and is maintained through correct source seed, field isolation where required, rouging,
controlled seed multiplication, separate harvesting, proper processing and careful
storage. Seed purity should not be confused with germination, viability, vigor or seed

health. High-purity seed helps establish the intended crop population, reduce weed
and varietal contamination and maintain crop uniformity, but final crop yield also
depends on genotype, environment, seed quality and management.

58. Final Master Memory
Physical purity
PP=Pure SeedTotal Sample×100  Total\ Sample   100  

Seed lot composition
Seed Lot=Pure Seed+Other Crop Seed+Weed Seed+Inert Matter 

Genetic purity
Correct Variety→True-to-Type Plants→Genetic Purity     

Maintaining purity
Source Seed+Isolation+Rouging+Separate Harvest+Cleaning+Proper Storage→Seed Purity   

Seed quality
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  =   +
  +   +    

Most important distinction
SEED PURITY = CORRECT SEED + MINIMAL UNWANTED CONTAMINATION   
and:
PURITY≠GERMINATION≠VIABILITY≠VIGOR≠SEED HEALTH 
     
One-line memory: Seed purity tells us how closely a seed lot conforms to the intended
crop and variety and how free it is from unwanted crop seeds, weed seeds and inert
material; it is an important component of seed quality but is not the same as
germination, vigor, viability or seed health.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Germination Percentage
## 1. Introduction
Germination percentage is one of the most important indicators of the physiological quality of seed.
It tells us what proportion of seeds in a tested sample are capable of producing normal seedlings
under the prescribed germination test conditions.
In simple terms:
Germination percentage tells us how many seeds successfully germinate and produce
normal seedlings out of the total number of seeds tested.
The basic formula is:
Germination (%)=Number of normal seedlingsTotal number of seeds tested×100        100  

## 2. Definition
Germination percentage is the percentage of seeds in a sample that produce normal
seedlings under specified test conditions within the prescribed testing period.
The words normal seedlings, specified conditions, and prescribed period are important because
germination is measured using a defined testing procedure rather than simply counting any seed that
shows a root.

3. Basic Formula
GP=NT×100  T  100  
Where:
- GPGP = germination percentage
- NN = number of normal seedlings
- TT = total number of seeds tested

## 4. Example
Suppose:
- Seeds tested = 100
- Normal seedlings = 85
Then:
GP=85100×100GP=   100  100 GP=85% 
This means that 85% of the tested seeds produced normal seedlings under the specified test
conditions.
It does not mean that exactly 85% will necessarily emerge in a farmer's field.

5. Another Example
Suppose 400 seeds are tested and 348 produce normal seedlings.
GP=348400×100GP=   400  100 GP=87% 
Therefore, the germination percentage is 87%.

6. What Is a Normal Seedling?
A normal seedling is a seedling that has the essential structures needed for continued development into
a healthy plant, according to the applicable seed-testing rules.
Depending on the crop, this generally involves properly developed:
- root system,
- shoot,
- primary structures,
- necessary supporting tissues.
A seed that begins germinating but produces a severely abnormal seedling may not be counted as a
normal germinating seedling.
Therefore:
Radicle Emergence≠Automatically Normal Seedling 
  

7. Germination Process
The biological process can be represented as:
Viable Seed→Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Norm
al Seedling        

Step 1 — Imbibition
The dry seed absorbs water.

Step 2 — Metabolic activation
Enzymes and respiration become active.

Step 3 — Reserve mobilization
Stored:
- starch,
- proteins,
- oils
are converted into usable forms.

Step 4 — Cell division and expansion
Embryonic tissues begin growing.

Step 5 — Radicle emergence
The embryonic root emerges.

Step 6 — Seedling development
The shoot develops and the seedling becomes established.

8. Requirements for Germination
Three fundamental requirements are:
Water+Suitable Temperature+O2 
However, depending on the species, light/dark conditions, substrate and other factors may also
influence germination.

Water
Water initiates:
- imbibition,
- enzyme activation,
- metabolism,
- reserve mobilization,
- cell expansion.
Too little water:
Water deficiency→Germination↓Water\ deficiency Germination Excess water can also be harmful:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Respiration problemsWaterlogging O 2  Respiration\ problems

Temperature
Seeds have a temperature range involving:
- minimum/base temperature,
- optimum temperature,
- maximum temperature.
Conceptually:
Tmin<Topt<Tmax <T  opt <T  max   
Too low:
Metabolism↓→Germination slowsMetabolism  Germination\ slows
Too high:
Enzyme/membrane damage→Germination↓Enzyme/membrane\ damage Germination Oxygen
Germinating seeds generally require oxygen for aerobic respiration.

O2→Respiration→ATP→Growth 
Waterlogging can reduce oxygen availability.

9. Germination Percentage vs Emergence
Percentage
These are different.

Germination
Measured under defined test conditions.

Field emergence
Actual appearance of seedlings above the soil surface.
Therefore:
Germination%≠Field Emergence% 
For example:
95% laboratory germination95\% 
may result in:
80% field emergence80\% 
if field conditions are unfavorable.

10. Why Field Emergence Can Be Lower
Even high-germination seed can have poor field establishment because of:
- drought,
- excessive moisture,
- soil crusting,
- compaction,
- unsuitable temperature,
- incorrect sowing depth,

- salinity,
- poor seed-soil contact,
- soil-borne pathogens,
- insects,
- poor seedbed preparation.
Thus:
Good Seed+Poor Field Conditions→Poor Emergence  +     

11. Germination Percentage vs Viability
These are closely related but different.

Viability
Whether a seed is alive and has the potential to germinate.

Germination
Whether it actually produces a normal seedling under specified conditions.
Therefore:
Viability≠Germination   
A viable seed may fail to germinate because of:
- dormancy,
- unsuitable temperature,
- insufficient water,
- lack of oxygen,
- unfavorable light conditions,
- other physiological restrictions.

12. Germination Percentage vs Seed Vigor
Germination percentage
Measures the proportion that produces normal seedlings under defined test conditions.

Seed vigor
Reflects the seed's ability to:
- germinate rapidly,
- emerge uniformly,
- establish strongly,
- perform under a range of conditions.
Therefore:
High Germination%≠High Vigor 
Two seed lots can both have 90% germination but differ substantially in field performance.

13. Germination Percentage vs Seed Purity
These are completely different measurements.

Seed purity
What is present in the seed lot?

Germination percentage
How many tested seeds produce normal seedlings?
Purity≠Germination 
Example:
Seed lot Purity Germination
A
99%
70%
B
90%
95%
Both measurements provide different information.

14. Germination Test
A germination test is a controlled test used to determine the percentage of seeds producing normal
seedlings under specified conditions.
A simplified procedure is:
Representative Sample→Place Seeds Under Test Conditions→Maintain Moisture + Temperature + Ox
ygen→Observe→Classify Seedlings→Calculate Germination % 

      

15. Number of Seeds Tested
Common test sizes may involve a specified number of seeds, often arranged in replicates.
For example:
4×100=400 seeds4 100=400 
If 360 are classified as normal seedlings:
GP=360400×100GP=   400  100 90% 
Actual official testing procedures depend on crop and applicable seed-testing rules.

## 16. Replicates
Seeds are often divided into multiple replicates.
For example:
- Replicate 1 → 100 seeds
- Replicate 2 → 100 seeds
- Replicate 3 → 100 seeds
- Replicate 4 → 100 seeds
Total:
400 seeds400 
Replicates help assess consistency and reduce the influence of random variation.

17. Germination Test Conditions
A germination test controls important environmental conditions such as:

Temperature
Maintained within the appropriate range for the crop.

Moisture
Adequate moisture is supplied.

Oxygen
Seeds must have adequate gas exchange.

Substrate
Examples can include:
- paper,
- sand,
- other standardized germination media.

Light
Some species require specified light conditions.
The exact requirements are crop- and method-specific.

18. Germination Count
During testing, seedlings are evaluated at prescribed counting times.
There may be:
- first count,
- final count.

First count
Provides information about early germination.

Final count
Determines the final germination percentage under the specified test.
The exact counting schedule varies by crop and testing standard.

19. First Count vs Final Germination
Suppose:

Initial count:
70 normal seedlings70 
Final count:
90 normal seedlings90 
Then:
Final Germination=90% =90\%
The first count provides information about speed and early performance, while final germination
describes the final proportion meeting the germination criteria.

20. Germination Speed
Two seed lots may have the same final germination but different germination speeds.

Lot A
Most seeds germinate early.

Lot B
Seeds germinate slowly but eventually reach the same final percentage.
Thus:
Final Germination≠Germination Speed   
This is one reason seed vigor tests are useful.

21. Germination Rate
A simple germination rate can be expressed using germination over time.
For more detailed analysis, researchers may use:

Mean Germination Time (MGT)
MGT=∑niti∑ni    n i   
where:
- nin i = number of seeds germinated at time tit i
- tit i = time after the beginning of the test.

Lower MGT generally indicates faster germination.

22. Example of Mean Germination Time
Suppose:
Day Seeds germinated
Total:
Then:
MGT=(20×2)+(30×3)+(30×4)+(10×5)90MGT=
   90  =40+90+120+5090=
  90  MGT=3.33 days   
This gives information about how quickly the seeds germinated, not simply how many germinated.

23. Germination Energy
Germination energy generally describes the proportion of seeds germinating rapidly within an early
specified period.
It is related to seed vigor.
The exact definition and calculation vary according to the testing method.
Conceptually:
Higher Early Germination→Greater Germination Vigor 
  

24. Factors Affecting Germination Percentage
Internal factors
- seed viability,

- seed maturity,
- seed age,
- genetic characteristics,
- dormancy,
- seed composition,
- physiological condition,
- mechanical damage,
- seed-borne infection.

External factors
- water,
- temperature,
- oxygen,
- light,
- salinity,
- substrate,
- soil condition,
- pathogens,
- sowing depth.

25. Seed Dormancy
A viable seed may fail to germinate even when some favorable conditions are present because of
dormancy.
Viable Seed+Favorable Conditions+Dormancy→No Germination 
Dormancy can be associated with:
- hard seed coat,
- physiological inhibitors,
- immature embryo,
- hormonal balance,

- environmental adaptation.

26. Dormancy vs Dead Seed
This distinction is critical.

Dormant seed
Alive but temporarily unable to germinate under the test conditions.

Dead seed
Not viable.
Therefore:
Dormant≠Dead 
A viability test can sometimes help distinguish them.

27. Seed Priming and Germination
Seed priming is controlled hydration of seeds before sowing so that metabolic processes begin without
allowing full radicle emergence.
Types include:
- hydropriming,
- osmopriming,
- halopriming,
- biopriming,
- nutrient priming.
Conceptually:
Priming→Metabolic Activation→Faster/More Uniform Germination 
The response depends on crop, seed lot and priming method.

28. Seed Treatment
Seed treatment can protect seeds and seedlings against certain:
- pathogens,
- insects,
- physiological problems.
Healthy seed and appropriate treatment can improve establishment, but treatment cannot compensate
for poor seed viability.

29. Seed Aging
As seeds age, physiological deterioration can occur.
Aging→Cellular Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓ 
Factors accelerating deterioration include:
- high moisture,
- high temperature,
- poor storage,
- oxidative damage,
- insects,
- fungi.

30. Seed Moisture and Germination
Seed moisture strongly affects storage life and physiological quality.
Generally:
Poor Storage→Moisture/Temperature Stress→Deterioration→Germination↓ 
However, appropriate storage moisture differs among crop species and seed types.

31. Mechanical Damage
Damage during:
- harvesting,
- threshing,
- processing,
- transportation,
can injure the embryo or other important seed structures.
Mechanical Damage→Viability/Vigor↓→Germination↓ 

32. Seed-Borne Pathogens
Some pathogens can be carried on or within seeds.
They can:
- reduce germination,
- cause seed rot,
- cause seedling disease,
- reduce emergence.
Therefore:
Seed Health→Germination+Seedling Establishment 

33. Salinity and Germination
High salt concentration can create osmotic stress.
Salinity↑→Water Uptake↓→Germination↓ 
Some salts can additionally produce ion toxicity.
Salt tolerance differs among crops and varieties.

34. Waterlogging and Germination
Excess water can fill soil pores and reduce oxygen.
Waterlogging→O2↓→Aerobic Respiration↓→Germination/Seedling Growth↓ 

35. Temperature Stress
Low temperature
Temperature↓→Metabolism↓→Germination slowsTemperature  Metabolism  Germination\ slows

Excessive temperature
Temperature↑→Membrane/Enzyme Stress→Germination↓Temperature  Membrane/Enzyme\ Stress  Germination Therefore, the optimum temperature range is important.

36. Germination Percentage and Seed Rate
Germination percentage affects the number of seeds expected to produce seedlings.
Suppose:
- desired plants = 100,000,
- germination = 90%.
Ignoring other establishment losses:
Required seeds=1000000.90Required\ seeds =   0.90 
111111 seeds approximately   
In practical farming, field emergence and establishment should also be considered.

37. Purity + Germination
If seed purity is less than 100%, the amount of pure viable seed is lower than the total seed weight.
A simplified pure-live-seed concept is:

PLS(%)=Purity(%)×Germination(%)100 
 100   
Example:
Purity = 90%
Germination = 80%
PLS=90×80100PLS=   100  PLS=72% 
This concept is especially useful in forage-seed calculations.

38. Germination Percentage and Field
Establishment
A more realistic establishment chain is:
Seed Quality→Germination→Emergence→Establishment→Plant Population 
Losses can occur at every stage.
Therefore:
Germination% is not equal to final plant population   

39. Germination Percentage and Yield
Germination affects initial plant establishment.
Germination→Plant Population→Crop Establishment→Yield Potential 
But yield is affected by many additional factors:
Yield=f(G,E,G×E,Water,Nutrients,Pests,Diseases,Management) 
Therefore, high germination alone does not guarantee high yield.

40. High Germination but Poor Field
Performance
Consider:
95% laboratory germination   
but:
60% field emergence   
Possible reasons include:
- drought,
- crusted soil,
- deep sowing,
- compaction,
- salinity,
- waterlogging,
- soil pathogens,
- unsuitable temperature.
This demonstrates why seed testing and field management must be considered together.

41. Germination Percentage in Seed Quality
The complete seed-quality framework is:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  =   +
  +   +    
Germination is primarily a physiological quality indicator.

42. Relationship Between Major Seed Terms
Term
Seed purity
Genetic purity
Viability

Main question
What is in the seed lot?
Is it the correct variety?
Is the seed alive?

Term
Main question
Germination % How many produce normal seedlings under test conditions?
Vigor
How rapidly and uniformly can seeds establish?
Seed health
Is the seed affected by biological contaminants?
Moisture
How much water does the seed contain?

43. Important Distinction: Germination vs
Emergence
Germination
Occurs when the embryo resumes growth and meets the criteria for a normal seedling under a
germination test.

Emergence
Occurs when the developing seedling successfully emerges through the soil surface in the field.
Therefore:
Germination is a seed event   
while:
Emergence is a seedling-field event   

44. Important Distinction: Germination vs Vigor
Consider two lots:

Lot A
- Germination = 95%
- Rapid and uniform

Lot B
- Germination = 95%
- Slow and uneven
Both have the same final germination percentage, but Lot A may have greater vigor.
Thus:

Same Germination≠Same Vigor   

45. Important Distinction: Germination vs
Viability
Suppose 100 seeds are alive but 10 are dormant under the test conditions.
It is possible for:
Viability=100% =100\%
while:
Germination<100% <100\%
depending on the test.
Thus:
Viable Seed Can Be Dormant   

46. Practical Seed-Lot Diagnosis
If germination percentage is low, investigate in this order:
Seed Source→Viability→Dormancy→Moisture→Temperature→Oxygen→Seed Health→Mechanical
Damage          
For field emergence, additionally investigate:
Soil Moisture+Soil Temperature+Sowing Depth+Compaction+Crusting+Salinity+Pests/
Diseases  +   +   +
  +   +   +    

47. Common Misconceptions
"90% germination means 90% field emergence."

❌ Not necessarily.

"A seed that develops a tiny root is always counted as germinated."

❌ Not necessarily. Seed-testing standards generally distinguish normal and abnormal seedlings.
"Germination percentage tells seed vigor."

❌ Not completely.

Vigor requires additional assessment.

"100% germination means every seed will produce a healthy plant."

❌ No.

Field conditions can prevent establishment.

"Low germination always means the seed is dead."

❌ No.

Dormancy or unsuitable test conditions can reduce germination.

"Germination is the same as emergence."

❌ No.

Germination≠Emergence 

48. Exam-Ready Definition
Germination percentage is the percentage of seeds in a representative sample that
produce normal seedlings under specified environmental conditions and within the
prescribed testing period. It is calculated as the number of normal seedlings divided
by the total number of seeds tested, multiplied by 100. Germination depends on seed
viability, maturity, dormancy, moisture, temperature, oxygen, seed health and other
factors. Germination percentage is an important indicator of physiological seed
quality but should not be confused with seed purity, viability, vigor or field emergence.
High laboratory germination does not guarantee equivalent field emergence because
soil moisture, temperature, oxygen, salinity, compaction, sowing depth, pathogens and
- ther field conditions influence establishment.

49. Final Master Memory
Formula
Germination(%)=Normal SeedlingsTotal Seeds Tested×100   Total\ Seeds\ Tested   100  

Germination sequence
Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Normal Seedling xed  Imbibition  Metabolic\ Activation  Reserve\ Mobilization  Radicle\ Emergence  Normal\ Seedling  

Main requirements
Water+Suitable Temperature+Oxygen 
with crop-specific requirements for light and other conditions.

Key distinctions
Germination≠Viability   
Germination≠Vigor   
Germination≠Purity   
Germination≠Field Emergence   

Complete seed-quality relationship
Seed Quality→Germination→Emergence→Establishment→Plant Population→Yield Potential        
One-line memory: Germination percentage tells us what proportion of a tested seed
sample produces normal seedlings under specified test conditions; it is calculated as
normal seedlings ÷ total seeds tested × 100 and is a measure of physiological seed
quality, not a guarantee of field emergence or final crop yield.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Seed Vigor
## 1. Introduction
Seed vigor describes the overall ability of a seed lot to germinate rapidly, emerge uniformly,
establish strong seedlings, and perform well across a range of environmental conditions.
It is different from simply asking whether a seed can germinate.
The key idea is:
Vigor=Speed+Uniformity+Strength+Stress Tolerance =   +
  +   +    
A seed lot can have high germination percentage but low vigor.

## 2. Definition
Seed vigor is the sum of those properties of a seed or seed lot that determine its
potential for rapid, uniform emergence and development of normal seedlings under a
broad range of environmental conditions.
The exact wording and test methods can vary among seed-testing frameworks, but the central concept
is performance beyond final germination percentage.

3. Why Seed Vigor Is Important
Seed vigor strongly affects early crop establishment.
High Seed Vigor→Rapid Germination→Uniform Emergence→Strong Seedlings→Better Stand Establi
shment       
Low-vigor seed may:
- germinate slowly,
- emerge unevenly,

- produce weak seedlings,
- be more sensitive to stress,
- deteriorate faster during storage,
- establish poorly under unfavorable field conditions.

4. Seed Vigor vs Germination Percentage
This is the most important distinction.

Germination percentage asks:
How many seeds produce normal seedlings under specified test conditions?

Seed vigor asks:
How rapidly, uniformly and robustly can those seeds perform, especially under lessthan-ideal conditions?
Therefore:
High Germination≠High Vigor   

## 5. Example
Consider two seed lots:
Characteristic
Lot A Lot B
Germination
95%
95%
Germination speed
Fast
Slow
Emergence
Uniform Uneven
Stress performance Strong Weak
Seedling growth
Strong Weak
Both lots have:
95% germination  
But Lot A may have substantially greater vigor.
This demonstrates why germination percentage alone cannot fully describe seed quality.

6. Germination vs Vigor vs Viability
Property
Main question
Viability
Is the seed alive?
Germination % Can it produce a normal seedling under specified test conditions?
Vigor
How rapidly, uniformly and robustly does it perform?
A useful relationship is:
Viability→Germination Capacity→Vigor/Performance     
But these are different measurements, not interchangeable quantities.

7. Seed Vigor Is a Seed-Lot Characteristic
Vigor is commonly evaluated at the seed-lot level.
Two lots of the same variety can have different vigor because of differences in:
- maturity,
- harvesting,
- drying,
- mechanical damage,
- storage,
- temperature,
- moisture,
- seed health.
Therefore:
Same Variety≠Same Seed Vigor   

8. Components of Seed Vigor
Important aspects include:

1. Germination speed
How quickly seeds germinate.

2. Emergence speed
How quickly seedlings emerge from soil.

## 3. Uniformity
Whether seeds germinate and emerge at approximately the same time.

4. Seedling strength
Ability to produce strong seedlings.

5. Stress tolerance
Ability to perform under unfavorable conditions.

6. Storage potential
Ability to retain quality during storage.

9. Seed Vigor During Germination
High-vigor seeds generally proceed efficiently through:
Imbibition→Membrane Repair→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence - xed  Imbibition  Membrane\ Repair  Metabolic\ Activation  Reserve\ Mobilization  Radicle\ Emergence  
Low-vigor seeds may have greater physiological deterioration and slower recovery after hydration.

10. Seed Aging and Vigor
Seed vigor often declines before a large decline in final germination becomes obvious.
A conceptual deterioration sequence is:
Seed Aging→Membrane Damage→Metabolic Dysfunction→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Seed Death ed  Seed\ Aging  Membrane\ Damage  Metabolic\ Dysfunction  Vigor  Germination  Seed\ Death  
This is why vigor can act as an early indicator of seed deterioration.

11. Membrane Integrity
Dry seeds undergo substantial physiological changes during rehydration.
High-quality seeds can restore membrane function effectively.
Deteriorated seeds may have:
- membrane leakage,
- electrolyte loss,
- poor cellular organization,
- impaired metabolism.
Therefore:
Membrane Integrity→Efficient Germination→High Vigor 
   

12. Respiration and Seed Vigor
After imbibition, respiration increases.
Substrate+O2→CO2+H2O+ATP +O 2  CO 2+H 2O+ATP  
ATP supports:
- cell division,
- cell expansion,
- enzyme activity,
- transport,
- seedling development.
High-vigor seeds generally establish active metabolism efficiently after hydration.

13. Reserve Mobilization
Seeds store nutrients as:
- carbohydrates,
- proteins,
- lipids.

During germination:
Starch→Sugars  Proteins→Amino acids 
 Lipids→Fatty acids + Glycerol  Efficient mobilization supports rapid seedling development.

14. Seedling Growth and Vigor
High-vigor seeds generally produce seedlings with better early growth.
Measurements can include:
- shoot length,
- root length,
- seedling dry weight,
- total seedling length.
One simple vigor index is:
Vigor Index−I=Germination(%)×Mean Seedling Length 
This is one commonly used formulation, although vigor-index methods can vary.

15. Example of Vigor Index
Suppose:
- germination = 90%
- average seedling length = 20 cm.
Then:
VI−I=90×20VI-I=90 20 VI−I=1800 
A higher value generally indicates stronger early performance when comparing seed lots using the
same test procedure.

16. Another Vigor Index
A commonly used biomass-based formulation is:
Vigor Index−II=Germination(%)×Seedling Dry Weight 
Again, exact methods and units should be stated when reporting results.

17. Germination Speed
A high-vigor seed lot usually germinates rapidly and uniformly.
For example:

Lot A
Day 1 → 20 seeds
Day 2 → 50 seeds
Day 3 → 20 seeds
Day 4 → 5 seeds

Lot B
Day 1 → 2 seeds
Day 2 → 10 seeds
Day 3 → 25 seeds
Day 4 → 58 seeds
Both could eventually reach:
95%95\%
But Lot A demonstrates faster early performance.

18. Mean Germination Time
A useful measure is:
MGT=∑niti∑ni    n i   
where:
- nin i = number of seeds germinated at time tit i,
- tit i = time after the start of the test.

Generally:
Lower MGT→Faster Germination 
when comparing the same crop and testing conditions.

19. Germination Rate vs Germination
Percentage
Germination percentage
Final proportion germinated.

Germination rate
How rapidly germination occurs.
Thus:
Percentage tells "how many"   
Rate tells "how quickly"   
Vigor incorporates this temporal performance along with other properties.

20. Seed Vigor Under Stress
This is where vigor becomes particularly important.
Seeds may face:
- drought,
- cold,
- heat,
- salinity,
- waterlogging,
- soil crusting,
- compaction.
High-vigor seeds generally have a better chance of establishing under suboptimal conditions.
Conceptually:

High Vigor→Better Performance Under Stress    
But vigor does not mean complete resistance to severe environmental stress.

21. Cold Stress and Vigor
Low temperature can slow:
- enzyme activity,
- membrane processes,
- respiration,
- root development.
A high-vigor seed lot may establish better under moderately unfavorable temperature conditions than a
low-vigor lot.

22. Drought Stress and Vigor
Low soil water limits imbibition.
Soil Water↓→Imbibition↓→Germination/Emergence↓ 
High-vigor seeds can have better establishment potential under moderate water stress, but they cannot
germinate without sufficient water.

23. Salinity and Vigor
High salt concentration creates osmotic stress and can interfere with water uptake.
Salinity↑→Water Uptake↓→Germination/Seedling Growth↓ 
Vigor tests under salinity can therefore help distinguish seed lots with different stress performance.

24. Waterlogging and Vigor
Excess water reduces oxygen availability.
Waterlogging→O2↓→Aerobic Respiration↓→Seedling Establishment↓ 
High vigor can improve performance under some suboptimal conditions but cannot eliminate oxygen
limitation.

25. Factors Affecting Seed Vigor
Genetic factors
- genotype,
- variety,
- hybrid,
- seed composition.

Pre-harvest factors
- plant nutrition,
- drought,
- heat,
- disease,
- maturity,
- growing conditions.

Harvest factors
- harvest maturity,
- mechanical damage,
- threshing intensity.

Post-harvest factors
- drying,

- temperature,
- moisture,
- processing,
- storage,
- insects,
- fungi.

26. Seed Maturity and Vigor
Seeds harvested at appropriate physiological maturity generally have better potential for high-quality
development.
Immature seeds may have:
- incomplete reserve accumulation,
- weak structures,
- poor longevity,
- reduced vigor.
However, the relationship is crop-specific.

27. Mechanical Damage and Vigor
Mechanical injury can damage:
- embryo,
- seed coat,
- storage tissues.
Mechanical Damage→Membrane/Embryo Injury→Vigor↓ 
Repeated handling can increase deterioration.

28. Storage Conditions and Vigor
Poor storage can accelerate deterioration.
A general relationship is:
High Moisture+High Temperature→Deterioration↑→Vigor↓ 
For many orthodox seeds, controlling moisture and temperature is central to maintaining quality.

29. Seed Health and Vigor
Pathogens can reduce:
- germination,
- seedling growth,
- emergence,
- uniformity.
Thus:
Seed Health→Seed Vigor→Seedling Establishment 
Seed health and vigor are related but are not identical measurements.

30. Seed Vigor Tests
Different tests evaluate different aspects of vigor.

A. Speed of germination
Measures how quickly seeds germinate.

B. Seedling growth test
Measures:
- root length,
- shoot length,
- seedling dry weight.

C. Accelerated aging test
Subjects seeds to controlled aging conditions and then evaluates germination.
Conceptually:
Artificial Aging→Stress Exposure→Germination Test 
Higher performance after the aging treatment generally indicates greater vigor.

31. Electrical Conductivity Test
This evaluates electrolyte leakage from seeds after controlled soaking.
Deteriorated membranes tend to leak more cellular solutes.
Membrane Damage↑→Electrolyte Leakage↑→EC↑ 
Generally:
Lower EC→Better Membrane Integrity→Higher Vigor 
when comparing the same crop under the same standardized method.

32. Tetrazolium Test
The tetrazolium test is primarily a viability assessment, rather than a complete vigor test.
Living tissues with active respiration reduce tetrazolium compounds and develop characteristic
staining.
Thus:
Tetrazolium→Rapid Viability Assessment 
It can provide useful physiological information but should not simply be treated as a universal vigor
measurement.

33. Cold Test
Seeds are exposed to controlled cold conditions and subsequently evaluated for germination/seedling
performance.
It can help assess performance under stressful low-temperature conditions in suitable crops.

34. Accelerated Aging Test
This is widely used as a vigor assessment method.
General sequence:
Seed Lot→Controlled High Temperature/Humidity→Artificial Aging→Standard Germination Test ed  Seed\ Lot  Controlled\ High\ Temperature/Humidity  Artificial\ Aging
 Standard\ Germination\ Test  
Seed lots retaining better germination after aging generally have higher vigor.

35. Controlled Deterioration
Seeds are subjected to controlled moisture and temperature conditions for a defined period and then
tested.
The concept is:
Stress Exposure→Deterioration→Measure Remaining Performance 

36. Seedling Growth Test
A simple approach is to compare seedling development.
Measurements include:
Root Length  Shoot Length 
Seedling Dry Weight 
Greater early growth under standardized conditions can indicate greater vigor.

37. Field Emergence Test
Seeds are planted under field or simulated field conditions and emergence is recorded.
For example:
Field Emergence(%)=Emerged SeedlingsSeeds Planted×100   Seeds\ Planted   100  
This can provide valuable information about real-world establishment, although field conditions vary
and results are environment-specific.

38. Vigor and Uniformity
Uniformity is particularly important for commercial crop production.
Suppose:

High-vigor lot
Emergence: 2,2,3,3,3,4 days  2,2,3,3,3,4 

Low-vigor lot
Emergence: 2,3,4,5,7,9 days 2,3,4,5,7,9 
The first lot establishes a more uniform crop.
Uniform establishment helps synchronize:
- growth,
- flowering,
- maturity,
- harvesting.

39. Vigor and Crop Competition
