Strong, rapidly emerging seedlings may establish earlier.
Fast Emergence→Earlier Canopy Development→Better Early Resource Capture 
This can improve competitive ability against weeds, although weed management remains necessary.

40. Vigor and Plant Population
High-vigor seed can improve establishment and help achieve the intended plant population.
Seed Vigor→Emergence→Established Plants→Plant Population 
Poor vigor can increase gaps and unevenness in the field.

41. Vigor and Yield
Seed vigor mainly affects early establishment, but good establishment can influence later crop
development.
High Vigor→Uniform Stand→Better Establishment→Yield Potential 
However:
High Vigor≠Guaranteed High Yield 
Final yield also depends on:
- genotype,
- soil,
- climate,
- water,
- nutrients,
- weeds,
- pests,
- diseases,
- crop management.

42. Vigor and Seed Storage
Vigor is particularly useful for monitoring seed deterioration during storage.
A possible pattern is:

Fresh Seed→Vigor Decline→Germination Decline→Loss of Viability 
    
Thus vigor can decline before conventional germination percentage shows a dramatic reduction.

43. Vigor and Seed Lot Comparison
Suppose:
Parameter
Lot A
Lot B
Germination
95%
95%
MGT
2.5 days 4.0 days
Seedling length
22 cm 15 cm
Accelerated aging germination
88%
65%
Although final germination is identical, the additional measurements indicate different physiological
performance.
This illustrates why:
Germination Test Alone   
may not fully distinguish seed-lot quality.

44. Seed Vigor and Seed Quality
Remember the complete framework:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  =   +
  +   +    
Vigor is primarily a physiological quality characteristic.

45. Seed Vigor and Your Previous Topics
Topic
Seed biology
Seed quality
Seed purity
Germination %
Viability

Main question
How does a seed develop and function?
Overall fitness of the seed lot
What unwanted material/seed is present?
How many produce normal seedlings under the test?
Are the seeds alive?

Topic
Main question
Seed vigor
How rapidly, uniformly and robustly do they perform?
Seed health
Are pathogens/biological contaminants present?
Certified seed
Does the lot meet applicable certification requirements?
Hybrid seed
Is it the intended F₁ cross?
OPV
Is the variety maintained through open pollination?
Improved variety Has it been deliberately bred/selected for desired traits?

46. A Very Important Example
Imagine two seed lots of the same variety.

Lot A
- purity = 99%
- germination = 95%
- vigor = high

Lot B
- purity = 99%
- germination = 95%
- vigor = low
The two lots have identical:
Purity=99% =99\%
and:
Germination=95% =95\%
but they can differ in:
- emergence speed,
- emergence uniformity,
- seedling strength,
- stress performance.
Therefore:
Seed Quality Cannot Be Described by Germination Alone   

47. How to Maintain High Seed Vigor
Before harvest
- maintain good crop nutrition,
- manage pests and diseases,
- avoid severe stress where possible,
- allow proper seed maturation.

During harvest
- harvest at appropriate maturity,
- minimize mechanical injury,
- avoid excessive threshing damage.

Processing
- gentle handling,
- appropriate cleaning,
- avoid excessive mechanical damage.

Drying
- dry appropriately for the seed species and storage purpose.

Storage
- control moisture,
- control temperature,
- protect against insects,
- protect against fungi,
- maintain proper packaging.

Before planting
- use appropriate seed treatment when required,
- use suitable storage conditions,
- avoid old/deteriorated seed where quality is inadequate.

48. Practical Diagnostic Framework
If seed germination is acceptable but field emergence is poor:
Check Vigor   
Then investigate:
Seed Storage→Mechanical Damage→Seed Health→Soil Moisture→Temperature→Sowing Depth→C
- mpaction→Salinity→Waterlogging           
This helps distinguish seed-related problems from field-related problems.

49. Common Misconceptions
"High germination means high vigor."

❌ No.

High Germination≠High Vigor 

"Vigor is the same as viability."

❌ No.

Viability asks whether the seed is alive; vigor describes performance potential.

"Vigor is only germination speed."

❌ No.

Speed is one component. Vigor also involves uniformity, seedling strength, stress performance and
- ther properties.

"A high-vigor seed can germinate without water."

❌ No.

Water is necessary for seed imbibition and germination.

"Vigorous seed guarantees high yield."

❌ No.

Vigor mainly supports establishment; final yield depends on many factors.

"Vigor is a permanent genetic property of a variety."

❌ No.

Seed-lot vigor can change substantially because of maturity, handling and storage.

50. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed vigor is the sum of the properties of a seed or seed lot that determine its potential
for rapid, uniform germination, emergence and development of normal seedlings
under a broad range of environmental conditions. It is an important physiological
component of seed quality and differs from germination percentage, which measures
the final proportion of seeds producing normal seedlings under specified test
conditions. Seed vigor is influenced by genotype, seed maturity, pre-harvest
environment, mechanical damage, seed health, moisture, temperature and storage
conditions. Vigor can be evaluated using tests such as germination speed, seedling
growth, accelerated aging, electrical conductivity, controlled deterioration, cold
testing and field emergence, depending on the crop and testing objective. High-vigor
seed generally provides faster and more uniform establishment and better
performance under moderately unfavorable conditions, but high vigor alone does not
guarantee high yield because crop performance also depends on genotype,
environment and management.

51. Final Master Memory
Core definition
Seed Vigor = Ability to Germinate Rapidly + Emerge Uniformly + Establish Strongly + Tolerate Subop
timal Conditions   

Deterioration
Aging→Membrane Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Viability Loss 

Germination vs vigor
Germination = How many?   
Vigor = How fast, how uniformly, and how strongly?   

Seed establishment
High Vigor→Rapid Germination→Uniform Emergence→Strong Seedlings→Good Stand       

Key tests
Speed of Germination    Seedling Growth    Accelerated Aging   
Electrical Conductivity   
Controlled Deterioration   
Cold/Stress Tests   

Most important distinction
VIABILITY≠GERMINATION≠VIGOR    
One-line memory: Seed vigor is the capacity of a seed lot to germinate quickly, emerge
uniformly, produce strong seedlings and maintain performance under a range of
environmental conditions; unlike germination percentage, which mainly measures the
final proportion of normal seedlings under specified test conditions, vigor reveals
differences in the quality and performance potential of seed lots.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Seed Viability
## 1. Introduction
Seed viability refers to whether a seed is alive and capable of developing into a normal plant under
suitable conditions.
It is one of the fundamental properties of seed quality.
The simplest way to remember it is:

Viability asks: “Is the seed alive?”
This is different from:
- Germination → Can it produce a normal seedling under specified test conditions?
- Vigor → How rapidly, uniformly, and strongly can it perform?
- Purity → What proportion of the seed lot is the intended seed?

## 2. Definition
Seed viability is the capacity of a living seed to remain physiologically alive and
capable of germination and development into a normal seedling when suitable
conditions are provided.
A seed may be viable but dormant, meaning it is alive but does not germinate immediately under the
conditions provided.

3. Basic Concept
The relationship can be represented as:
Living Seed→Viable Seed→Potential to Germinate→Normal Seedling 
    
But an important distinction is:
Viability≠Germination   
because dormancy or unsuitable test conditions can prevent a viable seed from germinating.

4. Viability vs Germination
This is one of the most important concepts in seed science.

Viability
Asks:
Is the seed alive?

Germination
Asks:
Does the seed produce a normal seedling under specified test conditions?
Therefore:
A viable seed may fail to germinate because it is dormant.   
For example, imagine 100 seeds:
- 95 are alive,
- 5 are dead,
- but 10 of the living seeds are dormant.
The viability may be approximately:
95%95\%
while observed germination under the particular test may be lower.
The exact relationship depends on the crop and testing conditions.

5. Viability Percentage
When viability is expressed as a percentage:
Viability(%)=Number of viable seedsTotal seeds tested×100        100  

Example
Suppose 100 seeds are tested and 92 are determined to be viable.
Viability=92100×100Viability=   100  100 Viability=92% 

6. What Makes a Seed Viable?
A viable seed must retain sufficient biological integrity to carry out the processes required for
germination and seedling development.
Important components include:
- living embryo,
- functional cellular membranes,

- functioning metabolic systems,
- intact genetic material,
- sufficient physiological integrity,
- ability to resume growth when conditions become suitable.
Conceptually:
Viability=Living Embryo+Physiological Integrity  +
   

7. Seed Viability and the Embryo
The embryo is the most important living component of the seed.
It contains the structures that develop into:
- root,
- shoot,
- leaves,
- eventually the mature plant.
If the embryo is severely damaged or dead:
Embryo Death→Seed Non-viability viability  

8. Major Parts of a Seed
A simplified seed structure is:
Seed=Embryo+Storage Tissue+Protective Covering 

Embryo
Living plant precursor.

Storage tissue
Provides energy and nutrients.
Examples:
- endosperm,

- cotyledons.

Seed coat
Provides protection against:
- mechanical damage,
- pathogens,
- excessive water loss,
- environmental stresses.
However, the seed coat itself is not the primary indicator of whether the seed is alive.

9. Physiological Basis of Viability
A viable dry seed is metabolically inactive or highly quiescent compared with a germinating seed, but
its cells remain capable of resuming metabolism.
When water becomes available:
Imbibition→Membrane Reorganization→Metabolic Activation→Respiration→Growth 
If the seed's physiological systems have deteriorated beyond recovery, germination cannot occur.

10. Viability and Germination Process
Under suitable conditions:
Viable Seed→Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Seedli
ng 
A non-viable seed cannot complete this process.

11. Viability vs Dormancy
This distinction is extremely important.

Viable seed
Alive and potentially capable of germination.

Dormant seed
Alive but prevented from germinating by internal or external regulatory mechanisms despite apparently
favorable conditions.
Therefore:
Dormant Seed⊂Viable Seeds   
Conceptually:
Viable+Dormant→No Immediate Germination 
but:
Dead Seed→No Germination 

12. Dormancy Example
Suppose 100 seeds contain:
- 80 immediately germinating seeds,
- 15 viable but dormant seeds,
- 5 dead seeds.
Then:
Viability=80+15=95% =80+15=95\%
while immediate germination may be:
80%80\%
This illustrates:
Viability>Observed Germination 
when viable seeds are dormant.

13. Viability vs Vigor
Viability
Whether the seed is alive.

Vigor
How well the seed performs.
Thus:
Viability = Life    Vigor = Performance   
A seed can be:
- viable,
- capable of germinating,
but still have low vigor because it germinates slowly or produces weak seedlings.

14. Example: Viability and Vigor
Two seed lots:
Property
Lot A Lot B
Viability
95% 95%
Germination
93% 93%
Germination speed
Fast Slow
Seedling strength Strong Weak
Vigor
High Low
Therefore:
Same Viability≠Same Vigor 

15. Viability vs Germination vs Vigor
Property
Main question
Viability
Is the seed alive?
Germination % How many produce normal seedlings under the test conditions?
Vigor
How rapidly, uniformly and strongly do they perform?
This distinction should be memorized.

16. Methods for Testing Seed Viability
Several methods can be used.
Important methods include:
1. Germination test
2. Tetrazolium test
3. Embryo examination
4. X-ray examination
5. Electrical/physiological methods in research and specialized testing
6. Other biochemical or imaging techniques
The appropriate method depends on the crop, purpose and testing standard.

17. Germination Test as a Viability Indicator
A standard germination test can provide information about viability because seeds that produce normal
seedlings are clearly alive.
However:
Germination Test≠Perfect Direct Measurement of Viability 
  
Why?
Because a viable seed may remain dormant or may fail under unsuitable test conditions.
Therefore, specialized viability tests can sometimes provide faster or more direct physiological
information.

18. Tetrazolium Test
The tetrazolium test, commonly called the TZ test, is an important rapid biochemical method for
assessing seed viability.
The principle is based on the activity of living tissues.

Living tissues contain active respiratory enzymes that can reduce tetrazolium compounds and produce
a characteristic colored reaction.
Conceptually:
Living Tissue→Respiratory Enzyme Activity→Tetrazolium Reduction→Characteristic Staining   Living\ Tissue  Respiratory\ Enzyme\ Activity  Tetrazolium\ Reduction
 Characteristic\ Staining  
The pattern and intensity of staining are interpreted according to the crop and testing protocol.

19. Why Tetrazolium Is Useful
Advantages include:
- relatively rapid,
- does not require waiting for complete germination,
- useful for dormant seeds,
- useful for seeds requiring long germination periods,
- helps identify living and dead tissues.
It can also help diagnose whether particular embryo regions are alive or damaged.

20. Important Limitation of Tetrazolium
Tetrazolium is primarily a viability test.
It should not automatically be interpreted as a complete measurement of:
- seed vigor,
- field emergence,
- genetic purity,
- seed health.
Thus:
TZ Test≈Viability Information 
rather than:
TZ Test=Complete Seed Quality 

21. Embryo Examination
In some cases, the embryo can be examined directly.
The objective is to determine whether critical embryo tissues are:
- intact,
- living,
- damaged,
- dead.
This can be useful for certain seeds or diagnostic situations.

22. X-Ray Examination
X-ray imaging can help reveal internal seed structures without destroying the seed.
It can identify conditions such as:
- empty seeds,
- insect damage,
- mechanical damage,
- malformed embryos,
- structural abnormalities.
Thus:
X-ray→Internal Structure→Potential Viability Assessment ray  Internal\
Structure  Potential\ Viability\ Assessment  
Its use depends on the crop and testing purpose.

23. Factors Affecting Seed Viability
Viability can be affected by conditions before harvest, during harvest, processing and storage.

A. Genetic factors
Different species and genotypes have different:

- longevity,
- storage behavior,
- dormancy characteristics,
- sensitivity to environmental conditions.

B. Seed maturity
Immature seeds may have:
- incomplete development,
- inadequate reserve accumulation,
- weaker physiological systems.
Proper maturation generally supports better viability and longevity.

C. Harvest conditions
Poor harvesting conditions can cause:
- mechanical injury,
- cracking,
- embryo damage,
- moisture problems.

D. Mechanical damage
Damage to the embryo can directly reduce viability.
Mechanical Damage→Embryo Injury→Viability↓ 

24. Moisture and Viability
Seed moisture is one of the major factors influencing storage life for many orthodox seeds.
Generally:

Moisture↑→Metabolic Activity↑→Deterioration↑→Viability↓ 
High moisture can also encourage:
- fungal growth,
- insect activity,
- heating,
- biochemical deterioration.
However, exact safe moisture levels depend on species and storage system.

25. Temperature and Viability
For many orthodox seeds:
High Temperature+High Moisture→Rapid Deterioration 
Long-term storage generally requires appropriate control of both seed moisture and temperature.
But storage behavior differs substantially among seed types.

26. Seed Aging
Seed viability generally declines as deterioration progresses.
A simplified sequence is:
Seed Aging→Membrane Damage→Metabolic Dysfunction→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Viability Loss\
boxed  Seed\ Aging  Membrane\ Damage  Metabolic\ Dysfunction  Vigor  Germination  Viability\ Loss  
This is why seed lots should be periodically tested when stored for long periods.

27. Membrane Damage
One of the early physiological consequences of seed deterioration is loss of membrane integrity.
Damaged membranes can cause:
- electrolyte leakage,

- poor compartmentalization,
- impaired metabolism,
- abnormal germination.
Therefore:
Membrane Integrity→Cellular Function→Seed Viability 

28. Oxidative Damage
Seed aging can involve oxidative stress and accumulation of reactive oxygen species (ROS).
ROS↑→Lipid/Protein/DNA Damage→Physiological Deterioration 
Antioxidant systems help control oxidative damage, but prolonged deterioration can overwhelm
protective mechanisms.

29. Seed-Borne Pathogens
Pathogens associated with seeds can reduce seed viability or cause abnormal seedlings.
They may cause:
- seed rot,
- embryo damage,
- reduced germination,
- seedling disease.
Thus:
Poor Seed Health→Reduced Seed Performance 
But seed health and viability remain distinct concepts.

30. Insects and Viability
Insects can damage:

- seed coat,
- endosperm,
- cotyledons,
- embryo.
Severe embryo damage can make seeds non-viable.

31. Storage Types: Orthodox and Recalcitrant
Seed storage behavior is important for understanding viability.

Orthodox seeds
Generally tolerate substantial drying and can often be stored under suitable low-moisture, lowtemperature conditions.
Examples include many:
- cereals,
- pulses,
- oilseeds.

Recalcitrant seeds
Generally do not tolerate extensive drying and may lose viability rapidly when dried too far.
Examples include some:
- tropical tree species,
- certain plantation/horticultural species.
Therefore:
Storage Strategy≠Same for All Seeds 

32. Viability During Storage
For a well-managed orthodox seed lot, the general pattern is:
Fresh High-quality Seed→Vigor Decline→Germination Decline→Viability Decline quality\ Seed  Vigor\ Decline  Germination\ Decline  Viability\ Decline  

This is a conceptual sequence rather than a fixed timeline.

33. Seed Viability and Seed Longevity
Viability
Current ability of seeds to remain alive/capable of germination.

Longevity
How long seeds can retain viability under specified storage conditions.
Therefore:
Viability = Present condition   
Longevity = Persistence over time   

34. Seed Viability and Seed Moisture
A useful conceptual relationship for many orthodox seeds is:
Storage Moisture↓+Storage Temperature↓→Longevity↑ 
within appropriate physiological limits.
This is why seed-storage management is fundamentally connected with maintaining viability.

35. Viability and Seed Quality
Recall the complete seed-quality framework:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health 
Viability is primarily associated with physiological quality.
However, overall seed performance depends on all four dimensions.

36. Viability and Seed Purity
These are different.

Seed purity
Measures the composition of the seed lot.

Viability
Measures whether seeds are alive.
For example:
Purity=98% =98\%
and:
Viability=90% =90\%
can occur in the same seed lot.
Thus:
Purity≠Viability 

37. Viability and Germination Percentage
Consider:
- 100 seeds tested,
- 90 are viable,
- 5 of those viable seeds are dormant.
Possible outcome:
Viability=90%Viability=90\%
but:
Germination=85%Germination=85\%
under that particular test.
This illustrates why the two concepts should not be treated as synonyms.

38. Viability and Seed Rate
Viability can influence the amount of seed needed to establish a desired population.
A simplified calculation is:

Required Seeds≈Desired PlantsViability or Expected Establishment Fraction   Viability\ or\ Expected\ Establishment\ Fraction   
But in practical crop production, germination, field emergence and establishment are more directly
relevant than viability alone.

39. Practical Seed Quality Chain
The complete sequence can be visualized as:
Genetic Quality+Physical Quality+Viability+Vigor+Seed Health 
leading to:
Germination→Emergence→Establishment→Plant Population→Yield Potential 

40. Example of Complete Seed Evaluation
Suppose a seed lot has:
- physical purity = 98%
- viability = 94%
- germination = 90%
- high vigor
- good seed health.
This is different information from simply saying:
"The seed has 90% germination."
A comprehensive seed assessment considers multiple properties.

41. Common Causes of Low Viability
Cause
Immature harvest
Excessive heat

Effect
Poor physiological development
Cellular damage

Cause
Effect
High moisture during storage Faster deterioration
Poor drying
Physiological damage
Mechanical injury
Embryo damage
Insects
Tissue destruction
Fungi
Seed deterioration
Long storage
Aging
Oxidative damage
Cellular deterioration
Poor packaging
Moisture/temperature fluctuations
Improper storage
Accelerated aging

42. How to Maintain Seed Viability
Before harvest
- allow appropriate seed maturation,
- control pests and diseases,
- avoid severe crop stress where possible.

Harvest
- harvest at appropriate maturity,
- minimize mechanical damage.

Drying
- dry according to the crop's requirements,
- avoid damagingly high drying temperatures.

Processing
- minimize cracking and embryo injury.

Storage
- maintain appropriate seed moisture,
- maintain appropriate temperature,
- protect from insects,
- protect from fungi,
- use suitable packaging,

- monitor storage conditions.

Monitoring
Periodically test seed quality when long-term storage is important.

43. Practical Diagnostic Situation
Suppose:
Germination=60% =60\%
You should not immediately conclude that:
Viability=60% =60\%
Possible explanations include:
- dead seeds,
- dormancy,
- unsuitable test conditions,
- seed injury,
- poor seed health.
Additional viability testing may help determine the cause.

44. Important Relationship Between Dormancy
and Viability
This is a high-value exam concept:
Viable + Dormant→Alive but does not germinate immediately 
  
whereas:
Non-viable→Dead and incapable of normal germination    
Therefore:
No Germination≠Automatically Dead Seed   

45. Major Seed Terms — Complete Comparison
Term
Viability

Meaning

Whether the seed is alive
Failure of a viable seed to germinate under otherwise suitable conditions because of
Dormancy
internal/regulatory constraints
Germination Production of normal seedlings under specified test conditions
Vigor
Speed, uniformity and robustness of seed performance
Degree to which the seed lot conforms to intended seed composition/identity and is free
Purity
from unwanted components
Seed health Status regarding pathogens and other biological problems
Longevity
Ability to retain viability over storage time
Memorize:
Viability→Life 
Dormancy→Temporary Germination Block  Germination→Normal Seedling 
Vigor→Performance 

46. Common Misconceptions
"If a seed doesn't germinate, it is dead."

❌ Not necessarily.
It may be dormant.

"Viability and germination are identical."

❌ No.

Viability concerns living capacity; germination is observed performance under specified conditions.

"Tetrazolium test measures seed vigor."

❌ Primarily it assesses viability.

"A viable seed always germinates immediately."

❌ No.

Dormancy can prevent germination.

"High viability guarantees high field emergence."

❌ No.

Field conditions and seed vigor also matter.

"Seed purity and viability are the same."

❌ No.

They describe different dimensions of seed quality.

47. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed viability is the capacity of a seed to remain alive and retain the physiological
potential to germinate and develop into a normal seedling under suitable conditions.
Viability is an important component of physiological seed quality and is distinct from
germination percentage, which measures the proportion of seeds producing normal
seedlings under specified test conditions, and from seed vigor, which describes the
speed, uniformity and robustness of seed performance. A viable seed may remain
dormant and therefore fail to germinate immediately. Seed viability can be assessed
through germination tests and specialized methods such as the tetrazolium test,
embryo examination and X-ray analysis. It is influenced by seed maturity, genetic
characteristics, mechanical damage, moisture, temperature, pathogens, insects,
- xidative damage and storage conditions.

48. Final Master Memory
Definition
Seed Viability = Whether the Seed Is Alive and Has the Potential to Produce a Normal Plant   

Basic formula
Viability(%)=Viable SeedsTotal Seeds Tested×100   Total\
Seeds\ Tested   100  

Critical distinction
Viability = Is it alive?   
Germination = Does it produce a normal seedling under the test?   
Vigor = How fast, uniformly and strongly does it perform?   

Dormancy
Viable+Dormant≠Dead 

Aging
Aging→Membrane Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Viability↓ 

Testing
Germination Test    Tetrazolium Test   
Embryo Examination    X-Ray Examination   

Final one-line memory
Seed viability is the living potential of a seed—the ability of its physiological systems
and embryo to remain alive and capable of producing a normal plant when suitable
conditions are provided; viability is not the same as germination, vigor, purity or seed
health.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Seed Storage
## 1. Introduction
Seed storage is the process of maintaining seeds under suitable environmental and handling conditions
between harvest and sowing so that they retain their:
- viability,
- germination capacity,

- vigor,
- genetic identity,
- physical quality,
- seed health.
The main objective is not simply to keep seeds physically present—it is to slow physiological
deterioration and preserve planting value.
The basic principle is:
Good Storage→Deterioration↓→Viability&Vigor retained 

## 2. Definition
Seed storage is the controlled preservation of seed from harvest until planting or longterm conservation under conditions that minimize deterioration and maintain
viability, germination, vigor, genetic integrity and seed health.

3. Why Seed Storage Is Necessary
Seeds may need to be stored:
- between harvest and the next planting season,
- between production and marketing,
- for emergency reserves,
- for breeding programs,
- in seed banks,
- for long-term genetic conservation.
Without proper storage:
Time+Moisture+Temperature+Oxygen+Pests/Pathogens→Seed Deterioration 

4. Main Objectives of Seed Storage
Good seed storage aims to:

1. maintain viability,
2. maintain high germination,
3. preserve seed vigor,
4. prevent insect damage,
5. prevent fungal growth,
6. prevent excessive moisture absorption,
7. prevent mechanical damage,
8. maintain genetic identity,
9. prevent contamination and mixing,
10.extend seed longevity.

5. The Most Important Storage Principle
For many orthodox seeds, a fundamental storage principle is:
Low Seed Moisture+Low Temperature+Suitable Packaging→Longer Storage Life  +   +     
But this is not universal for every seed species. Recalcitrant seeds, for example, cannot generally be
dried and stored in the same way as orthodox seeds.

6. Seed Deterioration
Seed deterioration is the gradual loss of physiological quality during storage.
A simplified sequence is:
Storage Time→Cellular Deterioration→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Viability↓ 
Deterioration is generally a biological process rather than simply the seed "getting old."

7. Important Seed Quality Changes During
Storage
As deterioration progresses:

Early stage
- vigor begins to decline,
- membrane integrity begins to deteriorate,
- biochemical changes increase.

Middle stage
- germination speed decreases,
- abnormal seedlings increase,
- field emergence becomes less reliable.

Advanced stage
- germination percentage decreases,
- viability is lost,
- seeds eventually die.
Conceptually:
Vigor Loss→Germination Loss→Viability Loss 

8. Seed Moisture
Seed moisture content is one of the most important factors determining storage behavior.
A basic wet-basis moisture formula is:
MC(%)=Weight of WaterFresh Seed Weight×100   Fresh\
Seed\ Weight   100  
Since:
Weight of Water=Fresh Weight−Dry WeightWeight\ of\ Water = Fresh\ Weight-Dry\ Weight
we can write:

MC(%)=Fresh Weight−Dry WeightFresh Weight×100   Fresh\ Weight   100  

9. Example of Moisture Calculation
Suppose:
- fresh seed sample = 100 g,
- dry weight = 90 g.
Water weight:
100−90=10g100-90=10g
Therefore:
MC=10100×100MC=   100  100 MC=10% 

10. Why High Seed Moisture Is Dangerous
For many orthodox seeds:
Moisture↑→Respiration↑→Heat↑→Deterioration↑ 
High moisture can also encourage:
- fungal growth,
- insect activity,
- microbial activity,
- biochemical reactions,
- heating,
- loss of viability.
Therefore, drying is a major part of seed storage preparation.

11. Seed Respiration During Storage
Seeds remain physiologically alive during storage and continue to respire at a low rate.

Organic Substrate+O2→CO2+H2O+Energy 
Under unfavorable storage conditions:
Respiration↑→Heat↑→Moisture/quality problems↑Respiration  Heat  Moisture/quality\ problems This can create a deterioration cycle.

## 12. Temperature
Temperature strongly affects the rate of many biological and chemical processes.
Generally for many orthodox seeds:
Temperature↓→Metabolic Activity↓→Deterioration Rate↓ 
Therefore, cool storage can extend longevity when combined with appropriate moisture and packaging.
However, cold alone does not make poor storage safe.

13. Temperature + Moisture
These two factors are especially important.
A conceptual relationship is:
High Temperature+High Moisture→Rapid Deterioration 
while, for many orthodox seeds:
Low Temperature+Low Appropriate Moisture→Longer Longevity 
The actual safe values depend on:
- species,
- seed type,
- packaging,
- storage duration,
- intended use.

14. Relative Humidity
Seed moisture interacts strongly with the relative humidity (RH) of the surrounding air.
Seeds can exchange moisture with the atmosphere until they approach an equilibrium moisture
condition.
Conceptually:
RH changes↔Seed Moisture changes 
Therefore, even properly dried seeds can absorb moisture if stored in humid air using unsuitable
packaging.

15. Seed Moisture Equilibrium
The relationship can be represented as:
Seed↔Air 
If surrounding air is humid:
Air moisture↑→Seed moisture↑Air\ moisture  Seed\ moisture If the surrounding air is dry:
Air moisture↓→Seed moisture↓Air\ moisture  Seed\ moisture This is why packaging and storage-room humidity are important.

## 16. Packaging
Packaging protects seed from:
- moisture,
- insects,
- rodents,
- contamination,
- mechanical damage,
- environmental fluctuations.
Packaging can broadly be:

Moisture-permeable
Allows exchange with surrounding air.

Moisture-resistant/hermetic
Greatly restricts moisture exchange.
The appropriate packaging depends on:
- seed type,
- initial moisture,
- storage environment,
- storage duration,
- intended use.

17. Hermetic Storage
Hermetic storage uses sufficiently moisture- and gas-tight packaging to isolate seed from external
environmental changes.
Its major principle is:
External Humidity Fluctuation→Seed Moisture Change↓ 
When properly designed and used with appropriately dried seed, hermetic systems can help maintain
quality.
However:
Hermetic packaging does not rescue seed that was stored too wet.
If wet seed is sealed, deterioration can continue inside the package.

## 18. Oxygen
Oxygen is required for normal respiration.
Reducing oxygen availability can slow certain biological processes, but seed storage should not be
reduced to simply "remove all oxygen."
The practical goal is to create an appropriate storage atmosphere and prevent harmful deterioration.
Modified-atmosphere and controlled-atmosphere systems may be used in specialized applications.

## 19. Light
Light can influence seed longevity and germination behavior depending on the species.
Some seeds are light-sensitive for germination.
During storage, protection from excessive light can help prevent unwanted environmental exposure, but
temperature and moisture are generally more central to storage longevity for many orthodox
seeds.

20. Seed Storage Types
Seed storage can be divided broadly into:

1. Short-term storage
Usually between harvest and the next planting season.

2. Medium-term storage
Seeds are maintained for longer periods under controlled conditions.

3. Long-term storage
Used for:
- genetic conservation,
- breeding materials,
- germplasm,
- seed banks.

21. Farm-Level Seed Storage
Farmers may store seed for the next planting season.
Important requirements include:
- properly dried seed,
- clean seed,
- suitable containers,

- cool and dry storage,
- insect protection,
- rodent protection,
- labeling,
- periodic inspection.
A simple chain is:
Harvest→Cleaning→Drying→Testing→Packaging→Storage→Periodic Monitoring 

22. Commercial Seed Storage
Commercial seed storage requires greater control.
It may include:
- controlled temperature,
- controlled RH,
- specialized packaging,
- seed treatment,
- pest management,
- inventory management,
- lot identification,
- germination testing,
- vigor monitoring,
- traceability.

23. Seed Bank Storage
Seed banks preserve genetic resources.
Objectives include:
- conservation of genetic diversity,
- breeding material preservation,

- research,
- future crop improvement,
- protection against genetic erosion.
A simplified process is:
Collection→Characterization→Cleaning→Drying→Packaging→Cold Storage→Periodic Monitoring
→Regeneration if Required 

24. Orthodox Seeds
Orthodox seeds generally tolerate drying to relatively low moisture levels and can often be stored for
long periods under suitable cool, dry conditions.
Many important crop seeds fall broadly into this category.
Examples include many:
- cereals,
- pulses,
- oilseeds.
The exact storage behavior varies among species.

25. Recalcitrant Seeds
Recalcitrant seeds generally cannot tolerate extensive drying and often lose viability rapidly when
dried too much.
Some tropical and woody species have recalcitrant or intermediate storage behavior.
Thus:
Orthodox≠Recalcitrant 
and:
One Storage Protocol Cannot Be Applied to All Seeds 

26. Intermediate Seeds
Some species exhibit storage behavior between orthodox and recalcitrant categories and are often
described as intermediate.
They may:
- tolerate some drying,
- be sensitive to very low moisture,
- have limited storage longevity.
Therefore, seed storage behavior is better viewed as species-specific rather than a single universal rule.

27. Seed Drying
Drying reduces seed moisture to a suitable level before storage.
General process:
Harvested Seed→Moisture Measurement→Controlled Drying→Target Moisture→Packaging 
Drying should be controlled because excessive drying or excessive heat can damage sensitive seeds.

28. Natural and Mechanical Drying
Natural drying
Uses ambient air and environmental conditions.

Mechanical drying
Uses controlled airflow and temperature.
Mechanical drying provides greater control, especially when environmental humidity is high or rapid
drying is required.
But excessive drying temperature can cause injury.

29. Seed Cleaning Before Storage
Cleaning removes unwanted materials such as:
- plant debris,
- soil,
- broken seed,
- weed seeds,
- other crop seeds.
Benefits include:
- better storage hygiene,
- improved airflow,
- reduced pest habitat,
- reduced contamination,
- easier handling.
However:
Cleaning≠Genetic Purification 

30. Seed Treatment Before Storage
Depending on crop, regulations and purpose, seed may receive treatments against:
- fungi,
- insects,
- other storage pests.
Treatment should be appropriate to the crop, seed's intended use and applicable regulations.
It does not replace proper drying and environmental control.

31. Insects in Seed Storage
Storage insects can:
- consume seed reserves,
- damage embryos,

- reduce weight,
- produce heat,
- contaminate seed,
- reduce germination and vigor.
The damage pathway is:
Storage Insects→Seed Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓ 

32. Fungi in Seed Storage
High moisture and warm conditions can favor fungal growth.
Potential effects include:
- seed discoloration,
- heating,
- deterioration,
- toxin risks in some situations,
- reduced germination,
- seedling disease.
Therefore:
High Moisture+Warm Storage→Fungal Risk↑ 

33. Rodents and Birds
Storage structures may also require protection from:
- rats,
- mice,
- birds,
- other animals.
These can cause:
- direct seed consumption,

- contamination,
- packaging damage,
- loss of stored quantity.

## 34. Sanitation
Good storage hygiene includes:
- cleaning storage rooms,
- removing old seed residues,
- controlling spills,
- cleaning equipment,
- inspecting containers,
- controlling pests.
A simple principle is:
Clean Storage→Lower Contamination/Pest Risk 

35. Seed Lot Identification
Every seed lot should be properly identified.
Useful information includes:
- crop,
- variety,
- seed lot number,
- production date/year,
- source,
- treatment,
- moisture,
- germination,
- testing information,

- storage date.
This prevents mixing and improves traceability.

36. Seed Mixing
Different varieties or lots should not be mixed accidentally.
For example:
Variety A+Variety B→Loss of Lot Identity 
This is particularly important for:
- certified seed,
- foundation seed,
- breeder material,
- hybrid seed,
- breeding populations.

37. Genetic Integrity During Storage
Proper storage should preserve the identity and quality of the seed lot.
Storage itself generally does not create new genetic variation in the same way breeding does, but
deterioration, contamination or accidental mixing can compromise the usefulness and identity of a seed
lot.
Therefore:
Correct Labeling+Lot Separation+Proper Storage→Identity Preservation 

38. Seed Vigor During Storage
One of the earliest quality changes can be loss of vigor.
Conceptually:
Storage Deterioration→Vigor↓ 

before:
Germination%↓ 
becomes obvious.
Therefore, monitoring vigor can sometimes provide an early warning of seed deterioration.

39. Seed Viability During Storage
As storage deterioration progresses:
Viability↓ 
Eventually:
Non-viable Seed viable\ Seed  
cannot produce a normal plant even under suitable conditions.

40. Seed Germination During Storage
The general pattern for many stored orthodox seeds is:
Storage Time↑→Vigor↓→Germination↓ 
The speed of decline depends on:
- species,
- initial seed quality,
- moisture,
- temperature,
- packaging,
- storage pests,
- seed health.

41. Seed Storage and Germination Percentage
Suppose a seed lot starts with:

95% germination95\% 
After poor storage, it may decline to:
75%75\%
The decline reflects deterioration.
However, there is no universal rate of decline; different species and storage conditions behave
differently.

42. Seed Storage and Seed Vigor
Suppose:

Initially
Germination=95%Germination=95\% Vigor=HighVigor=High
After prolonged poor storage:
Germination=92%Germination=92\%
but:
Vigor=LowVigor=Low
Later:
Germination=70%Germination=70\%
This illustrates why vigor can decline before germination percentage shows a major decline.

43. Seed Storage and Seed Viability
The progression can be summarized as:
High Quality→Vigor Loss→Germination Loss→Viability Loss 
This is a useful conceptual model for seed deterioration.

44. Seed Moisture Measurement
Moisture can be measured using different methods depending on the crop and testing system.

Examples include:
- oven-drying methods,
- electronic moisture meters,
- other standardized laboratory methods.
Electronic meters are convenient, but they should be appropriately calibrated for the seed species and
moisture range.

45. Monitoring Stored Seed
Storage should not simply be:
"Put seed in a container and forget about it."
Periodic monitoring can include:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- moisture,
- insect activity,
- fungal growth,
- germination,
- vigor,
- physical condition.
The monitoring frequency depends on the storage system and duration.

46. Seed Storage Management Flow
A comprehensive system is:
Harvest at Appropriate Maturity    ↓ Clean    ↓ Dry to Appropriate Moisture    ↓ Test Quality    ↓ Package Properly    ↓ Store Under Suitable Conditions    ↓ Monitor    ↓ Retest Before Planting   

47. Factors Affecting Seed Storage Life
Factor
Effect on storage
Seed moisture
Major determinant of deterioration
Temperature
Influences metabolic/chemical deterioration
Relative humidity Controls moisture exchange
Initial seed quality Higher-quality seed generally stores better
Seed maturity
Immature seed may have poorer longevity
Mechanical damage Accelerates deterioration
Pathogens
Can reduce quality
Insects
Damage seeds
Packaging
Controls moisture/environmental exchange
Oxygen/atmosphere Influences respiration and oxidation
Storage duration
Longer storage generally increases deterioration
Crop species
Determines storage behavior
Genotype
Can affect longevity
Sanitation
Affects pest/pathogen risk

48. Seed Storage and Seed Quality
Remember:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health 
Storage can affect all of these indirectly or directly.

Genetic
Preserve identity; prevent mixing.

Physical
Prevent breakage and contamination.

Physiological
Maintain viability, germination and vigor.

Health
Prevent insect and pathogen problems.

49. Seed Storage and Seed Purity
Storage should prevent contamination.
For example:
Seed Lot A↛Seed Lot B 
Good storage therefore involves:
- separate lots,
- proper labels,
- clean equipment,
- clean storage spaces,
- controlled handling.
But storage does not increase genetic purity.

50. Seed Storage and Hybrid Seed
Hybrid seed requires particularly careful identity preservation.
P1×P2→F1 
The stored seed must remain correctly identified as the intended F₁ lot.
Mixing it with another variety or hybrid can compromise its usefulness.

51. Seed Storage and Certified Seed
Certified seed must be maintained according to applicable seed-quality and regulatory requirements.
Important practices include:
- lot identity,
- labeling,
- appropriate packaging,
- quality testing,
- suitable storage,

- preventing contamination and mixing.
Certification status does not remove the need for good storage.

52. Seed Storage and Seed Banks
Seed banks have more stringent requirements because the objective is often long-term genetic
conservation.
The process may include:
Collection→Characterization→Viability Testing→Drying→Packaging→Cold Storage→Periodic Viabi
lity Monitoring 
If viability falls below an acceptable level, regeneration may be necessary.

53. Regeneration of Stored Germplasm
When stored seed loses viability, the genetic material may need to be regenerated by growing plants
and producing fresh seed.
Conceptually:
Stored Seed→Viability Decline→Regeneration→Fresh Seed→Re-storage 
storage  
Regeneration must be carefully managed to minimize unwanted genetic changes or contamination.

54. Important Storage Principle: Initial Quality
Storage cannot turn poor seed into high-quality seed.
Poor Seed+Good Storage≠High-quality Seed 
quality\ Seed  
Rather:
High-quality Seed+Good Storage→Quality Preservation quality\ Seed + Good\
Storage  Quality\ Preservation  
Therefore, seed should ideally enter storage with:

- high viability,
- high germination,
- good vigor,
- good physical purity,
- good seed health.

55. Seed Storage and Seed Deterioration
Equation
A useful conceptual model is:
Deterioration=f(Time, Moisture, Temperature, Oxygen, Seed Condition, Storage Environment)
 
The exact mathematical relationship is complex and species-specific.

56. Storage Economics
Good storage reduces:
- seed replacement costs,
- wastage,
- pest losses,
- quality losses,
- reprocessing requirements.
Thus:
Good Storage→Lower Seed Loss→Higher Usable Seed 
For commercial seed systems, maintaining quality also protects market value and regulatory
compliance.

57. Practical Farmer-Level Checklist
Before storage:
- Harvest mature seed.
- Clean seed.
- Remove damaged material.
- Dry appropriately.
- Measure moisture when possible.
- Test germination/quality where appropriate.
- Treat if required and permitted.
- Use suitable packaging.
- Label the seed.
During storage:
- Keep storage clean.
- Keep seed appropriately dry.
- Control temperature where possible.
- Protect against insects.
- Protect against rodents.
- Inspect for fungi.
- Prevent lot mixing.
- Monitor condition.
Before sowing:
- Check germination.
- Check vigor where important.
- Check physical condition.
- Check treatment/label information.
- Adjust seed rate if necessary.

58. Common Misconceptions
"All seeds should be stored extremely dry."

❌ No.

Storage behavior differs among orthodox, intermediate and recalcitrant seeds.

"Lower temperature always means better storage."

❌ Not by itself.

Temperature must be appropriate for the seed, moisture content and storage system.

"Hermetic packaging can safely store wet seed."

❌ No.

Wet seed sealed in a container can continue deteriorating.

"Storage increases seed quality."

❌ Generally, storage is intended to preserve quality, not improve it.
"Seed can be stored indefinitely."

❌ No.

Even under excellent conditions, longevity depends on species, initial quality and storage conditions.

"Good germination means the seed was stored properly."

❌ Not necessarily.

A seed lot may still have hidden deterioration or reduced vigor.

"Seed storage and seed conservation are exactly the same."

❌ Not exactly.

Storage can be short-term or long-term; conservation specifically emphasizes maintaining genetic
resources over time.

59. Complete Comparison of Seed Quality
Terms
Term
Main question
Seed purity
Is the seed lot free from unwanted seed/material?
Genetic purity Is it genetically the intended variety?
Viability
Is the seed alive?
Germination Does it produce a normal seedling under specified conditions?
Vigor
How rapidly, uniformly and strongly does it perform?
Seed health
Is it affected by pathogens/pests/biological problems?
Seed storage How can these qualities be preserved over time?
Seed longevity How long can quality be retained under specified conditions?

60. Complete Seed Storage Concept
The entire process can be remembered as:
Good Seed Production→Proper Harvest→Cleaning→Drying→Quality Testing→Suitable Packaging→
Proper Storage→Monitoring→Successful Planting           

61. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed storage is the process of preserving seeds between harvest and planting, or for
longer-term conservation, under suitable environmental and handling conditions to
minimize deterioration and maintain viability, germination, vigor, physical quality,
genetic identity and seed health. Important storage factors include seed moisture,
temperature, relative humidity, packaging, oxygen, pests, pathogens, mechanical
damage and storage duration. For many orthodox seeds, appropriate drying, cool
storage and suitable moisture-resistant packaging can substantially extend longevity.
However, storage requirements differ among species, particularly between orthodox,
intermediate and recalcitrant seeds. Good storage preserves initial seed quality; it
cannot convert poor-quality seed into high-quality seed.

62. Final Master Memory
Main objective
Seed Storage = Preserve Seed Quality Until Planting   

Most important principle
For many orthodox seeds:
Appropriate Low Moisture+Cool Temperature+Suitable Packaging→Longer Longevity  +   +     

Deterioration pathway
Storage Stress→Membrane/Oxidative Damage→Vigor↓→Germination↓→Viability↓ 

Seed moisture
MC(%)=Fresh Weight−Dry WeightFresh Weight×100   Fresh\ Weight  100  

Storage behavior
Orthodox Seeds→Generally Drying Tolerant  Recalcitrant Seeds→Drying Sensitive  Intermediate Seeds→Intermediate Storage Behavior 

Complete storage chain
Harvest→Clean→Dry→Test→Package→Store→Monitor→Retest→Plant 

Most important distinction
Good Storage Preserves Quality; It Does Not Create Quality.   
One-line memory: Seed storage is the controlled preservation of seed from harvest
until planting or conservation, with the primary goal of slowing deterioration and
retaining viability, germination, vigor, seed health, physical quality and genetic

identity through appropriate control of moisture, temperature, humidity, packaging,
pests and other storage conditions.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Seed Treatment
## 1. Introduction
Seed treatment is the process of treating seed before sowing to protect the seed and emerging
seedling, improve establishment, and sometimes enhance germination or early growth.
It is one of the most important practices in seed technology because the seed is particularly vulnerable
during the period from sowing → germination → seedling establishment.
The basic concept is:
Seed Treatment→Seed Protection/Physiological Enhancement→Better Establishment     
Seed treatment may involve:
- physical treatment,
- chemical treatment,
- biological treatment,
- physiological treatments such as priming,
- combinations of these.

## 2. Definition
Seed treatment is the application of physical, chemical, biological or physiological
treatments to seed before sowing to protect the seed and seedling from pests and
pathogens, improve germination and establishment, or enhance early plant
performance.
The exact treatment depends on:
- crop,

- seed type,
- target pest/pathogen,
- environmental conditions,
- farming system,
- treatment objective.

3. Why Is Seed Treatment Needed?
After sowing, seeds encounter:
- soil-borne fungi,
- bacteria,
- insects,
- nematodes,
- unfavorable moisture,
- temperature stress,
- salinity,
- competition from other organisms.
A vulnerable seed can therefore suffer:
Seed→Pathogen/Pest Attack→Poor Germination→Poor Emergence→Poor Stand 
Seed treatment attempts to interrupt this pathway.

4. Main Objectives
Seed treatment can be used to:
1. protect seeds from seed-borne pathogens,
2. protect seeds from soil-borne pathogens,
3. protect emerging seedlings,
4. control certain seed-attacking insects,

5. improve germination,
6. improve emergence,
7. improve seedling vigor,
8. improve stress tolerance,
9. introduce beneficial microorganisms,
10.improve nutrient availability in some systems,
11.reduce early crop losses.

5. Basic Seed Treatment Chain
Seed→Treatment→Protection/Physiological Change→Germination→Emergence→Seedling Establish
ment        

6. Major Types of Seed Treatment
Seed treatments can broadly be classified as:

1. Physical treatments
Examples:
- hot-water treatment,
- drying,
- heat treatment,
- physical cleaning.

2. Chemical treatments
Examples:
- fungicidal treatments,
- insecticidal treatments,
- chemical protectants.

3. Biological treatments
Examples:
- beneficial microorganisms,
- microbial inoculants,
- biocontrol organisms.

4. Physiological treatments
Examples:
- seed priming,
- osmopriming,
- hydropriming,
- halopriming,
- biopriming.

5. Nutrient treatments
Seeds may be treated with appropriate micronutrients or other nutrients where agronomically justified.

7. Chemical Seed Treatment
Chemical seed treatment involves applying an appropriate chemical product to seed before sowing.
Common purposes include:
- fungal disease protection,
- insect protection,
- nematode protection in appropriate systems.
The treatment may occur as:
- dry treatment,
- slurry treatment,
- film coating,
- other formulated applications.
The exact product and dose must follow the crop-specific label and applicable regulations.

8. Fungicidal Seed Treatment
Fungicidal seed treatment is used to protect against certain fungal pathogens.
It can target:

Seed-borne pathogens
Pathogens carried:
- on the seed surface,
- within seed tissues.

Soil-borne pathogens
Pathogens present in the soil that attack germinating seeds or seedlings.
The general mechanism is:
Fungicide on Seed→Pathogen Suppression→Seedling Protection 

9. Diseases That May Be Managed Through
Seed Treatment
Depending on crop and pathogen, seed treatment can help manage diseases causing:
- seed rot,
- seedling rot,
- damping-off,
- seedling blight,
- certain seed-borne infections.
However:
Seed treatment is not a universal cure for all crop diseases.
Its effectiveness depends on:
- pathogen,
- treatment,
- dose,
- seed quality,

- soil conditions,
- timing,
- environmental conditions.

10. Insecticidal Seed Treatment
Some seed treatments protect seeds and young seedlings from certain insect pests.
The treatment may protect against:
- seed-feeding insects,
- soil insects,
- early-season pests.
Conceptually:
Treated Seed→Early Pest Protection→Seedling Survival↑ 
The appropriate active ingredient and legal use depend on crop and jurisdiction.

11. Biological Seed Treatment
Biological seed treatment uses beneficial microorganisms or biological agents.
Examples can include:
- Rhizobium,
- Azotobacter,
- Azospirillum,
- phosphate-solubilizing microorganisms,
- beneficial fungi,
- biocontrol organisms such as appropriate Trichoderma strains.
These organisms can interact with the seed, rhizosphere and developing roots.

12. Rhizobium Seed Inoculation
This is especially important for legume crops.
Rhizobium bacteria can establish symbiotic relationships with suitable legumes.
The basic relationship is:
Legume+Rhizobium→Root Nodules→Biological Nitrogen Fixation 
Nitrogen fixation can be represented conceptually as:
N2→NH3/NH4+ 
The actual symbiotic process involves complex biochemical mechanisms.

13. Why Rhizobium Treatment Is Important
Appropriate inoculation can support:
- nodulation,
- biological nitrogen fixation,
- nitrogen nutrition,
- plant growth,
- productivity.
But successful inoculation requires:
- compatible Rhizobium strain,
- compatible legume,
- viable inoculant,
- suitable soil/environmental conditions.

14. Important Precaution with Biological
Inoculants
Some chemical seed treatments can negatively affect beneficial microorganisms.
Therefore:

Chemical Treatment≢Biological Inoculation 
If both are required, compatibility and application sequence should follow the inoculant/product
recommendations.

15. Trichoderma-Based Treatment
Certain Trichoderma strains can be used as biological seed treatments.
They may help through:
- competition with pathogens,
- antibiosis,
- mycoparasitism,
- induced plant defense,
- root colonization.
Conceptually:
Beneficial Microbe→Pathogen Suppression+Root Interaction→Improved Establishment 
Performance is strain- and environment-dependent.

16. Seed Priming
Seed priming is a controlled hydration process in which seeds absorb enough water to initiate
metabolic preparation but generally not enough to allow full radicle emergence before sowing.
The basic idea is:
Controlled Hydration→Metabolic Preparation→Drying→Sowing 
Priming is therefore different from simply soaking seeds until they germinate.

17. Types of Seed Priming
Important types include:

Hydropriming
Uses water.
Seed+Water→Controlled Hydration 

Osmopriming
Uses an osmotic solution to control water uptake.

Halopriming
Uses suitable salt solutions.

Hormonal priming
Uses appropriate plant growth regulators.

Nutrient priming
Uses selected nutrients.

Biopriming
Combines controlled hydration with beneficial microorganisms.

18. Why Priming Is Used
Priming may improve:
- germination speed,
- germination uniformity,
- emergence,
- early seedling growth,
- performance under some stress conditions.
Conceptually:
Priming→Faster/More Uniform Germination→Better Establishment 
The response is crop-, seed-lot- and protocol-specific.

19. Seed Priming vs Seed Treatment
These terms are sometimes used broadly, but conceptually they are different.

Seed treatment
Broad category covering treatments applied to seed.

Seed priming
A specific physiological technique involving controlled hydration and related processes.
Therefore:
Priming⊂Broad Seed Treatment Category   
depending on the classification system.

20. Seed Treatment vs Seed Coating
Seed treatment
Applying a protective or physiological treatment to seed.

Seed coating
Applying a material around the seed, often to:
- carry active ingredients,
- improve handling,
- add inoculants,
- add nutrients,
- modify seed size/shape.
A coating may contain a seed treatment, but:
Seed Treatment≠Seed Coating 

21. Seed Pelleting
Seed pelleting involves covering small or irregularly shaped seeds with material to make them more
uniform in size and shape.

Benefits include:
- easier mechanical sowing,
- more uniform spacing,
- easier handling,
- ability to incorporate selected materials.
Commonly useful for some vegetable and horticultural seeds.

22. Seed Film Coating
Film coating applies a thin layer of material to the seed.
It can carry:
- pesticides,
- inoculants,
- nutrients,
- polymers,
- other permitted materials.
The objective is often:
Uniform Application+Reduced Dust+Better Handling 
depending on the formulation.

23. Hot-Water Seed Treatment
Hot-water treatment is a physical method used against certain seed-borne pathogens.
The general principle is:
Controlled Heat→Pathogen Reduction 
But the temperature and treatment duration must be precisely controlled.
Too little treatment may fail to control the pathogen.
Too much:

Excessive Heat→Seed Injury→Germination↓Excessive\ Heat  Seed\ Injury  Germination Therefore, crop-specific protocols are essential.

24. Nutrient Seed Treatment
Seeds can sometimes be treated with nutrients to support early plant nutrition.
Potentially used nutrients include:
- zinc,
- molybdenum,
- other micronutrients,
depending on crop and deficiency risk.
The principle is:
Seed Treatment→Early Nutrient Availability→Seedling Nutrition 
This does not replace the crop's entire nutrient requirement.

25. Seed Treatment and Germination
Appropriate treatment can improve germination when the limiting problem is treatable.
For example:
Seed Pathogen→Seed Treatment→Pathogen Suppression→Germination↑ 
But treatment cannot overcome:
- dead seed,
- severe drought,
- extreme temperature,
- unsuitable soil,
- severe mechanical damage.

26. Seed Treatment and Seed Vigor
Appropriate treatment can support early vigor.
Treatment→Healthier Seedling→Early Growth→Better Establishment 
Priming may directly affect germination rate and uniformity.

27. Seed Treatment and Seed Viability
This distinction is important.
Seed treatment is generally intended to protect or enhance seed performance.
It cannot restore a dead seed.
Dead Seed+Seed Treatment≠Viable Seed 
Therefore:
Seed Treatment≠Replacement for Good Seed Quality   

28. Seed Treatment and Seed Purity
Seed treatment does not increase genetic purity.
For example:
Genetically impure seed+Fungicide≠Genetically pure seed  +
   Therefore:
Treatment≠Genetic Purification 

29. Seed Treatment and Seed Health
This is where treatment has a major role.
Seed Treatment→Pathogen/Pest Management→Improved Seed Health 
But treatment is only one part of seed-health management.

30. Seed Treatment and Storage
Some seed treatments may help protect seed during storage, but storage management remains
essential.
The sequence should still be:
Proper Drying+Suitable Storage+Appropriate Treatment 
rather than relying on treatment alone.

31. Seed Treatment Procedure
A general procedure is:

Step 1 — Identify the problem
Determine:
- crop,
- variety,
- seed condition,
- target pathogen/pest,
- environmental risk.

Step 2 — Select the treatment
Choose an appropriate:
- chemical,
- biological,
- physical,
- priming,
- nutrient treatment.

Step 3 — Calculate the required quantity
Use the product or protocol recommendation.

Step 4 — Apply uniformly
Ensure good seed coverage where the treatment requires coating.

Step 5 — Dry or condition if required
Follow the treatment instructions.

Step 6 — Label
Record:
- crop,
- variety,
- lot,
- treatment,
- date.

Step 7 — Sow appropriately
Avoid excessive delay or unsuitable storage after treatment.

32. Dry Seed Treatment
In dry treatment, a measured quantity of formulation is mixed with seed.
General principle:
Seed+Dry Formulation→Uniform Coating 
Proper mixing is essential for uniform exposure.

33. Slurry Treatment
A liquid-based formulation may be applied to seeds.
Seed+Treatment Slurry→Coating→Drying/Conditioning 
The process should provide adequate coverage without damaging seed.

34. Seed Treatment Equipment
Depending on scale, equipment may include:
- manual mixing containers,
- seed treaters,
- rotary drum treaters,
- batch treaters,
- continuous seed-treatment systems,
- coating equipment.
Large-scale systems aim for:
Uniform Dose+Uniform Coverage 

35. Uniformity of Treatment
Poor application can result in:

Under-treatment
Dose too low→Poor protectionDose\ too\ low  Poor\ protection

Over-treatment
Dose too high→Potential phytotoxicityDose\ too\ high  Potential\ phytotoxicity
Therefore:
Correct Product+Correct Dose+Uniform Application 
is essential.

## 36. Phytotoxicity
Phytotoxicity means harmful effects of a treatment on the plant or seed.
Excessive or inappropriate treatment can cause:
- delayed germination,
- reduced germination,

- abnormal seedlings,
- poor emergence,
- root injury.
Therefore:
More Treatment≠Better Treatment 

## 37. Compatibility
Different treatments may interact.
For example:
- fungicide + inoculant,
- insecticide + microbial inoculant,
- nutrient + chemical treatment,
- multiple coating materials.
Compatibility must be checked before mixing.
Treatment A+Treatment B⇏Always Compatible 

38. Seed Treatment and Soil Conditions
Even properly treated seed can fail under poor soil conditions.
For example:
Treated Seed+Severe Drought→Poor Emergence  +  
 - r:
Treated Seed+Severe Waterlogging→Poor Emergence  +    Therefore:
Seed Treatment+Good Agronomy→Best Establishment 

39. Seed Treatment and Integrated Pest
Management
Seed treatment should generally be viewed as one component of Integrated Pest Management (IPM).
A broader system is:
Healthy Seed+Seed Treatment+Crop Rotation+Sanitation+Resistant Variety+Monitoring+Targeted Con
trol 
This reduces dependence on any single control measure.

40. Seed Treatment and Disease-Resistant
Varieties
These are complementary.

Disease-resistant variety
Provides genetic resistance.

Seed treatment
Provides protection against specific target pathogens/pests.
Therefore:
Genetic Resistance+Seed Treatment→Integrated Protection 
But resistance does not necessarily eliminate the need for seed treatment, and seed treatment does not
create genetic resistance.

41. Seed Treatment and Drought-Resistant
Varieties
These address different problems.

Drought-resistant variety
Genetic adaptation to water deficit.

Seed priming
May improve early establishment under certain stress conditions.
Therefore:
Drought Adaptation≠Seed Treatment 
They can nevertheless be combined.

42. Seed Treatment and Hybrid Seed
Hybrid seed can also be treated.
Hybrid Seed+Appropriate Treatment→Protected F1 Seed 
Treatment does not change the fact that it is an F₁ hybrid.

43. Seed Treatment and Certified Seed
Certified seed can also receive appropriate treatments.
Certification and treatment describe different dimensions:
Certified=Seed certification status    Treatment=Presowing protection/enhancement   

44. Important Safety Principle
Chemical seed treatments can be hazardous if handled improperly.
Safe handling requires following:
- product label,
- legal requirements,
- recommended dose,
- protective equipment,
- storage instructions,
- disposal requirements,
- crop-specific restrictions.

Treated seed should never be confused with food or feed.

45. Environmental Considerations
Improper seed treatment can contribute to environmental contamination.
Potential concerns include:
- chemical residues,
- effects on non-target organisms,
- dust exposure,
- contamination of soil or water,
- improper disposal.
Therefore:
Correct Product+Correct Dose+Correct Application→Effective Treatment+Reduced Risk 

46. Seed Treatment and Pollinators
Some systemic insecticidal seed treatments can have environmental implications beyond the seed itself,
depending on active ingredient, crop and conditions.
Therefore, seed treatment should be selected based on:
- target pest,
- actual need,
- label,
- environmental risk,
- integrated pest-management principles.

47. Seed Treatment and Germination Percentage
Treatment may increase germination when pathogens or physiological limitations are responsible.
For example:

Untreated
70% germination70\% 

Appropriately treated
90% germination90\% 
This does not mean every seed treatment increases germination. If the seeds are already healthy and
high quality, treatment may produce little or no increase.

48. Seed Treatment and Field Emergence
The ultimate practical objective is often establishment:
Seed Treatment→Seed Protection→Germination→Emergence→Establishment 
But the final result still depends heavily on field conditions.

49. Seed Treatment and Yield
Seed treatment generally acts most strongly during the early crop establishment stage.
Treatment→Better Establishment→Better Stand→Potentially Better Yield 
But:
Seed Treatment≠Guaranteed Yield Increase 
Yield depends on the entire production system.

50. Comparison of Major Seed Treatments
Treatment
Main purpose
Fungicidal treatment
Manage certain fungal seed/soil-borne pathogens
Insecticidal treatment Protect against certain early-season insects
Biological treatment
Introduce/use beneficial microorganisms
Rhizobium inoculation Support nodulation and biological N fixation in compatible legumes
Trichoderma treatment Biological disease suppression/plant-microbe interaction

Treatment
Hydropriming
Osmopriming
Halopriming
Biopriming
Nutrient treatment
Hot-water treatment
Film coating
Pelleting

Main purpose
Controlled hydration
Controlled hydration using osmotic conditions
Priming using salt solutions
Priming combined with beneficial microorganisms
Supply selected nutrients during early establishment
Manage certain seed-borne pathogens through controlled heat
Uniformly carry active ingredients/materials
Improve seed shape/handling and allow incorporation of selected materials

51. Seed Treatment vs Seed Quality
Seed treatment is not a substitute for quality seed.
The complete sequence should be:
Good Genetic Quality+Good Physical Quality+Good Viability+Good Vigor+Good Seed Health+Appro
priate Treatment 
rather than expecting treatment to correct every problem.

52. Common Misconceptions
"Seed treatment means only fungicide treatment."

❌ No.

Seed treatment includes chemical, biological, physical and physiological approaches.

"Seed treatment increases seed viability."

❌ Not necessarily.

It can protect viable seeds, but it cannot make dead seeds alive.

"Seed treatment increases genetic purity."

❌ No.

Treatment≠Genetic Purification 

"All seeds should receive the same treatment."

❌ No.

Treatment must be crop-, pathogen-, pest- and situation-specific.

"More chemical means better protection."

❌ No.

Excessive treatment can cause phytotoxicity.

"Treated seed will always have higher germination."

❌ No.

Benefits depend on the limiting problem and treatment effectiveness.

"Seed treatment replaces resistant varieties."

❌ No.

Genetic resistance and seed treatment are different management tools.

"Priming and treatment are exactly the same."

❌ Not exactly.

Priming is a specific physiological treatment involving controlled hydration; seed treatment is the
broader category.

53. Complete Relationship With Previous Seed
Topics
You have now covered an important sequence:
Seed Biology→Seed Quality→Seed Purity→Germination%→Seed Vigor→Seed Viability→Seed Stor
age→Seed Treatment 
Each answers a different question:

Topic
Main question
Seed biology
How does a seed develop and function?
Seed quality
How good is the seed lot overall?
Seed purity
Is the lot free from unwanted seeds/material?
Germination % How many produce normal seedlings under the test?
Vigor
How rapidly, uniformly and strongly do they perform?
Viability
Are they alive?
Storage
How do we preserve seed quality?
Treatment
How do we protect/enhance seed before planting?

54. Complete Seed Treatment Decision
Framework
Before treating seed, ask:

1. What is the problem?
Pathogen? Insect? Nutrient deficiency? Slow emergence? Stress?

2. Is treatment actually needed?
Not every seed lot requires every treatment.

3. What crop is involved?
Treatment effectiveness is crop-specific.

4. What is the target?
A treatment should have a defined target.

5. What treatment is appropriate?
Chemical, biological, physical or physiological?

6. Is the treatment compatible?
Especially important when multiple products or inoculants are used.

7. Is the dose correct?
Avoid under- and over-treatment.

8. How should it be applied?
Dry, slurry, coating, priming, etc.

9. How should treated seed be handled?
Follow label/protocol requirements.

10. Is it safe?
Protect workers, environment, livestock and consumers.

55. Master Seed Treatment Flowchart
High-Quality Seed   
↓
Identify Risk/Objective   
↓
Select Appropriate Treatment   
↓
Apply Correct Dose + Uniform Coverage   
↓
Protect/Enhance Seed   
↓
Germination   
↓
Emergence   
↓
Seedling Establishment   
↓
Healthy Crop Stand   

56. Exam-Ready Definition
Seed treatment is the application of physical, chemical, biological or physiological
treatments to seed before sowing to protect seeds and emerging seedlings from specific
pests and pathogens, improve germination and emergence, enhance early seedling
growth or introduce beneficial microorganisms. Major forms include fungicidal and

insecticidal treatments, biological inoculation such as Rhizobium, beneficial microbial
treatments, hot-water treatment, nutrient treatment and seed priming methods such
as hydropriming, osmopriming and biopriming. Effective seed treatment requires
selection of a crop- and problem-specific treatment, correct dose, uniform application
and compatibility with the seed and other treatments. Seed treatment is not a
substitute for high-quality seed and cannot restore dead seed, increase genetic purity
- r guarantee high yield.

57. Final Master Memory
Definition
Seed Treatment = Pre-sowing Protection or Physiological Enhancement of Seed   

Main purposes
Protection+Germination+Vigor+Establishment 

Major categories
Chemical+Biological+Physical+Physiological+Nutritional 

Disease protection
Seed Treatment→Pathogen Suppression→Healthy Seedling 

Biological inoculation
Legume+Rhizobium→Nodulation→N2 Fixation 

Priming
Controlled Hydration→Metabolic Preparation→Faster/More Uniform Germination 

Deteriorated seed
Dead Seed+Treatment≠Viable Seed 

Genetic purity
Treatment≠Genetic Purification 

Correct application
Correct Product+Correct Dose+Correct Crop+Correct Target+Uniform Application 

Overall relationship
Good Seed+Appropriate Treatment+Suitable Soil/Weather+Good Management→Successful Crop Esta
blishment 
One-line memory: Seed treatment is a pre-sowing practice used to protect seed and
emerging seedlings from specific pathogens and pests or to physiologically enhance
seed performance through chemical, biological, physical or priming methods; it
improves the probability of successful establishment but cannot replace high-quality
seed or good field management.

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
Biological Seed Treatment
## 1. Introduction
Biological seed treatment is a seed-treatment method in which beneficial living microorganisms or
biological agents are applied to seeds before sowing to improve seed health, nutrient acquisition, root
development, stress response, or protection against certain pathogens.
It is an important component of:
- biological agriculture,
- integrated nutrient management,
- integrated disease management,
- sustainable crop production,
- microbial inoculation technology.
The basic concept is:

Seed+Beneficial Biological Agent→Seed/Rhizosphere Colonization→Improved Establishment  +      

## 2. Definition
Biological seed treatment is the application of beneficial microorganisms or other
biological agents to seed before sowing to promote plant growth, improve nutrient
acquisition, enhance seedling establishment, or suppress specific seed- and soil-borne
pathogens.
Unlike chemical seed treatment, biological treatment relies primarily on living organisms or
biologically derived mechanisms.

3. Why Biological Seed Treatment Is Used
Biological seed treatment can have several objectives:
1. improve seedling establishment,
2. promote root development,
3. improve nutrient acquisition,
4. facilitate biological nitrogen fixation,
5. increase phosphorus availability,
6. suppress certain pathogens,
7. stimulate plant defense,
8. improve rhizosphere microbial activity,
9. support crop performance under some stresses,
10.reduce dependence on some chemical inputs where appropriate.

4. Basic Mechanism
The general sequence is:
Seed Treatment→Microbial Attachment/Colonization→Root/Rhizosphere Interaction→Biological Effe
ct→Improved Plant Performance       
The biological effect depends on the microorganism.

5. Important Biological Agents
Major groups include:
Biological agent
Rhizobium
Azotobacter
Azospirillum
Phosphate-solubilizing
microorganisms
Potassium-solubilizing
microorganisms
Trichoderma

Major purpose
Symbiotic nitrogen fixation in compatible legumes
Free-living/associative nitrogen-related benefits
Associative microbial support, particularly with grasses
Improve availability of some forms of soil phosphorus
Can mobilize certain soil K pools

Biological suppression of some plant pathogens
Biocontrol and plant-growth-promoting effects in some
Bacillus spp.
strains
Biocontrol and plant-growth-promoting effects in some
Pseudomonas spp.
strains
Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) Improve root-associated nutrient and water acquisition
Other beneficial microbes
Crop- and strain-specific effects
The exact organism and strain are important because not every species or strain has the same
function.

6. Rhizobium Seed Treatment
One of the best-known examples is inoculating legume seeds with compatible Rhizobium bacteria.
The simplified process is:
Legume Seed+Compatible Rhizobium→Root Colonization→Nodulation→Biological N2 Fixation ed  Legume\ Seed + Compatible\ Rhizobium  Root\ Colonization  Nodulation
 Biological\ N 2\ Fixation  
Inside nodules, the symbiotic bacteria convert atmospheric nitrogen into biologically usable nitrogen
forms.
Conceptually:
N2→NH3/NH4+N 2 NH 3/NH 4 +

This process contributes to nitrogen nutrition of the legume.

7. Rhizobium–Legume Specificity
A critical point is:
Rhizobium inoculation is not a universal treatment for every crop.
The bacterial strain must be compatible with the host legume.
Therefore:
Correct Legume+Compatible Strain→Effective Nodulation 
Poor strain compatibility can result in poor nodulation.

8. Biological Nitrogen Fixation
Nitrogen gas in the atmosphere is abundant, but most plants cannot directly use atmospheric N2N 2.
Certain microorganisms can convert it into biologically usable nitrogen.
The simplified reaction is:
N2→NH3→NH4+ 
The actual nitrogenase-mediated process is energetically expensive and requires specialized
biochemical machinery.

## 9. Azotobacter
Azotobacter species are free-living nitrogen-fixing bacteria associated mainly with soils and certain
non-legume cropping systems.
They can contribute to:
- biological nitrogen fixation,
- production of growth-promoting compounds,
- rhizosphere activity.
However, their nitrogen contribution and field effectiveness depend strongly on:
- species/strain,

- soil conditions,
- crop,
- population establishment,
- environmental conditions.

## 10. Azospirillum
Azospirillum is an associative bacterium commonly studied with grasses and other crops.
Potential effects include:
- associative nitrogen fixation,
- root development,
- production/modulation of plant-growth-related compounds,
- improved nutrient acquisition.
A simplified concept is:
Azospirillum→Root Interaction→Root Development/Nutrient Acquisition 
It should not be interpreted as a complete replacement for fertilizer nitrogen in every crop.

11. Phosphate-Solubilizing Microorganisms
Some microorganisms can increase the availability of phosphorus by transforming less-available forms
into forms that plants can access more readily.
Mechanisms may include:
- organic acid production,
- chelation,
- mineral solubilization,
- enzyme activity.
Conceptually:
Less Available P→Microbial ActivityMore Available P  More\ Available\ P  
This can support phosphorus acquisition under suitable soil conditions.

12. Potassium-Solubilizing Microorganisms
Some microorganisms can help mobilize potassium from certain mineral pools.
Conceptually:
Mineral-bound K→Microbial ActivityMore Available K bound\ K
  More\ Available\ K  
The magnitude of the effect depends on:
- soil mineralogy,
- microorganism,
- crop,
- environmental conditions.

13. Trichoderma-Based Seed Treatment
Certain strains of Trichoderma are used as biological agents for disease management.
They may act through:

Competition
Competing with pathogens for:
- space,
- nutrients.

Mycoparasitism
Some strains can directly attack other fungi.

Antibiosis
They may produce compounds that inhibit certain pathogens.

Induced resistance
They can stimulate plant defense responses.

Root colonization
They can establish around roots and influence the rhizosphere.

Conceptually:
Trichoderma→Colonization+Competition+Antagonism+Defense Activation→Disease Suppression xed  Trichoderma  Colonization + Competition + Antagonism + Defense\ Activation
 Disease\ Suppression  

## 14. Bacillus
Certain Bacillus species and strains can be used as biological seed-treatment organisms.
Potential mechanisms include:
- competition,
- antimicrobial compound production,
- root colonization,
- biofilm formation,
- induced systemic resistance,
- plant-growth promotion.
But these effects are strain-specific.
Therefore:
Bacillus spp.≠One Universal Biological Function 

## 15. Pseudomonas
Some Pseudomonas strains can function as plant-growth-promoting or biocontrol microorganisms.
Potential mechanisms include:
- competition,
- siderophore production,
- antimicrobial compounds,
- nutrient mobilization,
- induced plant defense.
The important concept is:

Beneficial Pseudomonas→Root/Rhizosphere Interaction→Growth Promotion/Disease Suppression ed  Beneficial\ Pseudomonas  Root/Rhizosphere\ Interaction  Growth\
Promotion/Disease\ Suppression  

16. Mycorrhizal Treatment
Mycorrhiza is a symbiotic association between fungi and plant roots.
Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) can extend the effective soil-exploration network beyond the
immediate root surface.
Conceptually:
Root+AMF→Extended Hyphal Network→Improved Nutrient/Water Acquisition 
They are particularly associated with improved acquisition of relatively immobile nutrients such as
phosphorus.

17. Plant–Microbe Interaction
The seed treatment microorganism may eventually interact with:
- seed surface,
- emerging radicle,
- roots,
- rhizosphere,
- surrounding soil.
The process can be represented as:
Seed→Germination→Radicle→Root Colonization→Rhizosphere Interaction 

## 18. Rhizosphere
The rhizosphere is the soil region influenced by plant roots.
Roots release compounds such as:
- sugars,

- amino acids,
- organic acids,
- other exudates.
These can influence microbial communities.
Thus:
Root Exudates→Microbial Recruitment/Activity→Rhizosphere Interaction 
Biological seed treatment attempts to introduce useful microorganisms early in this process.

19. Plant Growth-Promoting Rhizobacteria —
## Pgpr
Some beneficial bacteria are classified as plant-growth-promoting rhizobacteria (PGPR).
Potential mechanisms include:
- nitrogen fixation,
