39. Exam-Ready Definitions
Sucker
A sucker is a vegetative shoot arising from the basal, underground, or root-associated
portion of a plant that can develop into an independent plant and may be used as
vegetative planting material.

Graft
A graft is a vegetatively propagated plant produced by joining a scion from one plant
with a compatible rootstock of another plant so that the two tissues unite and function
as a single composite plant.

40. Final Takeaway
The key concepts are:
Sucker = Naturally produced vegetative shoot    Graft = Artificial union of scion + rootstock   
For grafting:
Scion+Rootstock→Cambial alignment + healingGraft Union→Composite Plant  +
         
For suckers:
Parent Plant→Basal Bud→Sucker→Independent Plant      
And the single most important practical principle for both is:
Correct Genetic Identity+Healthy Source Material+Proper Propagation→Quality Planting Material ed    +   +  
  

## Module 7 — Seed & Planting
## Material
## 7. Rootstocks
## 1. Introduction
A rootstock is the lower portion of a plant, including its root system and lower stem, onto which a
scion is grafted or budded.
The rootstock provides the plant's:
- root system,
- anchorage,
- water uptake,
- mineral uptake,
- and potentially specific tolerance/resistance characteristics.
The basic grafting structure is:
Rootstock+Scion→Grafted Plant +   

## 2. Definition
A rootstock is the rooted lower portion of a plant used as the supporting and root
system for a grafted or budded scion.
The simplest memory:
ROOTSTOCK = ROOT SYSTEM PART  
while:
SCION = DESIRED SHOOT/CULTIVAR PART  
3. Position of Rootstock
A grafted plant can be visualized as:
## Scion
┌──────────────┐

│ Desired

│

│ cultivar

│

│ shoots

│

└──────┬───────┘
│
## Graft Union
│
┌──────┴───────┐
## │ Rootstock
│

│

│

│ Lower stem

│

│ + roots

│

└──────┬───────┘
│
## Root System
Therefore:
Scion = Above graft union   
Rootstock = Below graft union   

4. Why Are Rootstocks Used?
Rootstocks are selected for characteristics that can complement the scion.
Important reasons include:

1. Root-system performance
A suitable rootstock can provide an effective root system.

2. Soil adaptation
Some rootstocks perform better under particular soil conditions.

3. Disease resistance/tolerance
Some rootstocks possess resistance or tolerance to specific soil-borne pathogens.

4. Pest resistance/tolerance
Some can reduce susceptibility to particular soil-associated pests.

5. Stress tolerance
Depending on the crop/rootstock, they may improve tolerance to:
- salinity,
- drought,
- flooding,
- certain temperature stresses.

6. Plant vigor
Rootstock can influence vegetative vigor.

7. Plant size
In some perennial fruit crops, dwarfing or semi-dwarfing rootstocks can control tree size.

8. Yield and quality
Rootstock can indirectly influence:
- flowering,
- fruit set,
- yield,
- fruit size,
- fruit quality.
These effects are combination-specific.

5. Rootstock Does Not Simply "Feed" the Scion
A common misconception is:
"The rootstock only supplies water."
That is incomplete.
The rootstock participates in complex physiological interactions involving:
- water uptake,
- mineral acquisition,
- hormones,
- carbohydrates,
- signaling molecules,

- stress responses,
- vascular transport.
Therefore:
Rootstock↔Scion    
is a physiological relationship.

6. Rootstock–Scion Interaction
The rootstock and scion influence each other.
For example:
Rootstock→ Water uptakeMineral uptakeHormonal signalsStress responses 
 \\  \\  \\
   
while the scion provides:
Photosynthesis→Carbohydrates→Transport to roots    Thus:
Rootstock + Scion=Integrated physiological system  =
   

7. Rootstock and Water Relations
Roots absorb water from the soil.
The simplified pathway is:
Soil→Roots→Xylem→Scion→Leaves       
Rootstock characteristics such as:
- root distribution,
- root density,
- hydraulic properties,
can influence plant water relations.
But performance still depends on:
- soil,

- irrigation,
- atmospheric demand,
- genotype,
- management.

8. Rootstock and Nutrient Uptake
Rootstocks can differ in their ability to acquire nutrients.
The pathway is:
Soil Nutrients→Rootstock Roots→Xylem→Scion      
Therefore rootstock selection can be important where soil nutrient availability or soil chemistry is a
major limitation.

9. Rootstock and Disease Resistance
Some rootstocks have resistance or tolerance to particular soil-borne diseases.
For example:
Resistant Rootstock+Susceptible Scion→Potentially Improved Root-Zone Protection  +     
But this does not mean the entire plant becomes resistant to every disease.
The resistance is generally specific to:
- pathogen,
- rootstock,
- scion,
- environment,
- disease interaction.

10. Rootstock and Salinity
Some rootstocks can improve plant performance under saline conditions by influencing:
- ion uptake,

- ion exclusion,
- water relations,
- osmotic adjustment.
Conceptually:
Saline Soil→Rootstock Response→Ion/Water Regulation→Scion Performance 
   But:
Salt-tolerant rootstock≠Salt-proof plant    

11. Rootstock and Drought
A rootstock with a suitable root system may improve access to soil water or regulate water relations.
Possible mechanisms include:
- deeper rooting,
- greater root distribution,
- hydraulic properties,
- stomatal signaling.
However:
Drought tolerance=f(Rootstock + Scion + Environment + Management)  = f( )  

12. Rootstock and Plant Size
Some rootstocks can influence tree vigor and final size.
This is especially important in fruit-tree production.
Conceptually:
Rootstock Choice→Vigor→Canopy Size→Management + Yield 
    
Dwarfing rootstocks can allow:
- smaller trees,
- higher planting densities,

- easier pruning,
- easier harvesting.
But the exact effect depends on the crop and rootstock.

13. Rootstock Compatibility
The rootstock must be compatible with the scion.
Compatible Rootstock+Compatible Scion→Successful Graft  +
    
Incompatibility can result in:
- poor union formation,
- weak growth,
- delayed decline,
- vascular problems,
- premature plant death.
Compatibility can sometimes be:
- immediate,
- delayed,
- partial,
- or combination-specific.

14. Rootstock Selection
A rootstock can be selected based on:
## 1. Compatibility
2. Soil type
3. Soil pH
## 4. Salinity
## 5. Drainage
6. Disease pressure
7. Pest pressure

8. Water availability
9. Desired plant vigor
10.Climate
11.Production system
12.Target yield and quality
13.Planting density
14.Longevity
Thus:
Rootstock Selection=f(Soil+Climate+Disease+Water+Scion+Production System) 

15. Rootstock Propagation
Rootstocks can themselves be produced through:
- seeds,
- cuttings,
- layering,
- clonal propagation,
- tissue culture.
Therefore:
Rootstock   
is a role/function in a grafted plant, not necessarily one particular type of plant material.

16. Seedling Rootstock
A rootstock produced from a seed is called a seedling rootstock.
Advantages can include:
- strong root development,
- genetic diversity,
- relatively simple production.
Limitation:

Seed propagation→Genetic variation   so individual rootstocks may differ in vigor and performance.

17. Clonal Rootstock
A clonal rootstock is propagated vegetatively from a selected genotype.
Selected Rootstock→Vegetative Multiplication→Clonal Rootstocks 
   
This can provide greater uniformity.

18. Rootstock vs Scion
Feature
Rootstock
Scion
Position
Below graft union
Above graft union
Main role
Root system/support
Desired shoot/cultivar
Provides
Roots, anchorage, water/mineral uptake Much of above-ground cultivar characteristics
Can influence Vigor, stress response, soil adaptation Fruit/flower and shoot characteristics
Used in
Grafting/budding
Grafting/budding

19. Rootstock vs Graft
These are not the same thing.
Rootstock=One component =   
while:
Graft=Union/process/result involving scion + rootstock =   

20. Rootstock Quality
Good rootstock planting material should have:
- correct genetic identity,
- healthy root system,
- appropriate age/size,
- good vigor,
- freedom from important pathogens,

- good compatibility with intended scion.

PART B — 8. Nursery Seedlings
## 21. Introduction
A nursery seedling is a young plant produced and initially grown in a nursery from seed under
controlled or managed conditions before being transplanted to the main field or orchard.
The general process is:
Seed→Nursery Germination→Seedling→Transplanting→Established Crop 
     

## 22. Definition
A nursery seedling is a young plant raised from seed in a nursery under managed
conditions and subsequently transplanted to its final growing location.
The key word is:
NURSERY = INITIAL RAISING AREA  

23. Why Raise Seedlings in a Nursery?
Instead of sowing every seed directly into the main field, seedlings can first be raised in a nursery.
Advantages include:

Better environmental control
The nursery can provide suitable:
- moisture,
- temperature,
- shade,
- protection.

Efficient seed use
High-value seed can be used more efficiently.

Better early-stage management
The vulnerable seedling stage can receive close attention.

Uniform seedlings
Proper nursery management can produce relatively uniform transplants.

Early establishment
The plant has already developed a root and shoot system before field transplantation.

Efficient field use
The main field can be prepared while seedlings are being raised.

24. Nursery Seedling Development
A simplified sequence is:
Seed→Imbibition→Germination→Emergence→Seedling→Transplantable Seedling        

25. Main Parts of a Nursery Seedling
A young seedling typically contains:

Root system
- primary root,
- lateral roots,
- root hairs.

Shoot
- stem,
- young leaves,
- apical meristem.

Cotyledons
Seed leaves that may provide stored reserves and/or become photosynthetic.

True leaves
Develop after or alongside the initial seedling structures.

26. Nursery Types
Nurseries can be:

Open nursery
Seedlings are raised under relatively natural outdoor conditions.

Protected nursery
Seedlings are raised under structures such as:
- shade houses,
- greenhouses,
- polyhouses,
- insect-proof structures.

Container nursery
Seedlings are raised in:
- trays,
- pots,
- cells,
- bags,
- other containers.

27. Seedling Trays
Modern nurseries commonly use plug trays or cell trays.
Each cell contains a growing medium and usually supports one seedling.
Advantages:
- uniform spacing,
- efficient use of seed,
- easier handling,
- reduced root disturbance,
- efficient transport.

28. Nursery Growing Medium
A good nursery medium should provide:
- adequate water retention,
- drainage,
- aeration,
- physical support,
- suitable pH,
- low contamination risk.
The balance is important.
Water Retention+Aeration+Drainage→Healthy Root Development  +
  +     

29. Nursery Seedling Water Management
Seedlings have small root systems and are sensitive to water stress.

Too little water
Water deficit→Wilting→Growth reduction    Too much water
Excess water→Aeration↓→Root stress/disease risk     Therefore:
Adequate moisture + Good drainage   
is essential.

30. Nursery Nutrition
Seedlings require nutrients for:
- root growth,
- leaf development,
- photosynthesis,

- structural development.
However, excessive fertilizer can cause:
- salt injury,
- root damage,
- excessive soft growth.
Therefore:
Balanced Nutrition≠Maximum Fertilizer    

31. Light Management
Light affects:
- photosynthesis,
- stem elongation,
- leaf development,
- seedling morphology.
Too little light may cause:
Low Light→Weak/Elongated Seedlings   Excessive radiation can cause stress in sensitive seedlings.
Shade should therefore be crop- and stage-appropriate.

32. Temperature Management
Seedlings are particularly sensitive to temperature.
Suitable temperature supports:
- germination,
- root growth,
- shoot growth,
- photosynthesis.
Extreme temperature can reduce:
- emergence,

- growth,
- survival.

33. Nursery Disease Management
Young seedlings are vulnerable to diseases such as damping-off.
The general disease relationship is:
Susceptible Seedling+Pathogen+Favorable Environment→Disease 
+   +     
High moisture, poor drainage and overcrowding can increase disease risk for some pathogens.
Good practices include:
- clean growing media,
- sanitation,
- proper watering,
- adequate spacing,
- healthy seed,
- appropriate disease management.

34. Hardening of Seedlings
Hardening is the gradual acclimatization of nursery-grown seedlings to the conditions they will
experience after transplanting.
Nursery:
Protected Conditions 
↓
Hardening:
Gradual Exposure 
↓
Field:
More Variable Conditions 
Hardening can improve transplant survival by reducing transplant shock.

35. Transplant Shock
When a nursery seedling is moved to the field, it may experience:
- root disturbance,
- water stress,
- high radiation,
- wind,
- temperature changes.
This can cause:
Transplant Shock→Temporary Growth Reduction    
Proper hardening and careful transplanting can reduce the problem.

36. Characteristics of a Good Nursery Seedling
A desirable transplant generally has:
- healthy roots,
- sturdy stem,
- appropriate height,
- healthy green leaves,
- good root-to-shoot balance,
- no serious pest/disease symptoms,
- adequate vigor,
- appropriate physiological age,
- good transplanting capacity.
The goal is not necessarily the largest seedling.
Healthy + Sturdy + Well-Rooted>Simply Very Large  >
   

37. Root-to-Shoot Ratio
Seedling quality can be considered using the balance between roots and shoots.

R:S=Root BiomassShoot Biomass  Shoot\ Biomass   
A balanced root and shoot system generally supports better transplant establishment.

38. Nursery Seedling Age
Seedlings should generally be transplanted at an appropriate physiological stage rather than simply
based on calendar age.
Too young:
- fragile,
- poorly developed roots.
Too old:
- root binding,
- transplant shock,
- reduced establishment,
- premature reproductive development in some crops.
Therefore:
Optimum Transplanting Stage=f(Crop+Variety+Environment+Seedling Condition)
  = f(Crop+Variety+Environment+Seedling\ Condition)  

39. Root Binding
Container-grown seedlings can become root-bound when roots grow excessively within the container
and form dense coils.
This can interfere with:
- root expansion,
- water uptake,
- establishment after transplanting.
Therefore container size and transplant timing are important.

40. Nursery Seedling Quality
A useful framework is:

Seedling Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health 

Genetic
Correct variety/cultivar.

Physical
Healthy structure and appropriate size.

Physiological
Strong growth and good transplanting capacity.

Health
Free from significant pests and diseases.

41. Nursery Seedlings vs Direct Sowing
Nursery seedlings
Direct sowing
Seed first raised in nursery
Seed planted directly in field
Later transplanted
No transplanting
Better early-stage control possible
Greater exposure to field conditions from beginning
Requires nursery infrastructure/labor Simpler establishment system
Transplant shock possible
No transplant shock
Useful for many horticultural crops Common in many cereals, pulses and other direct-sown crops

42. Nursery Seedling vs Seed
Seed
Nursery seedling
Reproductive structure
Young plant
Contains embryo
Contains developed root and shoot
Requires germination
Already germinated
Used for sowing
Used for transplanting
Usually dry/ dormant structure Actively growing organism

43. Nursery Seedling vs Rootstock
These are fundamentally different concepts.

Nursery seedling
A young plant raised from seed.

Rootstock
The rooted lower portion selected to support a scion in grafting/budding.
A nursery seedling can become a rootstock if it is specifically raised for that purpose.
Thus:
Nursery Seedling→May be used as Rootstock    
but:
Every Nursery Seedling≠Rootstock   

44. Nursery Seedling vs Graft
A nursery seedling is normally a single young plant.
A grafted plant contains:
Rootstock + Scion   
A nursery may raise the rootstock first and then perform grafting.
The workflow can be:
Seed→Rootstock Seedling→Grafting→Grafted Nursery Plant→Field       

45. Propagation Chain
Your planting-material concepts can now be connected:
Seed→Nursery Seedling    
- r:
Vegetative Material→New Plant    
- r:
Rootstock+Scion→Grafted Plant  +

47. The Most Important Distinctions
Rootstock
The root-supporting component of a grafted plant  

Scion
The desired shoot/cultivar component  

Graft
The union of scion and rootstock  

Nursery seedling
A young plant raised from seed before transplanting  

48. Master Flowchart
Seed-based pathway
Seed→Germination→Nursery Seedling→Hardening→Transplanting→Crop 
      

Grafting pathway
Rootstock Seedling+Scion→Cambial Alignment→Graft Union→Grafted Plant  +       

Vegetative pathway
Cutting/Tuber/Bulb/Rhizome/Sucker→Sprouting/Rooting→New Plant     

49. Exam-Ready Definitions
Rootstock
A rootstock is the rooted lower portion of a plant onto which a scion is grafted or
budded, providing the root system, anchorage and influencing water, nutrient, vigor
and stress-related characteristics of the composite plant.

Nursery seedling
A nursery seedling is a young plant raised from seed under managed nursery
conditions and subsequently transplanted to its final growing location.

50. Final Memory Map
ROOTSTOCK=Roots + lower stem + support for scion  =     SCION=Desired aboveground cultivar portion  =    
GRAFT=Scion + Rootstock  =    
NURSERY SEEDLING=Seed → young plant → transplant  =
   
And the most important relationship is:
Rootstock↔Graft Union↔Scion     
while:
Seed→Nursery→Seedling→Transplant→Established Plant       

Core idea: Rootstock is a component of a grafted plant, whereas a nursery seedling is a young plant
raised from seed for later establishment. A nursery seedling may itself be used as a rootstock, but the
two terms are not synonymous.
